### A.20. Executing Entity Information

1. **AFC Capital Partners (ACP):** a limited liability company incorporated on 6 July 2021 under the laws of the Republic of Mauritius having its registered office at 4th Floor, 3rd Avenue, CyberCity, Ebene, Mauritius. ACP is an independent fund management subsidiary of AFC. ACP was created to provide infrastructure project finance from institutional investors and offer them the opportunity to invest in projects that contribute to low carbon and climate resilient infrastructure projects. ACP is also the general partner and fund manager of the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF). ACP was granted on 6 July 2021 a Global Financial Services Commission (GFSC) Capital Services Commission, Mauritius. ACP has applied for and received the Capital Investment License (CIS) manager license from the Financial Services Commission, Mauritius. ACP is not a fund investment manager.

2. **Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF):** an infrastructure fund incorporated on 6 July 2021 in the Republic of Mauritius (ICRF Mauritius) under the form of a limited partnership, without legal personality, in accordance with the Partnership Act 2011 of Mauritius and (i) independently managed by AFC Capital Partners (ACP), (ii) committed to climate proof the African infrastructure by integrating climate change adaptation and mitigation measures as articulated in this funding proposal in the design, implementation, financing, construction and operations of the infrastructure projects. ICRF financing will be provided through a combination of equity or quasi-equity and debt; the ICRF would invest in infrastructure projects with AFC Capital Partners and other investment partners (such as banks, and other financial institutions), including by leveraging private sector capital. Through its co-investment approach to offer institutional investors access to high-quality, climate-resilient investment opportunities in climate resilient infrastructure.

3. **The "General Partner":** an entity to be formed by the Accredited Entity which is expected to be a limited liability company under Mauritius law. The General Partner will be owned, directly or

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 4

#### Africa Finance Corporation (AFC) - a multilateral financial institution based in Lagos, Nigeria, created by African sovereign states to provide financing for infrastructure projects aimed at addressing Africa's infrastructure deficit and challenging operating environment. AFC has committed over USD 16 billion to support transformative sustainable development initiatives across African countries. AFC is a regional entity accredited by UGF to provide concessional loans and other growth solutions to Africa's development and climate change challenges as it relates to infrastructure projects. The proposal outlines the feasibility of exercising certain rights and obligations in respect of the ICFR programme in a co-investment approach with AFC.

**A21 Executive Summary (Max 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)**

**Executive summary:**

African key development sectors have already experienced unprecedented losses and damages attributable to extreme weather events. Exposure of people, assets, and infrastructure to climate hazards is increasing compounded by rapid urbanization, infrastructure deficit, and growing population in informal settlements. Although Africa has contributed among the least to greenhouse gas emissions, the region bears a disproportionate share of the adverse impacts of climate change. The vulnerability of critical infrastructures to climate change is dire as the continent records some of the most extreme climatic conditions globally. This makes it imperative for African countries to prioritize investments in infrastructure assets.

Over the past 50 years (from 1970 to 2019), Africa recorded 1,695 disasters that caused the loss of 731,747 lives and USD 38.5 billion economic damages. Over the same period, Africa accounted for 15% of weather-related disaster events globally (https://www.cimh.rutgers.edu/our-work/working-group-ii). A failure to adapt by not taking climate change into account in the design, construction, and maintenance of infrastructure assets will not only cause costs to owners and operators but will leave entire communities vulnerable.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

Infrastructure assets respond differently to climate events, depending on their nature and type. Their technical engineering design and their vulnerability to the projected climate in a given location will determine how well they can withstand these events. Consequently, the vulnerability of an asset is also a factor of the technical specifications and structural elements of that asset components and their degree of exposure to the changing climate hazards. Climate resilience is therefore not only about designing infrastructure assets with the right materials and structural elements that will make them ready to cope with tomorrow's changing climate. The term has also been used in relation to the ability of an asset to adapt to future changes, including those that are beyond the design horizon. Infrastructure is built for long time horizons (20 to 100 years), giving latitude to a very wide variation in possible future conditions and events. This means that decisions made today will lock in vulnerability if they ignore to take climate change into consideration.

Africa's annual infrastructure spending surpassed US$ 100 billion for the first time in 2018. Building resilient infrastructure is therefore critical, especially considering the coming years and considering the large amount of financing that infrastructure investments require, making them a major contributor to Africa’s economic growth. Infrastructure assets can provide long-term benefits and lasting benefits, while avoiding being destroyed prematurely before they fully deliver on their investment return and societal benefits. Given the uncertainty about the future and the risk to invest in little or no more than 10 years of useful life for infrastructure projects, it is important that investments are made with a view to the lifetime of an infrastructure asset. It is therefore critical to better understand the different characteristics for infrastructure assets (e.g., durability, sustainability, innovation).

Making infrastructure assets climate resilient is a very robust solution* to avoid massive losses and catastrophic consequences*. The proposed Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF) is set up to provide blended finance at scale towards building climate resilience in Africa. The ICRF will support projects that could be associated to infrastructure damages in Africa. The programme seeks to build climate resilient infrastructure assets by providing blended finance for the design and construction of infrastructure assets. By combining blended capital strategies with capabilities established with interwoven policy interventions while incentivizing climate risk preparedness and adaptation, the ICRF will support the climate-resilient sustainable development of Africa. The ICRF aims at mobilizing 50,365,031 direct investments for 442 projects benefiting beneficiaries and making infrastructure assets worth $2 billion USD more resilient to the effects of climate change.

* In relation to blended capital strategies, the aim is to catalyze blended finance at scale towards evidence-based climate-resilient infrastructure in Africa. The transformative approach of the programme is that it will provide blended finance for the design and construction of infrastructure assets out of the design and construction of infra-assets arising out of the aforementioned capacity building programmes. As these projects are typically non-renewable, there is understandably an aversion among infrastructure project developers to invest these additional project costs attributable to building resilience. Hence, the success of the ICRF will be in its ability to provide blended finance at scale and to demonstrate a significant increase in project costs on account of incorporating climate resilience.

* In relation to capacity building preparedness and policy interventions, the programme will address new needs for infrastructure projects by providing robust climate data and information to governments, engineers, project sponsors, and investors to enable them to make more informed decisions about how best to design and construct infrastructure assets that are resilient to the effects of climate change and extreme weather events. In addition, the programme will strengthen the regulatory framework in Africa through the development of a Climate Resilient Infrastructure Project (CRI) products for the long-term viability of climate resilient infrastructure (CRI) investments in Africa.

ICRF will primarily target greenfield projects, and on selective basis, could invest in the resilience of existing infrastructure projects. Indicatively, at least 75% of the ICRF portfolio will be dedicated to new investments

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

In CRI assets (including greenfield expansion and growth investments) and up to 25% may be invested in existing infrastructure assets to be made climate-resilient through climate adaptation solutions. New infrastructure and expansions will be prioritized, planned, designed, built, and operated in the light of climate change impacts. The design and implementation of CRI projects will need to take into account how they are affected differently due to climate change - opportunities to climate-proof existing infrastructure will be examined on a case by case. The ICRF programme will be dedicated to the development and investment in new climate-resilient infrastructure assets.

* 1) Climate-resilient Transport and Logistics (Ports, Roads, Bridges, Railways, Airports etc.)
* 2) Climate-resilient Energy Systems
* 3) Climate-resilient Water Resources
* 4) Climate-resilient Telecommunication and Digital Infrastructures

**Programme Outcomes:**

AFC will play a catalytic role in promoting the development of climate resilient infrastructure through its investment, expertise, and strong partnerships at the country and pan African levels. This multi-country program is designed to scale three mutually reinforcing components: scaled blending finance through diversified sources of risk mitigation; capacity building for de-risking projects; and enhanced task parametric insurance in infrastructure investments, improving climate risk assessments and strengthened new project preparation.

The programme will be implemented by AFC in close collaboration with the GCF. It will focus on climate-resilient infrastructure services and infrastructure assets (all-weather roads, bridges, power supply, public transport, etc.).

This proposal seeks USD 26M in Equity and USD 11M in Grant funding from the Green Climate Fund to deliver three (3) outcomes:

**Outcome 1: Blended finance is mobilized at scale to accelerate CRI investments in the beneficiary African countries, with a focus on infrastructure assets most vulnerable to climate hazards and for improved access of vulnerable people to climate-resilient infrastructure services.**

GCF will provide equity investment into ICRF to leverage private and institutional capital at the fund level and at the project level to accelerate investments into climate resilient infrastructure projects in Africa, especially in countries with high vulnerability to climate change.

**Outcome 2: Improved climate risk assessments and adaptation solutions for CRI; improved capacity scaling up CRI in Africa to support systematic integration of climate risks assessments into planning and financing of new infrastructure assets.**

AFC will provide USD 8 M worth technical assistance (TA) to create capacity and deploy climate risk assessment and adaptation solutions with climate innovations for CRI Asset Classes. Also, ICRF will identify and bridge gaps in the technical capacity and infrastructure for collecting, processing, and disseminating data on climate risks and adaptation solutions, including climate risk insurance and other financial instruments.

**Outcome 3: Strengthened regulatory framework and innovative climate risk parametric insurance (CRI) is mainstreamed for the long-term viability of CRI investments in Africa**

GCF will further provide USD 3 million to support policy dialogue and incentives, including the full feasibility and design of a new scheme to expand climate risk parametric insurance applications to the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 8

## Infrastructure Sector: Exploring Innovative Approaches Tailored for the African Context

### Background and Objectives of the Programme

The programme aims to support infrastructure development, clean energy, economic zones, and telecoms. The programme will reach 50,365,031 direct beneficiaries and 144,115,769 indirect beneficiaries. The programme will favorably affect women as the most vulnerable population groups to climate change and the largest segment of the population in Africa. The programme will also contribute to the achievement of the goals of the Paris Agreement.

### Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF) - Strategy and Fund Structure

#### Investment Strategy
- **Objectives**
  - Increase resilience against climate-related infrastructure assets.
  - Reduce vulnerability to natural disasters, weather events, and resource shocks.
  - Increase capacity for disaster risk management.
  - Increase access to clean energy generation on the distribution side.

- **Investment Priorities**
  - Water supply and sanitation
  - Energy (including renewable energy)
  - Transport infrastructure
  - Telecommunications

#### Starting Points
- The investment selection process will be based on a country-specific climate risk assessment.
- The ICRF will have co-investment or co-contribution equity to create a pool of funds for the ICRF.

#### Impressive Fund Structure
- The fund will have a clear governance structure with a Board, Secretariat, and Technical Working Group. It will not be involved in project implementation but will provide technical assistance.

## Climate Resilient Infrastructure: Trade-off between Resilience Cost, Return and Bankability - GCF concessional equity is the enabler to balance the equation

### Making investable climate resilient infrastructure

It is planned, designed, built and operated in a way that makes it possible for the project to be financially viable even under changing climate conditions.

* It can also enhance rapid set-up and recovery from disruptions caused by these climate conditions
* The physical impacts of climate change - such as increased frequency or severity of extreme weather events and sea-level rise - will not necessarily make infrastructure more vulnerable.
* Infrastructure should be designed, built and operated in a way that makes it possible for the project to be financially viable even under changing climate conditions.

### Increased costs and returns

The conventional capital invested by GCF is set to absorb the overall cost of resilience measures. The innovative capital structure will not only support the overall return on investment but also ensure that the benefits of any private green finance are shared with the beneficiaries of infrastructure services. This will be achieved through a combination of concessional equity and revenue-sharing mechanisms.

### PROJECT PROGRAM INFORMATION

#### B.1. Project Description (max: 1000 words, approximately two pages)

Climate change problem: To date, the design of infrastructure typically assumes a future climate that is the same as today's climate. However, a changing climate and the resulting more extreme weather events may push some assets beyond their designed tolerance levels. This presents direct threats to the assets as well as significant knock-on effects for people and communities.

Infrastructure adaptation: The costs associated with adapting existing infrastructure are estimated at 20% of total climate change adaptation costs, typically estimated to be between 60 and 80 percent of total climate change adaptation costs in developing countries. These risks underscore the need to address the adaptation of critical infrastructures taking into account their specific exposure, risks, and hazards.

Climate change and variability in Africa presents significant but varying adverse consequences for infrastructure systems. This poses huge implications on the growth of African cities, countries' infrastructure services, the quality of life of citizens and development. Climate change impacts vary across different regions due to existing deficit, their low density and vulnerability to climate risks, which affect the continent's growth. Climate risks to infrastructure are both pervasive and diverse.

Figure B.1. Africa map of African cities and associated risk clusters (Kareem et al., 2020)

However, the biggest threats to assets differ significantly depending on the technical specifications of asset components and their degree of exposure to the changing climatic hazards. These specifications point out the importance of the identification of infrastructure metrics for the successful design, planning, location, investment, construction and maintenance of critical assets.

*Will infrastructure bend or break under climate stress?*

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

## Analysis of best climate data over Africa: Climatologically, more precipitation is recorded within the latitude bands of 20°N and 20°S for the period 1976-2005 as agreed in both gridded observation by the Climate Research Unit (CRU) and the World Meteorological Organization (WMO) Global Historical Climatology Network Coordinated Regional Downscaling Experiment 2 (CORDEX) Africa domain (Figure B.1.2, a & b). Relative to CRU, the RCM ensemble shows a good pattern correlation of 0.85, although with RMSE of 339 mm/year for precipitation and 4.7 K for annual mean temperature. The modelled precipitation is higher than observed in the Saharan desert region. Although modelled minimum and maximum temperature have RMSE of 1.41°C and 2.02°C respectively, they are lower than observed.

Figure B.1.2: Spatial patterns of mean annual total precipitation (first column), annual minimum temperature (second column) and annual maximum temperature (third column) from CRU (top row) and RCM ensemble means (bottom row) for the period 1976-2005.

According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) sixth assessment report[1] (IPCC AR6), a number of characteristics exist in Africa. Some of such changes are that the mean temperatures and hot extremes have exceeded natural variability in all land regions since 1850-1900. For example, the average temperature over the last three decades has increased by about 1°C. This increase is largely influenced by human-induced climate change drivers, such as anthropogenic emission and land use changes[2]. The sea level around Africa has risen at a rate of approximately 3 mm/year since 1900. Sea level rise is reported to continue around the continent in response to increased frequency and severity of coastal flooding in low-lying areas due to coastal erosion and loss of most sandy coasts with adverse consequences for seaports and other critical infrastructures situated around the coastline.

**Climate change projections and future climate over Africa: Models project precipitation and temperature in the future climate to change significantly in Africa*. According to the IPCC AR4, additional increases in global warming will cause changes in hot and cold temperature extremes and increase in mean temperatures. The models indicate that the eastern part of East Africa is expected to reach SOC in some land areas at 4°C global warming level (Figure B.1.5). The percentage change in annual total precipitation over Africa is projected to be positive for most parts of the continent. Parts of Africa are expected to experience increases in precipitation while the western edge of West Africa and large parts of East Africa are expected to experience decreases annual total precipitation in the future. These signals are expected to intensify at 4°C global warming.**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

The uncertainty in future climate underscores the need for considering different climate scenarios, which has potential financial implications for adaptation and the pathways approach across the infrastructure system lifecycle. Largey, the financial situation for adaptation depends on the climate change scenario considered. For example, a higher sea level rise scenario would result in higher upfront costs of adaptation resulting in high cost savings over the lifecycle of an infrastructure project. On the other hand, lesser scenarios may lead to lower upfront adaptation cost. Though considerations of many potential future climates could complicate the analysis, it is important for decision-makers and planners to consider a range of climate scenarios when planning climate-sensitive infrastructures to avoid over- or under-investments in climate resilience.

## Impacts of climate change on infrastructure

Projected changes in climate parameters, such as temperature, precipitation, sea level rise, wind speed, etc., will have significant impacts on the built and unbuilt infrastructures throughout Africa. These effects will further have huge adverse implications on most African nations with already low quality, quantity and accessibility of infrastructures. Cities are expected to be more vulnerable due to their high population density and lack of infrastructure for water supply networks for access to water, energy, and food. Nonetheless, the understanding of current and future climate change impacts is critical in order to plan and design resilient infrastructure systems that can integrate into infrastructure planning and investments. Table B.1.7 presents some examples of potential direct climate change impacts on infrastructure. While these are not necessarily direct physical and socio-economic impacts, climate change may have indirect impacts on infrastructures.

## Côte d'Ivoire

In 2018 heavy rains were recorded in several parts of Côte d’Ivoire, including the departments of Bouaké, Daloa, Basam, and Aymé. Several streets in western Abidjan, which are located on a low-lying area, were heavily flooded, bringing the local industrial infrastructure to a standstill. The flooding also caused damage to the port zone was engulfed by water, preventing the movement of vehicles and ships. This has made urban infrastructure more resilient; a €48 million loan was provided for the Abidjan Port Base.

## 1099

In 2008, devastating floods affected all the southern regions of Togo, including Lomé, the capital city. Eleven (11) bridges, including the Togblékpe, Lilikpo, and Bokpoto bridges were destroyed by heavy rains. Bridge 1, which links the capital to the interior of the country and Batoua, was also severely damaged. The cost of the Amalakope bridge was approximately 2.7 billion CFA francs. The total cost for the reconstruction of all these bridges is estimated at €30 million. The Balocto bridge located on the National Road N11 was also destroyed by heavy rains, thus disrupting traffic.

### 2009 Namibia Floods and impacts on toll road

Namibia experiences frequent transport failures due to flooding. The 2009 floods caused the destruction of infrastructure, including a bridge over the Kunene River in Namibia. In 2009, damage to the transport and energy utilities was estimated at $15 million and $34 million, respectively[2]. Another effect of climate change that Namibia has experienced is drought. This has had devastating effects on the country in terms of the total number of people affected by food insecurity and poverty. Indeed, previous droughts have cost the country about $1 billion in lost revenue from tourism and agriculture output. The economic impact of the 2015 flood out of the affected areas are estimated at $3.8 billion[2].

The likelihood of increasing climate change potential effects on infrastructure assets in the Programme countries is high (See Annex 2a – County level climate analysis including a detailed programme country specific vulnerability assessment). This calls for a systematic approach to building case for earlier intervention for a systematic integration of climate risk assessment, integration, reduction and transfer in critical infrastructure planning, design, and financing. In the absence of proper consideration of climate risks in ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology (Annex 23c), the investors’ risk aversion linked with infrastructure associated with climate change will increase to divert available resources from other sectors.

Malnutrition is one of the focus areas of the SDC’s Assessment Report, Climate Change 2022. For urban areas, the report points out the establishment of inflexible infrastructure in cities and settlements that cannot be easily adapted or modified as a result of climate change. This calls for early consideration in ICFR design and for its implementation. Towards that end, and a state-of-the-art ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology (Annex 23c) has been developed to support the systematic consideration of climate risks for the infrastructure, taking into account impacts on nearby vulnerable population and users, value chains and other dependent infrastructures. This methodology supports the systematic consideration of climate change impacts in the design phase of a given infrastructure project and its associated impacts. Three case studies testing the methodology (for road, port and solar plant) have been provided as supplementary material.

ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology (Annex 23c & Operations Manual [Annex 21])

The ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology and Approach in Annex 23c is designed as a “tool” that would enable the programme to systematically integrate climate change considerations into all aspects of the programme. Every asset has its own unique vulnerabilities to different climate hazards and extremes, making it important for the ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology (Annex 23c) to be applied at every stage of design of a given infrastructure; then, climate change could gradually disrupt this infrastructure to increase operational costs, widen the funding gap, and eventually impact societies and economic growth. Due to the complexity of climate change impacts on infrastructure assets, the ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology prioritized for the ICFR programme to quantify the socioeconomic and cost implications of the climate hazards and extremes that are most likely to occur in the future. The methodology is designed as an infrastructure financing and design tool. It establishes the scientific evidence and climate rationale for ICFR investments.

The ICFR Climate Assessment Methodology and Approach (Annex 23c) is built on the premises of the programmatic approach. It is underpinned by a detailed climate assessment based on CMIP6 models and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## B

impact assessment that considers different types of infrastructure crucial to economic growth. The ICRF's methodology is comprised of a multitude of methods targeting different climate resilience, infrastructure, and socio-economic impacts. The approach provides a decision tree for specific response options based on the ICNRF's pipeline to ensure investments are provided to projects with real need for building resilience.

### 1. The first step consists of a detailed climate analysis, considering understanding of past, present day and future climate in a given location and understanding of potential impacts on targeted infrastructure. This includes consideration of the frequency of the extreme weather events and drivers for this infrastructure site (See annex Z3a).

### 2. The second step is to conduct a dynamic system modeling using the outputs of the climate assessment to quantify the impacts of changes in climate stressors on an infrastructure of interest. The system dynamic analysis is useful as it can be customized to assess not only impact on the infrastructure but also the vulnerability of the infrastructure and susceptibility of the users of the infrastructure and susceptible to be affected when damaged and dependent users), wider system dynamics, and the potential for adaptation.

### 3. Third, the assessment results from both the climate stressors and system modeling analysis are used in a socio-economic and financial analysis to assess the impacts on the project's social and financial aspects based upon different scenarios and ultimately for decision making (See Annex Z3b, a).

### 4. Fourth, a pathway adaptation strategy of the programme will allow to prioritize well informed and cost-effective adaptation measures that can be implemented in the short term as well as long-term engineering design options (both structural and soft measures). An adaptation pathways strategy considering uncertainties will be proposed to plan measures as climate change scenarios unfold. It will allow to build multiple options and flexibility into project adaptation plans.

The Methodology includes safeguard for a system approach consideration for integrated infrastructure systems and nature-based solutions to ensure socioeconomic and financial sustainability of ICRF projects

## Infrastructure sectors and assets will also inform climate risks assessment as part of the ICRF due diligence process.

### Status of climate adaptation financing flow in Africa (Source: COP: 2022)*

Current levels of climate finance in Africa fall far short of needs. The total cost of implementing NDCs in Africa between now and 2030 is estimated at USD 1,584 billion. Meeting these needs between 2020 and 2030 requires, on average, USD 250 to 280 billion each year (see Figure B1.9). In 2020, total climate finance for Africa was USD 67 billion, of which USD 40 billion was committed to NDCs, representing only 12% of the amount needed. The financing gap is undoubtedly significant. With the continent’s total GDP of USD 3 trillion in 2020, this represents a funding gap of approximately 8%, or USD 250 billion per year for climate finance, above and beyond current flow levels every year for the next 10 years. The estimated USD 250 billion annual financing gap is expected to be met by domestic resources in Africa, largely coming from international public sources and domestic and international private actors (COP: 2022).

Implementing Africa’s climate response will cost around USD 277 billion annually between now 2020-2030 (COP: 2022) based on each country's NDCs. African Governments have committed USD 26.4 billion of domestic public finance to climate change adaptation and mitigation, representing only 9% of the total cost of implementing priorities, compounding from concurrent crises. African countries may not be able to provide as much domestic financing for climate action as has been estimated. In fact, 52 out of 54 African countries are either in debt distress or at high risk of debt distress (IMF: 2022).

### Figure B1.9: Estimated climate finance needs in Africa by region

| USD billion | Western Africa | Southern Africa | Central Africa | Eastern Africa |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Cost of implementing NDCs | $800 | $400 | $600 | $200 |
| Other (domestic finance needs from other sources) | $1,000 | $700 | $900 | $350 |
| Domestic finance committed | $1,200 | $800 | $1,100 | $400 |

Migration accounts for the largest share of reported needs in 2020-2030, at 66% of total climate finance needs. Adaptation accounted for only 24% of total climate finance reported needs, despite Africa being highly vulnerable to climate change. Figures on access to climate finance also show lower

## Infrastructure investments in Africa: As part of a large effort to expand and upgrade its infrastructure system, Africa is planned substantial investment over next decades.

Infrastructure investments in Africa are planned substantial investment over the next decade. For example, the World Bank capital spending for combined both regional initiatives and country-level masterplans averages about $46 billion per annum, for a total of $789 billion through 2030. In addition to this, there is also a large amount of private sector investment in infrastructure projects across Africa.

In all climate models unanimously show that severe weather extremes will put considerable pressure on the existing infrastructure system. This means that the infrastructure system will require increased maintenance and more frequent rehabilitation. While climate change is projected to take a heavy toll on the existing infrastructure system, sectors facing the greatest risk are those with high exposure priorities to boosting responsiveness to the impacts that are already heavily felt in the continent.

These priorities are reflected in African countries nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and other adaptation plans. The nineteen (19) individual African countries highlighted the need to build climate-resilient infrastructure systems in their NDCs adaptation priorities.


## Barriers: Although there are many actions identified that are relevant for all infrastructure sectors, where Africa faces significant barriers to improve infrastructure resilience to climate change, especially in Africa include:

- Increased exposure to climate change risk in Africa
- Inadequate capacity to assess risks, vulnerabilities and impacts of climate change on the infrastructure system.
- Lack of access to funding for adaptation projects.
- Absence of construction codes, standards, and metrics for climate integration in infrastructure design and financing. Therefore, the imperative to address these barriers that enable today’s and future generations to build resilient infrastructure systems that are not only economic but also environmentally sound.

ICRF: ICRF and alignment with national and regional priorities: ICRF is a first attempt to demonstrate that financing adaptation and resilience of infrastructure can deliver a strong return both by reducing costs from climate-related damage to the infrastructure itself and by avoiding pronouncements.

cumulative effects on the wider society. This programme will also support national and regional infrastructure master plans that are made resilient to climate impacts. This effort will complement existing arrangements for climate risk management in Africa such as the African Risk Capacity (ARC) set up by the African Development Bank (AfDB) in collaboration with the African Union Commission, the United Nations Economic Commission for Africa (UNECA) and the World Bank including through the Global Facility for Disaster Reduction and Recovery (GFDRR). The programme will support infrastructure risk management and develop the region’s capacity to integrate climate change considerations into the planning and design of infrastructure projects. It will also provide technical assistance on how to manage climate hazards on the physical infrastructures.

B (a) (2) Theory of change and narrative diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

Theory of change (ToC): This programme is designed to offer tailored financial products (concessional first loss equity), technical assistance funding to mainstream climate risk assessment in infrastructure investments and the development of a market for insurance as an alternative instrument for managing risks related to climate-resilient infrastructure while reducing the impact of climate hazards on the physical infrastructures in Africa. The programme will also support the development of a regional framework for building resilience to climate change in Africa by attracting private sector investments with a particular interest in climate resilient infrastructure (CRI). This will be achieved through three main interventions: (1) the first effort to mobilize private capital in climate resilient infrastructure (CRI) in Africa. In addition to AFC’s efforts, the programme will also support the development of a regional framework for building resilience and systematic integration of climate risks in infrastructure planning, design, location, construction, financing, and operation. The programme will provide technical support to assess the potential future climate impact on or around existing infrastructure.

On best effort, AFC will work with other partners to pilot and create market for a parametric insurance scheme as a complementary instrument for climate resilient infrastructure in Africa. Climate risk insurance shall be used to mitigate the financial risks associated with climate change by catalyzing access to data, metrics and standards, as well as adequate legal frameworks. Also, the introduction of new conventional and innovative climate-resilient infrastructure assets will have a transformational impact in establishing CRI as a distinct asset class.

The transformative approach of the programme is the introduction of tailor-made capital structures that allow private investors to incorporate climate risk data into the design and construction of the new and existing infrastructure. The programme will also support the development of a regional framework for the overarching objective of the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF or “the Fund”) to sit support African countries in building resilient infrastructure through the following: (1) providing access to evidence-based, climate-resilient, reliable, and durable infrastructure assets. Investments in climate-proof infrastructure will ensure that Africa’s infrastructure is not only more productive but also more sustainable; (2) ensuring that all infrastructure projects in Africa are designed with climate resilience in mind so that they will have access to climate-resilient, reliable, and affordable infrastructure assets. Considering that financing the construction of new infrastructure is a major challenge for African countries, the ICRF seeks to mobilize concessional capital to absorb the substantial increase in project costs on account of climate change; (3) ensuring that all infrastructure projects in Africa are designed with climate resilience in mind so that they will have access to climate-resilient, reliable, and affordable infrastructure assets. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 28

## Stakeholders will also benefit from improved conditions and enabling environment that can attract long-term and stable green investments.

The positive Climate and SDG impacts will link to and directly support host country Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), primarily through the project’s close alignment with direct beneficiaries, including private sector developers (primarily private sector entities, thus encouraging and empowering local developers) in terms of their ability to access finance.

This project will have a significant climate change adaptation impact, both directly through the provision of concessional pilot climate-resilient infrastructure and indirectly and structurally through the mainstreaming of climate resilience into the development of national policies and strategies. In addition to promoting climate-resilient infrastructure, the ICRF will play a key role in strengthening economic development by creating jobs, increasing access to education and health services, and generating job creation, contributing to the development of national economies. Social sustainability will be achieved by increased safety through resilient infrastructure assets, access to education by offering efficient road networks, and improved health outcomes by providing reliable water supply systems and enhanced security. Environmental considerations are at the core of ICRF approach, the programme will set a clear environmental objective for each project, with a focus on biodiversity conservation, air quality and safety. The Environmental and social management system for ICRF is presented in Annex 6.

Anchor funding from the GCF will unlock private investors (pension funds, insurance companies, family offices, etc.) to invest in climate-resilient infrastructure projects in Africa. This will be done through projects including in infrastructure but do not have access to attractive investment vehicles to invest at both the national and regional levels.

A key factor affecting private investors’ decision to invest in climate-resilient infrastructure is bankability that requires a clear revenue stream, future cash flow to make it attractive. One key barrier that may constrain or hamper the bankability of climate resilient infrastructure is the risk adjusted return. Climate resilient infrastructure projects are often seen as high-risk and therefore require higher returns for investors to invest in incremental cost. Yet, many of the benefits of investing in infrastructure may be hard to monetize, particularly when it comes to long-term resilience and adaptation. This makes it difficult for private investors to participate in this type of resilient infrastructure investment as these are not yet considered as an asset class. However, growing examples in other markets (not Africa) show how blended finance and other innovative financing mechanisms can be used to make climate-resilient infrastructure projects and the infrastructure into adequate revenue streams with an optimal risk-sharing allocation in combination with other sources of funding. The ICRF will use a variety of financial instruments and innovative financing tools designed to overcome financial, technical, and regulatory barriers to investing in infrastructure. (See section D.1.2 for more details).

## Goal statement / Impact: Paradigm shift

The ICRF is built on the premise that IF blended capital is mobilized at scale to de-risk and accelerate investments in climate resilient infrastructure (CRI), then climate risk assessments and regulatory frameworks for CRI will be developed, leading to a paradigm shift towards more resilient and sustainable development pathway BECAUSE vital infrastructure will be more resilient to climate hazards, and therefore less vulnerable to disruptions. This will lead to better infrastructures for livelihoods and other social services. ICRF design structure encompasses three outcomes:

### Outcome 1 - Blended finance is mobilized at scale to accelerate CRI investments in the beneficiary African countries for infrastructure program assets made resilient and able to withstand climate hazards, thereby improving access to basic public services (all weather roads, bridges, power supply, ports and logistics, social economic zones, telecommunications, and digital infrastructure density).


---
**Page 33**
---

By providing anchor financing in the form of junior equity, the GCF contribution will allow ICRIF to mobilize private capital at the fund level. The target size of the ICFP capital is up to USD 750 million, including USD 240 million of concessional financing from the GCF, to be channeled by AFC through AFC Capital Partners (AFCP) into a dedicated investment vehicle. This will allow ICRIF to scale up its climate resilience and adaptation extent (25% maximum) to improving the quality of existing and building new roads, bridges, airports, markets, schools, hospitals, water supply systems, and other critical infrastructure assets in Africa. With one main climatic driver, ICRIF will contribute to climate resilience and sustainable development reaching 30 million people by 2030. This investment will create over 150,000 direct jobs and bring infrastructure assets a $2 billion USD worth more resilient to climate change.

The core pillar of the ICFRP is to de-risk investments towards an emerging recognition of the needs for CRI Asset Classes while ensuring alignment with Paris Agreement. De-risking of investments will be achieved by providing concessional financing from GCF, and by reducing the cost of capital through insurance by covering the incremental cost of climate-resilient measures with GCF’s patient first loss equity capital. The ICFRP will provide concessional capital at scale to accelerate investments in resilient infrastructure in the African targeted countries.

The ICRIF mission will be supported by the managing partner AFC Capital Partners in collaboration with designated climatic technical firms for CRI investments, backed by the AFC's strong track record to co-invest and manage climate risk. The GCF Technical Assistance funding (TA) will enable scaling up of CRI in Africa through a series of activities aimed at building capacity and expertise in countries around climate change to increase preparedness and risk management led by AFC. These activities include: developing a comprehensive framework for the identification, evaluation, and selection of optimal adaptation solutions for ICRIF investments in CRI avoiding risk of maladaptation and ensuring state-of-the-art climate assessment and decision-making tools for CRI investments. The programme will help to overcome the technical barriers by using the GCF Technical Assistance funding (TA) to create an enabling environment and scale up investments in CRI in Africa. The TA will address challenges related to the identification, evaluation, selection, and implementation of adaptation solutions, and capacity in countries around climate change to increase preparedness and risk management led by AFC. These activities include: developing a comprehensive framework for the identification, evaluation, and selection of optimal adaptation solutions for ICRIF investments in CRI avoiding risk of maladaptation and ensuring state-of-the-art climate assessment and decision-making tools for CRI investments.

Outcome 3 will address regulatory barriers to ensure the programme’s parallel shift affect in the regulatory environment. The GCF will support ICRIF to adopt new climate-resilient infrastructure asset classes, favorable investment policies and fiscal incentives on a country and regional level. This will be achieved by providing concessional financing from GCF, to be channeled through AFC tailored for Africa to transfer residual climate change risks and seek donors to sponsor CRI for ICRIF and other investments in critical infrastructure assets. This intervention will enable the development and implementation of new regulatory frameworks that support the scaling up of CRI in Africa. The initiative will be supported by a USD 1 million GCF grant to AFC, which will contribute to designing tailored policies and regulations to facilitate the scaling-up of CRI in Africa.

Outcome 2 - Improved climate risk assessments and adaptation solutions for CRI: improved capacity for scaling up CRI in Africa for infrastructure assets made resilient and able to withstand climate change impacts, with improved access of vulnerable people and communities to climate-resilient infrastructure services. The programme will help to overcome the technical barriers by using the GCF Technical Assistance funding (TA) to create an enabling environment and scale up investments in CRI in Africa. The TA will address challenges related to the identification, evaluation, selection, and implementation of adaptation solutions, and capacity in countries around climate change to increase preparedness and risk management led by AFC. These activities include: developing a comprehensive framework for the identification, evaluation, and selection of optimal adaptation solutions for ICRIF investments in CRI avoiding risk of maladaptation and ensuring state-of-the-art climate assessment and decision-making tools for CRI investments.

Outcome 3 - Strengthened regulatory framework and innovative climate risk parametric insurance (CRI) is mainstreamed for the long-term viability of CRI investments in Africa


---
**Page 34**
---

B

ICRF investments will support national climate priorities, in particular countries' choices to prioritize Public-Private Partnership for infrastructure financing considering their restricted fiscal space. ICRF addresses both climate related and typical risks and barriers to infrastructure financing, with the later remaining valid for all projects.

Outcome 1 addresses Barrier 1 - financial: limited capital flows to infrastructure investments in Africa due to investors' perception of a high-risk (risk allocation and financial instruments mismatch), low return on investment, lack of credit rating and asset values due to incremental cost, limited benefit from climate risk insurance, low appetite from private sector.

Outcomes 2 and 3 will be achieved through activities for enabling environment to ensure sustainability and scalability of the ICRF in the program countries and potentially expanding knowledge sharing at a regional and global scale, which will help to overcome barriers 2 and 3.

Barrier 2 - technical: absence of site-specific infrastructure development management methodology (lack of data, methodologies, tools, guidelines, templates, reference cases), lack of standards and metrics on climate-resilient infrastructure; no technical assistance for projects design and implementation).

Barrier 3 - regulatory: acceleration required for adaptation investments through regulatory incentives (gaps in the regulatory framework for CRIs at national level, gaps in the regulatory framework for CRIs at a regional level, missing at a national and regional level). Outcome 2 will be achieved through interventions for strengthening the regulatory framework for CRI in the region and overcoming the barriers.

The ICRF programme has a country specific approach in addressing risks and barriers for infrastructure development. The ICRF programme is based on the ICFs (1a & 1b) as developed in support of the programme activities and is an integral part of the ICRF Climate Assessments and Investment Decision Framework. The ICRF programme will be centred around three main components: a country study - physical climate risk assessment with country specific climate change hazards, across infrastructure assets (Annex 2), creating country-specific risks and barriers overview proactively for infrastructure projects meeting country's needs and priorities and streamlining projects eligible for ICRF investments.

Outcomes 2 and 3 will be implemented with the support of USD 1.5 million of GCF funds. All will require a combination of financial, technical analysis and technical engineering services as well as legal / procurement firms for the delivery of technical assistance services on a competitive basis in accordance with the AFC's procurement policy.

ICRF programme will have a demonstrative effect of the investment case and long-term viability of integrated climate resilience in infrastructure projects. Therefore, the Fund will seek exit from the projects as GCF de-risking at the fund level will no longer be required. The investors would be more comfortable deploying capital in Africa at scale for this new climate resilient infrastructure asset class as part of the successful completion of the programme.

# Theory of change diagram:

## Impact/Programme Goal

If blended capital is mobilized as equity to the local and provincial investments in diverse resilient infrastructures (CRI) and climate risk transfer mechanisms (CRT), CRT will be a critical component of the overall resilience strategy, and vulnerable people and communities will have improved access to these infrastructures for livelihoods and other social services.

### Objectives or Outcomes

1. **Infrastructure is made resilient and able to withstand extreme weather events:** Bridges, roads, water supply systems, telecommunications, and digital infrastructure.
2. **Stronger climate risk management framework and tools are in place:** Climate risk assessments, early warning systems for extreme weather events, insurance products, telecommunication and digital infrastructure.

### Outputs

1. 3.1. ICRF is designed to provide blended capital to the local and provincial investments in diverse resilient infrastructures (CRI) in Africa.
2. 3.2. Strengthened climate risk management framework and tools are in place for CRI in Africa.
3. 3.3. Increased CRT capacity for CRI in Africa.

### Activities

1. 2.1. Strengthening the governance, oversight, and accountability of ICRF investments in CRI in Africa.
2. 2.2. Building capacity for climate risk management in Africa.
3. 2.3. Developing a regulatory framework and tools to support CRT in Africa.

### Drivers, Enablers

1. Countries are willing to participate in the CRI programme for climate risk preparedness and climate risk transfer which can be translated into a complete regulatory framework, and fiscal incentives.
2. All NOLs will be able to provide data support for parametric insurance.
3. Infrastructure stakeholders support transparency and make infrastructure climate risk disclosure information available.
4. Investors have an appetite to invest in CRI in Africa but require debt-risk financing instruments to provide sufficient financial leverage.

### Assumptions

ICRF is designed based on the assumptions that:
- Countries are willing to participate in the CRI programme for climate risk preparedness and climate risk transfer which can be translated into a complete regulatory framework, and fiscal incentives.
- All NOLs will be able to provide data support for parametric insurance.
- Infrastructure stakeholders support transparency and make infrastructure climate risk disclosure information available.
- Investors have an appetite to invest in CRI in Africa but require debt-risk financing instruments to provide sufficient financial leverage.
- The demonstration effect is sufficient to attract private sector investments to secure longer term viability. Governments will set aside adequate funding for infrastructure O&M.

B.3 Project/program description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The programme's main objective is to increase climate resilience in African infrastructure and enhance the livelihoods of the population by offering reliable, safe, and affordable climate-resilient infrastructure assets.

This project will support the design and implementation of a portfolio of projects that focus on the design and validation of infrastructure in nineteen (19) countries in the sub-Saharan area. The programme seeks to achieve this goal by raising US$ 750 million which will enable the financing of portfolio components, including but not limited to: 1) Climate-resilient Infrastructure Systems (e.g., Water Supply, Roads, Bridges, Railways, Airports etc.), 2) Climate-resilient Energy Systems, 3) Climate-resilient Social Services and Public Health Systems, and 4) Climate-resilient Information and Digital Infrastructures.

B.3.1 Programme Outcomes, Outputs and Activities

To address the aversion of private investors to finance the increasing costs associated with adaptation in renewable energy projects, infrastructure projects, or projects with no revenue potential, the ICRF will be structured as a blended finance vehicle with a junior and senior tranche (see Figure B.3.1). Infrastructure investment has the potential to generate long-term benefits to society in terms of inclusive economic growth


---
**Page 38**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 34

and improvements to safety and wellbeing. Such investment could contribute immensely to key policy priorities, such as making societies more resilient, addressing disparities across regions and cities, and promoting sustainable development. It can also help countries to close the infrastructure gap by adopting a climate-resilient pathway for infrastructure development by promoting, upgrading, and integrating climate risk management measures in infrastructure while delivering helping 50 million direct beneficiaries and 144 million indirect beneficiaries (Annex 22 includes detailed breakdown of the indicative beneficiaries – populations with improved access to water supply services, sanitation services, electricity, transport, health care facilities, education, digital infrastructure services, and other programme infrastructure assets).

To achieve the expected objectives, the programme is divided into three mutually reinforcing and interlinked components as set out in the following three outcomes:

- **Outcome 1 - Blended finance is mobilized at scale to accelerate CRI investments in the beneficiary African countries for infrastructure assets made resilient and able to withstand climate hazards and for improved access of vulnerable people and communities to climate-resilient infrastructure.**

Equity investment into ICFR: the objective is to leverage private and institutional capital at the fund level for further investments into climate resilient infrastructure projects and programs in Africa. The key output of this component will be a set of four (4) CRI-focused funds, namely: 1. Climate-resilient Transport and Logistics; 2. Climate-resilient Energy Systems; 3. Climate-resilient Economic zones; and 4. Climate-resilient Urban Development. These funds will have strong impact and innovation potential. These sectors correspond to GCF result areas for adaptation and increased resilience.

- **ARA1 - Most vulnerable people and communities**
- **ARA3 - Infrastructure and built environment**

Output 1.1 - ICFR is fully operational and raised capital at the fund level

### Capital Structure of ICRF:
The capital of the ICRF will be appropriately divided into two tranches so as to attract multiple investor classes:

- **Tier 1 Interest** or "Junior Interest" will hold a junior equity position in the structure of the ICRF, which shall provide limited risk through the ICRF acting as the principal entity to attract commercial capital into the structure – by providing a first loss buffer to the ICRF. Tier 1 Interest are proposed to be issued to ACP and other investors.

- **Tier 2 Interest** or "Senior Interest" will rank in a senior equity position; Investors in the Tier 2 Interest will benefit from first loss capital protection provided by investors in the Tier 1 Interest. The Tier 2 Interest shall also provide limited risk through the ICRF acting as the principal entity to attract commercial capital into the Fund without capital protection offered by GCF. Tier 2 Interest will be issued to ACP and other investors against capital commitments to the ICRF.

Note: i) The SPV or Platform companies are the selected sub-projects in line with the eligibility criteria. There are no sub-funds involved; ii) AIFC, on behalf of GCF, will invest $200M in Tier 1 class units.

### Alignment of Interest:

* **GCF investment is required to provide junior equity for the Tier 1 capital tranche to support the mobilization of commercial capital in the senior equity tranche (Tier 2 tranche).** "Commercial Capital" is defined as any form of private sector financing, including but not limited to debt instruments, insurance companies, sovereign wealth funds, DFIs or Foundations. Any additional concessional funds raised by the ICRF will be used for Tier 1 capital.

* **There is a strong alignment between AIFC and GCF in this innovative fund structure:** AIFC is taking a double risk exposure as ICRF is a co-investment fund; i) AIFC will directly invest in each sub-project (this portion of the ICRF is referred to as the "AIFC equity"), ii) AIFC will be the first funder, and iii) AIFC will invest in the senior equity tranche of the ICRF Fund. As a result, GCF has invested additionally as the anchor investor in Tier 1 junior equity tranche, which is crucial to mobilize funding at scale for climate-resilient infrastructure investments in Africa.

### Output 1.2: Co-investments - ICRF investments in sub-projects

#### 1.2.1: Deal origination, due diligence and execution of investments in sub-projects

* The ICRF's investments in sub-projects will be selected and managed by AIFC in cooperation with GCF.
    * It is expected that AIFC and GCF will enter into one or several support services agreement(s) (the "SSA") to facilitate the co-investment. Under this arrangement, AIFC will provide to GCF administrative and technical services as well as i) investment operations services, ii) transaction advisory services, iii) due diligence services, iv) legal and regulatory services. This ensures access to AIFC's sector and country expertise and its specialists in environmental, social and governance matters.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 37

It is also expected that AFC and the ICRF (represented by the General Partner) will enter into a co-investment agreement (the "Co-Investment Agreement") which will define the allocation of investment opportunities between AFC and the ICRF, thereby ensuring access to AFC's pipeline of projects for consideration by the ICRF. Under this arrangement, the ICRF will have the option (not the obligation) to co-invest alongside AFC. The decision of the ICRF to participate in an opportunity would be made independently by the Investment Committee of the ICRF.

## Deal Screening

AFC vet test the characteristics of originated projects against the investment eligibility criteria and apply the appropriate assessment tools as per the Project Development Assessments and Investment Decision Making Process (see Annex 21 Operations Manual and Climate Change Adaptation Guidelines). The AFC will ensure that all relevant factors affecting the attractiveness of the investment opportunity. Climate risk assessments and environmental and social considerations are an integral part of this process.

## Project Development is core to ICRF investment thesis: AFC will work closely with AFC in the identification, development, construction, and operation of the infrastructure investments considered by the ICRF. The lead role for project development is typically assumed by AFC, which has extensive experience on the continent. Project development is a source of project pipeline for ICRF and creates opportunities to engage with potential projects that are not yet in the pipeline.

Climate resilience measures are integrated in the project design and development phase with specific focus on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.

## Deal Appraisal: Each of AFC and ACP will apply its investment appraisal, due diligence and approval procedures in appraising potential Fund Investments, and only those sub-projects qualified under both the AFC’s and ICRF’s investment criteria for investment under the framework. The ACF Investment Committee is charged with four key objectives:

These are:
(i) to leverage AFC's investment expertise as the premier sustainable infrastructure solutions provider; market knowledge and understanding of climate and development impact investments, including the ability to conduct comprehensive due diligence in the appraisal process;
(ii) to maintain independence of investment decision-making on behalf of the ACP, management of the ICRF, and the ICRF itself. The AFC Investment Committee will only consider projects that meets the requirements of the ICRF and the GCF; and (iv) to make the processing of investments as streamlined as possible while maintaining a high standard of due diligence.

The AFC Investment Committee is responsible for deal origination, due diligence, and approval process. It works closely with AFC colleagues to identify, appraise, structure, and negotiate transactions and actively participates in the transaction execution phase (e.g., signing of agreements). The ACP will work closely with AFC (in Nigeria) in partnership with AFC under the Support Services Agreement(s); and the Co-Investment Agreement mentioned above. The ACF team will be responsible for deal origination, due diligence, and approval process.

The ACP Teams consist of staff with complementary and specific skills and experience required, which includes project development expertise, transaction execution, financial structuring and mobilization expertise, operational asset management experience, proven buy-and-build track record in the infrastructure sector, exit know-how, industry knowledge and environmental and social expertise.

As mentioned above, the ACP team in cooperation with AFC will conduct comprehensive due diligence on projects under consideration. This will include site visits, meetings, teleconferences, and written questionnaires with members of the potential investment's management. A thorough analysis is required to build a strong management projections and key assumptions subject to sensitivity analysis (see Annex 21 Operations Manual for more details). Assuming the ACP team wishes to proceed, the ICRF will typically enter into a non-binding Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) with AFC to confirm that both the ICRF would be willing to make an investment. Throughout the initial evaluation process,


---
**Page 42**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 38

## ACP team will be in frequent communication with AFC co-investment team and the Investment Committee to gauge overall interest in the opportunity and discuss the opportunities and risks associated with the potential investment.

After a term sheet is executed, the ACP team in cooperation with AFC will conduct a full due diligence of the transaction, including by engaging third-party advisors as appropriate, including legal, tax, accounting and regulatory, as needed. The ACP and the AFC teams are also responsible for ensuring that all transactions comply with applicable laws, regulations, and ESG policies. Depending on the circumstances, technical consultants may be engaged for additional expertise.

* **The ACP team will prepare an investment memo for formal presentation and review by the Investment Committee, which summarizes the investment opportunity and key deal terms.** The Investment Committee will then make a formal determination whether or not to proceed with the transaction. Any material changes relating to the transaction will need to be further approved by the Investment Committee.

Following approval of the Investment Committee, the ACP team, with the assistance of external counsel, will prepare and negotiate final investment agreements.

### 1.2.2: Portfolio management

✓ As from the investment, AFC and the ACP team will be involved with the growth and development of the portfolio companies. Most often, this is accomplished through one or more seats on a company’s board of directors. If the ACP team holds a majority interest in the portfolio company, it will negotiate appropriate contractual rights and minority protections. The ACP team may also perform regular review of the performance of the investments.

### 1.2.3: Reporting to investors and GCF

✓ **Definitive reporting requirements will be specified in the ICRF documentation.** It is expected that investors would receive unaudited financial information on a frequency to be agreed, audited year-end financial statements, and a narrative update on each portfolio company on a frequency to be agreed.

✓ Once received, any such document should be remitted to the GCF by AFC in its role as the Adviser/Manager.

### 1.2.4: Exit assets, return capital plus profit to investors; close of ICRF

✓ **AFC, by virtue of having completed many transactions and full exits over the last 15 years, maintains profitable relationships with a number of the leading operators that operate in Africa. The ICRF is expected to be able to leverage these relationships along with AFC’s other contacts to consider the full range of exit options available for each transaction.** It will be necessary to conduct due diligence to assess the depth of strategic investor interest for key assets and also to follow the evolution of any equity co-investment opportunities that may arise in the course of the investment. The Investment Committee will review the proposed exit strategies, timing and exit projections on a quarterly basis. Any significant changes to these plans (e.g., extending an exit beyond 15 months before the proposed exit timing, and key decisions about when to launch an exit, the appointment of advisors etc.) will be approved by the Investment Committee.

✓ It is expected that the significant majority of exit transactions will consist of a sale for cash proceeds while other exits may include co-investments (including AFC on behalf of the GCF), subject to reserves for expenses and contingent liabilities.

AFC will use GCF Technical Assistance funding (TA), in collaboration with ACP, to strengthen climate risk assessments and adaptation solutions for ICRF investments in CRI Asset Classes. The TA will be also used to build capacity of national authorities and groups representing infrastructure users on climate change management, including the development of a national climate change action plan and knowledge, and capacity in countries around climate change in order to increase preparedness and risk management.

The ICFR programme will identify and bridge gaps in technical capacity and integrate climate adaptation measures into projects for selecting, designing, implementing, operating and maintaining infrastructure and its impact on infrastructure in the participating countries. The programme will respond to such gaps by sharing knowledge and best practices through training of national authorities and groups representing investments on the Continent through public accessible sources and trainings for public and private sector practitioners.

**Parametric climate risk insurance for infrastructure is currently new in Africa. On the other hand, large global insurers have already developed and are offering parametric products in this market; therefore there is an opportunity to replicate, develop, and apply these parametric risk insurance products in Africa.**


---
**Page 45**
---

With support of GCF technical assistance funding (US$1 Million), AFC will support the design and development of parametric climate risk insurance products for the infrastructure sector in Africa. AFC will undertake a literature study on the development of parametric climate risk insurance products for infrastructure, with a focus on how to engage with industry players developing insurance companies, private sector actors, government agencies in target countries, investors, industry groups such as the African Infrastructure Investment Facility (AIIF), and other relevant stakeholders. The project will support capacity building activities (training) for key stakeholders in target countries and organize events to raise awareness of climate risk insurance products for infrastructure. AFC will also provide technical funding on best effort basis to implement innovative and transformational parametric risk insurance solutions as part of the CIF’s programme, and provide a strong demonstration effect with paradigm shift potential.

Stakeholder Engagement: As a member of the Coalition for Climate Resilient Investment, and a member of the International Finance Corporation (IFC) Infrastructure Dialogue, AFC has been actively engaged in discussions on climate resilience. The IFC expressed a strong commitment in building a market for climate resilient infrastructure. The IDF is a public-private partnership led by the insurance industry and supported by international organizations. The IDF was established at the 2014 World Economic Forum in Davos, Switzerland, and officially launched in 2015 and was officially launched by leaders of the United Nations, the World Bank and the insurance sector. The IDF aims to build a market for climate resilient infrastructure through developing risk management capabilities to build greater resilience and protect for people, communities, businesses, and governments from the impacts of climate change.

Efforts to improve disaster resilience and address the protection gap have historically been primarily driven by a hand of private/public stakeholders. Given the scale, scope and complexity of the resilience and preparedness challenge, it is critical that all levels of government, civil society organizations, and all relevant stakeholders, both public and private, are critical to meeting the needs of climate-vulnerable people. Addressing the low level of insurance penetration in developing countries has become more urgent considering the increasing frequency and severity of extreme weather events associated with climate change. Building the capacity of key stakeholders in target countries is essential.

Climate Resilient Infrastructure: The Role of Insurance in Climate Risk Management

Infrastructure is a critical component of any country’s economy, it is designed, developed, built, and operated in a way that anticipates, prepares for, and adapts to changing climate conditions. It can also be adapted to reduce the risk of climate-related disruptions. However, infrastructure is not a resilient infrastructure redesign but may not fully eliminate the risk of climate-related disruptions. According to the Sixth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change, Adaptation does not prevent all adverse impacts from occurring and it cannot be used as an excuse for inaction.

With increasing global warming, losses and damages increase and become increasingly difficult to avoid, with adaptation alone being insufficient to address these risks. In many cases, climate change impacts cannot be forecasted with full precision; there are residual risks that adaptation may not fully address.

Need for Insurance: The use of insurance as a risk transfer mechanism can be instrumental to protect infrastructure assets against future loss due to climate change. The CIF programme aims to support the development and implementation of innovative solutions in Africa, including the development of products that are climate-proof and further enhance the resilience of infrastructure investments. The long-term objective is to promote the use of insurance as a risk transfer mechanism for infrastructure projects in Africa, which would progressively stimulate the creation of effective climate risk management tools and practices. This will help reduce the exposure of investors, lenders, owners and operators of infrastructure assets continue to face multiple challenges posed by climate change.

Parametric Climate Risk Insurance: The term parametric insurance describes a type of insurance contract that insures a policyholder against the occurrence of a specific climate-related events (e.g., excessive rainfall, droughts, etc.). The payout is triggered when an agreed upon threshold is reached and the magnitude of the event as agreed in the insurance policy. The payouts would support the sub-projects by

## B3.2 Guiding principles on climate resilience integration in ICRF Investment process

### During project development and preparation, ICRF will work with relevant national government and the project sponsors to integrate climate resilience into the Public-Private Partnership (PPP) joint framework.

#### Project Identification Phase: Infrastructure needs to be resilient to climate impacts and to help build the resilience of communities. The public partner can prioritize projects that build resilience through infrastructure. The public partner gains an understanding of the key climate hazards relevant to the project by conducting a high-level climate risk screening.
- **Project Identification Phase:** Infrastructure needs to be resilient to climate impacts and to help build the resilience of communities.

#### Project Appraisal Phase: During the project appraisal phase the public partner integrates principles around decision-making under uncertainty. In this phase, practitioners will assess the key climate hazards relevant to the project and identify Nature-based Solutions that can be used to mitigate and adapt to the climate risks.
- **Project Appraisal Phase:** During the project appraisal phase the public partner integrates principles around decision-making under uncertainty.

#### Tender and Award Phase: A complete competitive partnership with climate resilience as a core component of the tender scoring and award processes will ensure an appropriate balance between the public and prospective private sector. In a dissent, the Request for Proposals (RFP) and documents to ensure the balance in risk allocation.
- **Tender and Award Phase:** A complete competitive partnership with climate resilience as a core component of the tender scoring and award processes.

#### Contract Management Phase: Contract management plays a key role in monitoring the achievement of performance requirements and situations of high climate risk. Proactive engagement by the parties can
- **Contract Management Phase:** Contract management plays a key role in monitoring the achievement of performance requirements and situations of high climate risk.

**Figure B.3.4 Resilience intervention points within the PPP project framework**

| Project identification phase | Project appraisal phase | Contract development phase |
|---|---|---|
| Identify climate resilient projects | Climate risk assessment | Performance monitoring and learning cycle |

Traditional infrastructure providers, such as governments and state-owned utility service providers, are under financial pressure as demand for public infrastructure exceeds available public finance. Budgets are strained and will likely remain constrained. Governments are increasingly looking to private investment in PPPs to provide the necessary capital to deliver critical infrastructure projects. The use of a blended incentive mechanism for optimizing capital and operational investments, reinforced by the lenders' commitment to climate resilience, will help governments to achieve their development goals while improving efficiency. The use of the PPP model will integrate best practices of climate resilience into infrastructure investment, design, operations, and maintenance. This includes embedding climate resilience into PPP contracts, incorporating climate resilience benchmarks in O&M standards, and leveraging data to monitor resilience options and maximize benefits for both parties.

**Figure B.3.5 Typical PPP structure**

| Initiator | Lenders |
|---|---|
| Government (Public Partner) | Development Finance Institutions (Private Sector) |

| Shareholders | Users |
|---|---|
| Operators | General contractor |
| Equity/financial investor | Specialized maintenance contractor |
