# Integrating Climate Risk Management within the ICF Investment Process

Climate resilience is embedded in ICF investment process. ICF has established a tailored co-investments approach to leverage the solid investment track record of its parent, Africa Finance Corporation. ACF takes an efficient, disciplined and proactive approach to all aspects of the investment process, with a focus on effective risk management, climate change adaptation, environmental resilience integration, balanced risk-adjusted return, rigorous portfolio management and exit management.

The climate risk management process of ICF is designed to fully integrate key stages of the ICF investment process. The climate risk management process is an iterative process that is ongoing and addressed, including the identification and integration of potential adaptation response measures to reduce the adverse impacts of climate change on investments. This includes a systematic assessment of climate risks and resilience/adaptation options, including quantification of their costs and benefits at each stage of the asset owner and broader societal and system impacts.

B3.3 ICRF Corporate Governance Framework

The ICRF has a robust governance framework aligned with best international standards. Figure B3.6c reflects the three committees that constitute the direct governance body of the fund.

Figure B3.6c: ICRF Governance – Committees formulation

| Roles and Responsibilities |
| --- |

i) **The ICRF’s Climate Governance Committee (CGC)**

- The CGC operating under AFC Capital Partners (ACP) will oversee the application of ACPs Framework for each investment, review the quality of due diligence and ensure that each investment integrates a climate risk assessment and includes resilience measures for climate-proofing, and is aligned with ICRF investment and environmental policies.
- The final approval of all ICRF investments considered by the ICRF Fund must be endorsed by the climate governance committee from the climatic perspective. The CGC will consist of various members with prior climate investment-related experiences.

ii) **Investment Committee**

- Reviews all investments with a focus on the quality of due diligence (financial and commercial terms, climate-proofing, risk assessment, and resilience measures; environmental and social risk, legal, etc.)
- Ensure compliance with the Fund’s investment strategy and policies
- Final Approval of all ICRF investments considering the primary due diligence undertaken by the ACPs AFC joint co-investment team and the recommendations of the climate governance committee.

The members of the committee include various members with prior climate investment-related experiences to effectively fully their role as describe below.

iii) **Advisory Committee**

- The Advisory Committee is an advisory and consultative function to AFC Capital Partners (ACP)
- This committee considers all matters involving conflicts of interest as presented by ACP to obtain the committee’s consent or in some cases, the consent of all investors.
- The Advisory Committee expected to consist of members of core investors in the ICRF Fund.

### B.3.4 Climate Risk and Impact Assessment Methodology: Guiding Principles

ACP will employ a roster of technical consulting firms to undertake location specific climate risk assessments for each project.

ICRF investments integrate climate change risks into decision-making (ex-ante) and directly address identified risks, vulnerabilities, or effects while avoiding inadvertent increases in vulnerability of systems or sectors that are not part of the project’s main focus. Each investment should:

- reduce risk, exposure, or sensitivity to climate change.
- increase adaptive capacity.
- develop capacity to respond to developments on risks, vulnerabilities, or effects; or
- address effects directly linked to climate change.

ACP will apply the principles of the Adaptation Finance Methodology (AFM) and Guidance[1], the principles of Physical Climate Risk Assessment Methodology (PCRIM)[2] for Coalition for Climate Resilient Investment (CCRI), the World Bank’s Climate Bonds Framework, or the EU Commission’s Renewable Investment Officer Handbook[3] of the Global Centre for Adaptation (GCA) in the EU context taking into consideration the African context.

**Guiding Principle 1: Understand vulnerability and change:** For each sub-project, the context of climate vulnerability must be set out clearly using a robust evidence base. This could take a variety of forms, including literature reviews, case studies and reports or original climate vulnerability analysis conducted as part of preparation of a project.

Examples of good practice in conducting original analysis include using records from trusted sources such as meteorological stations, satellite imagery, and other reliable data sources to identify past and recent climate trends, including any departures from historic means. These may be combined with climate change projections or scenarios for the region, based on the best available science and plausible future scenarios, to explore the full range of projected outcomes and uncertainties. Climate projection uncertainties should be presented transparently and interpreted carefully. The timescale of the projected

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

## Part I: Scoping and Data Gathering & Climate Risk Screening (location specific)

The aim of this climate risk screening is to develop a climate risk profile which identifies the key climate risks that the project, its ecosystem (supply chains, independent infrastructures, natural environment etc.), and the communities it benefits are exposed to. This will help identify how these hazards interact with infrastructure vulnerabilities to determine the risk profile associated with the project. It also helps understand what the project is exposed to, with their evolving possibilities over time under different climate scenarios.

This involves collecting location specific climate data on historic climate and projected climate change, relevant to the identified climate hazards, engineering data, and financial performance data, including:

- **Identify thresholds:** Understand any climate threats critical to successful delivery of the infrastructure project. The impacts of these hazards should be considered in the design of assets, asset management objectives or standards used in asset design should also be identified.
- **Understand performance of the asset –** or similar assets under historic climate data. Data to analyze include:
  - Historic records of climate stressors such as temperature, rainfall and wind patterns as well sea level, in the locality of the project.
  - Records of extreme events, such as floods, droughts, or heatwaves, and how the asset was impacted by these events.

- **Understand how climate is projected to change –** data to collect includes climate projection data relevant to the hazards, time periods and climate scenarios.
- **Scope of analysis:** Consider all infrastructure assets (supply chains, independent infrastructures, natural environment etc. Highlight key interdependencies that could lead to cascading effects).
- **Review of the asset life cycle and design and provide this data to climate science workstream.**
  - Identify and confirm relevant infrastructure asset management KPIs (e.g., downtime or availability requirements) for each asset.
  - Ensure that the project team is aware of the necessary linkages between asset performance and design are quantifiable.

- **Quantify the risks:** Consider the potential impacts on operations and maintenance plan (e.g., scheduled/unscheduled downtime, response to extreme events).

AFC and ACP have significant networks to originate infrastructure projects across the target countries. This work is typically undertaken by a climate data analytics provider and an engineering firm working in collaboration with AFC and ACP, and the project developer.

## Part 2: Climate Risk and Impact Assessment

The climate risk assessment is built on the findings of the high-level climate risk screening, developed during the Project Identification Phase, to assess the risks qualitatively or quantitatively depending upon the context. The key steps of the assessment are:

- **Analyze hazards to which the project, its ecosystem and beneficiaries are exposed**
  - Analyze the projects and beneficiaries’ vulnerability
  - Assess the potential negative impacts on its surroundings or project beneficiaries (e.g., IUCN scenarios)
  - Summarise the climate risk assessment findings for the project


---
**Page 54**
---

With the support of the GCF technical assistance, AFC in collaboration with ACP will procure the service of specialized climate risk assessment firms to undertake a systematic assessment of the project. The experts work closely with AFC’s sector specialists and ACP Climate team. The climate risk assessment is finally reviewed and validated by the climate governance committee of ICRF, and the results of the climate risk assessment are shared with all stakeholders involved in the project. Following the completion of the climate risk assessment, the procured climate analysts and engineering firms will undertake the following:

### Analyse the project’s vulnerability
The analysis will assess the exposure to the hazards. This includes identifying the project and beneficiaries’ susceptibility to damage if exposed to the hazards, and the ability of the system or its features to absorb shocks and resist impacts. We can do this by assessing the project bringing forward the impacts and root causes identified.

#### Assessment of materiality
The materiality of an asset’s exposure to physical climate risks (and its climate resilience) includes a quantification of the likelihood of the asset to specific climate risks in terms of physical damages and associated repair costs, reduction in its availability or usability, and potential increase in its operating and maintenance costs. The assessment will also include an identification of the materiality thresholds. This is meant to identify materiality thresholds based on a combination of engineering, environmental, legal, commercial, financial, social and economic factors. The results of this analysis will be shared with the manager to meet their ongoing obligations to their various stakeholders in the face of chronic and acute climate change impacts. It will also provide information for the project’s risk management plan.

#### Sector entities which may have granted the right to build and operate the asset with certain conditions, local communities (including indigenous peoples) from and around the project area, as well as other sector entities which may have granted the right to build and operate the asset with certain conditions, local communities (including indigenous peoples) from and around the project area.

The impact assessment will determine whether or not several physical climate risks are material to the asset.

### Quantify impacts on KPIs
This analysis will identify and prioritize key risks, and for prioritized risk, it will quantify the scale of financial and non-financial impacts. These will be differentiated by impacts felt directly by the asset owner (e.g., structural damage leading to downtime of the asset) and those reduced cash flow as well as expenses for recovery and repair (e.g., increased maintenance costs). The analysis will also consider indirect impacts on other sector entities which may have granted the right to build and operate the asset with certain conditions, local communities (including indigenous peoples) from and around the project area.

### Interdependencies and systemic approach
Compared to the vulnerability analysis, risk assessment more readily facilitates identification of longer term consequences of climate change. It will also identify interdependencies between risks (technical, environmental, social and financial etc.) and allow for the interactions between factors to be considered. This is in line with a systems thinking approach. Hence, a risk assessment may well identify impacts that are not immediately obvious from a vulnerability analysis.

ICRF project assessments will also integrate a system approach which is a fundamental requirement for sustainable infrastructure investments, leveraging ACP’s experience and track record in infrastructure development projects. The system approach involves looking at the interconnections between assets (technical, environmental, social and financial etc.) and how they interact with each other within the asset chain. These boundaries and interdependencies are important for scoring risk and benefits assessments, as well as for understanding the resilience of a project to climate change impacts in terms of which part it can. This will ensure that the target investments improve the ability of assets and system to persist, adapt and transform in the face of climate-related stresses and shocks in a timely manner, and avoid further risks and negative consequences.

The system approach also allows for the identification of opportunities for co-benefits creation and maximize benefits and potential environmental and climate outcomes.


---
**Page 55**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 51

When selecting adaptation measures for the new build project, it is important to consider how the measures might affect the whole system and its vulnerability. High-level, broadly qualitative risk assessments can be undertaken in the early phases of the asset lifecycle, with more detailed quantitative assessments conducted later.

Infrastructure assets such as Transport, Energy, Information Communication Technology (ICT), will often have interdependence. These sectors rely heavily on each other to deliver their services, creating interdependencies that are not always obvious and may not be fully understood by decision-makers. As a result of climate change, these interdependencies have become increasingly interconnected. As a result, when one part of this system of systems stops working, the whole system is affected. The vulnerability of infrastructure assets to climate change is increased because interdependencies are an additional vulnerability. They amplify existing climate risks and emphasize the need to consider resilience as being a cross-sector, systemic issue.

As an example, Transport infrastructure is critical for functioning across sectors. As shown in Figure 6.3b, the Transport infrastructure is highly connected to all other infrastructure sectors, primarily because it provides a direct supply link. For low-income countries in Africa, the road network is the primary mode of transportation and is often used as a substitute for rail or air transport. The road network has knock-on effects in passenger and freight traffic, with knock-on effects on an already vulnerable area, affecting farmers (who are more likely to be affected by climate change), businesses, and other infrastructure facilities; accidents (road safety); trade corridors, all of which leading to significant negative impacts on the economy.

Figure B3.8: Sectoral interdependencies and their relationships (Transport, Energy and ICT which are part of ICRF sectors have significant interdependencies)

Part 3: Identity and Appraisal of Resilience Measures

Identify a range of options for the asset and quantify potential impact.

Once one or more physical climate risks have been determined to be material, a series of resilience options are identified. These options will offer incremental investments to improve the design of the asset (such as increased maintenance or planned shutdowns) or to reduce its vulnerability. Each option should be quantified with its range of economic, environmental, and social benefits identified.

Economic and financial analysis: Appraisal of resilience options – technical, economic and financial appraisal of each resilience option is conducted qualitatively or quantitatively given the key climate risks to the project, considering future scenarios. The analysis will identify a single option to incorporate into the project to strengthen its resilience.

This step will identify the economic feasibility of the project without resilience measures and the net benefits associated with incorporating resilience options for the project. To do so, estimate the net present value (NPV) and IRR of the project with no resilience measures based on the physical impacts of climate change on the project as determined in the climate risk assessment. Use a relevant time horizon, typically the full project life-of-the asset, using an account rate according to national or international standards.

The key steps of the economic analysis are:

- Estimate the net present value of the project with no resilience measures.
- Identify and quantify the incremental costs (costs and benefits) associated with alternative project designs that incorporate different resilience options.
- Update the NPV and IRR calculations to reflect the additional cost-benefits identified that are in addition to the direct resilience benefits. This is particularly relevant for Nbs, as these will often have knock-on benefits outside of the project itself (e.g., ecosystem services).
- Convert the cost and benefit flows into present values using the discount rate chosen according to national or international standards.
- Compare the estimated incremental costs of project design with the resilience option and the benefits of integrating that resilience option.
- Consider sensitivity analysis to account for uncertainties.
- Undertake a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) to appraise the resilience options.
- Rank the NPV and IRR of the alternative resilience options.

GCFC concessional funding: AFACIF will appraise the resilience options using a combination of methods described above. The incremental costs, benefits, and net present value (NPV) associated with each cost of resilience measures shall be documented. At the sub-project level, the portion of GCFC funding as part of the project’s total budget should be determined.

Illustrative application of Part 1-3 decision process for climate risk assessment and identification of resilience measures

Expected output will be derived to classify assets into the following three categories based on an array of risks associated with physical hazards:

i. Low exposure, indicating assets are in areas hardly exposed to relevant climate hazards,
ii. Moderate exposure, indicating assets are in areas more frequently exposed to relevant climate hazards; and

## High exposure, indicating assets are in areas extremely exposed to climate hazards

The combined assessment of the level of vulnerability and the expected exposure to a hazard will allow ICRF to develop an indicative level of climate risk for different assets. Scores for hazards and vulnerabilities will be assigned on a scale from 1 (low) to 5 (high). The following table provides guidance as to how assets are proceeding to deal execution stage and where Tier 5 investments are deprioritized due to combined high vulnerability and exposure.

| Tier | Indicating a combination of low risks emerging from vulnerability and exposure to climate hazards; |
| --- | --- |
| Tier 2 | Indicating a combination of low exposure to climate hazards but medium or high exposure to climate hazards; |
| Tier 3 | Indicating moderate risks emerging from vulnerability and exposure to climate hazards; |
| Tier 4 | Indicating a combination of moderate and high risks emerging from vulnerability and exposure to climate hazards; |
| Tier 5 | Indicating a combination of high risks emerging from vulnerability and exposure to climate hazards. |

### Part 4: Monitoring ICRF Resilience Metrics & Impact Tracking

Following the integration of climate risk management levers at the project development stage and eventual deal closure, the focus regarding climate risks moves on ongoing monitoring and management of climate risks and resilience metrics. This will be a key element in the review of the investment portfolio during the investment process. Periodic re-appraisals will allow us to recognize and manage risks emerging from changes in respective asset classes or markets, as well as new opportunities for improving the performance of assets, e.g., if new technology is available that can effectively contribute to reducing climate risks.

### Climate Risk (CR) = Exposure (E) x Vulnerability (V)

Climate risk is a function of exposure and vulnerability. Alt. alternatively R = V * E. Vulnerability and Exposure are non-weighted items and the order in which 'V' and 'E' are included in a risk function does not alter climate risks.

### Sectors Breakdown

#### Climate Resilience Indicators
- **Value of physical assets made more resilient to the effects of climate change (in USD)**
  - Reduced expected financial losses due to climate-change related events affecting infrastructure assets [amount of reduced expected financial exposure]
  - Reduced repair/maintenance costs because of climate-related events affecting infrastructure assets [amount in USD]
  - Reduced frequency and duration of outages due to climate related events (e.g., road blockages, power outage, port disruption, etc.) [number of disruptions]
- **Duration or downtime of infrastructure assets affected by climate-related events (in days)**
  - Reduced duration or downtime of infrastructure assets because of climate-related events affecting infrastructure assets [amount in USD]
- **Beneficiaries (male/female) of improved climate resilience due to investments in climate-resilient infrastructure** [number of beneficiaries]
- **Reductions in mortality following acute climate events (e.g., flooding, heatwaves, and other climate resilient events)**

### B3.5 Indicative Incremental Cost Analysis – ICRF Fund
The GCF uses US$ 240m catalytic first loss equity investment will support the mobilization of funding scale including US$1.25 billion leverage financing at project level; US$ 460 million from various institutional partners and US$380 million in co-financing from private sector. The GCF’s investments are expected to generate returns on investments in climate resilient infrastructure assets. The GCF's funding will be used primarily to fund the incremental costs for adaptation and resilience to de-risk investors, which represents about 11.4% of the total funding required at project level.

Making infrastructure assets climate resilient requires incremental costs. That said, there is no universal incremental cost benchmark. The calculation of incremental cost is asset-specific and location-specific, and depends on the climate risk assessments and resilience measures that are fit-for-purpose for each infrastructure asset and context. Incremental costs can be estimated using a range of methods depending on climatic events, depending on their nature and type, their technical and engineering design and their vulnerability to climate change impacts. The incremental cost calculation is typically done at the project level and is done on a case-by-case basis based on the technical specifications and structural elements of that asset component.

Climate proofing and ensuring Paris-alignment of global infrastructure investment will require premium of 9-27%. The incremental cost is calibrated on case-by-case basis and informed by the climate risk assessment and cost-benefit analysis. The methodology for calculating the incremental costs is based on the Climate-Resilient Infrastructure Costs (CRICs) developed by the Global Center on Adaptation (GCA) the High-Level Steering Group including all the Multilateral Development Banks: ADB, AfDB, ADB, EBRD, EIB, USAID, and World Bank.

Some studies provide a wide range of incremental costs varying from 3% to twenty-three (23%) point percent resulting from small frequency and magnitude of climate change hazard impact and depending on the asset type. For example, the GCA report (GCA, 2019) provides an extensive study on the Program for Infrastructure Development in Africa (PIDA) focusing on road and bridge infrastructure assets. The study found that the incremental cost varies from 3% to 27%. According to the World Bank (2020), and the total construction cost without adaptation is estimated at $728 billion. Excluding climate costs, the increment bottom line is 5%, up to 19% of this amount (representing an increase of 5% to 23%). The report also provides a framework for assessing climate related risks to road investments in Africa. A key finding is that, in the period from the present to


---
**Page 60**
---

2030, climate change could cause direct damages of tens of billions of dollars in damages to roads, which will require additional maintenance to preserve basic serviceability. preliminary estimation of damage to bridges suggests costs may be even higher (in excess of $15 billion, mean estimate). Apart from increased maintenance and repair costs, there are also the indirect economic impacts on the movement of goods and people, all of which has a substantial economic cost.

AFC collaborated on a similar analysis with the University of Oxford’s Programme for Sustainable Development to assess the potential physical damages on 121 African ports in (43) countries. Physical port asset damages and revenue losses equally large (both around $130 million USD per year) as present, future revenue losses will start to dominate, under high expected trade scenario

* **Physical asset damages:** 400 - 550 million USD per year depending on RCP scenario (translating to US$ 8.6 billion to US$ 12.3 billion by 2050)
* **Revenue losses:** 390 – 1230 million USD per year depending on RCP + trade scenario (translating to US$ 7.8 billion to US$4.8 billion by 2050)

## Grey, green and blue infrastructure can be defined more precisely as follows:

### Grey Infrastructure - Grey infrastructure is built up, engineered physical structures, often made of concrete, steel, asphalt, etc. Examples include roads, bridges, buildings, dams, levees, canals, energy ports, docks, embankments, sea walls, cemeteries and breakwaters for riverine and coastal flood protection, piped drainage systems, water treatment plants, cooling towers, power lines, transmission lines, sewers, stormwater management systems, air conditioning or cooling centers to cope with extreme heat.

### Green Infrastructure - Green infrastructure is built up, engineered physical structures characterized by well-functioning biological systems, primarily related to green spaces, that support biodiversity, natural ecological processes and to which the climate can be adapted. Examples include urban parks, gardens, wetlands, mangroves, salt marshes, coral reefs, sea grasses, sand beaches and dunes in the coast environment and many other types of coastal ecosystems.

### Blue Infrastructure - Blue infrastructure can also be characterized by well-functioning biological systems, primarily related to water bodies, that support biodiversity, natural ecological processes and to which the climate can be adapted. Examples include rivers, lakes, reservoirs, streams, as well as estuaries, seas, and oceans.

## B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### B.4.1 Executive Summary - Implementation arrangements

AFC will be responsible for the overall oversight of the framework implementation and will report to the GCF per the terms of the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). For managing the ICRF resources, a GCF Account will be set up within AFC to administer the GCF funds as follows:

1. AFC (in the name and on behalf of the GCF) will subscribe as the investor of record for the Junior Investment Capital Commitment (the “Junior Commitment”) under the Junior Subscription Agreement (as defined below) to be entered into between the ICRF and AFC (the “Capital Commitment Agreement”). The Junior Commitment, in its capacity as an executing entity of the ICRF, will agree, during a 10-year period from as soon as the first closing of the ICRF (the “Commitment Period”) to make capital contributions in US Dollars to the ICRF on each payment date determined by AFC. The Junior Commitment will be subject to the terms and conditions set forth in accordance with the investment policy of the ICRF, to pay the ICRF’s obligations, expenses and other liabilities as they arise from time to time. The Junior Commitment will also be required to pay an amount equal to its pro rata share of the aggregate capital contributions to be made by all investors in the ICRF on each payment date determined by AFC. The Junior Commitment is subject to a cap with respect to its downward Capital Commitment. The ICRF is targeting a total fund raise of US $750 million, therefore GCF will not be the sole investor in any company.

2. With the approval and authorization of AFC (in its capacity as an executing entity of the ICRF), the ICRF, in its capacity as an Executing Entity, will make investments in the relevant projects (each a “Fund Investment”). A Fund Investment may include, without limitation, investments in assets, in securities such as equity or debt instruments, in real estate, and/or other financial products or arrangements as means to achieve the overall objective of the Fund. The Fund Investments may involve a variety of transaction structures including but not limited to equity, project development, expansion financings, leveraged and unleveraged acquisitions, etc.

3. The Fund Investments will be selected, invested and managed by ACP:

a. in accordance with the AMA and the FAA through the Subsidiary Agreement(s) (as defined below); and

b. in cooperation with AFC under the terms of the Support Services Agreement(s) and the Co-Investment Agreement as described above under Outcome 1.

- **Key responsibilities and agreements defining the project's parameters include implementation agreements with the government, project capacity and elements such as the required climate risk assessment and environmental & social impact studies summarizing obligations to the development of renewable energy projects. The project will be implemented through a concession agreement or an off-taker Agreement, which for renewable energy, transport infrastructure, and other core infrastructure projects, is typically structured on a build-operate-transfer (BOT) basis.**

- **Revenues for the project are generated through a concession agreement or an off-taker Agreement, which for renewable energy, transport infrastructure, and other core infrastructure projects, is typically structured on a build-operate-transfer (BOT) basis. The revenue stream will be domiciled in the AFC member countries in which the projects are based, with or without the involvement of a local partner, as appropriate.**

- **Revenues for the project are generated through a concession agreement or an off-taker Agreement, which for renewable energy, transport infrastructure, and other core infrastructure projects, is typically structured on a build-operate-transfer (BOT) basis. The revenue stream will be domiciled in the AFC member countries in which the projects are based, with or without the involvement of a local partner, as appropriate.**

- **The construction works are arranged through an Equipment Supply Agreement (ESA) and Balance of Plant (BOP) agreement, usually contracted into an Engineering, Procurement & Construction (EPC) Contract. The number of EPC contractors used differs per sector and contract type. Key elements in the EPC contracts include: i) scope of work; ii) price and payment structure including advance payment guarantees. Other key elements are: iii) performance bonds; iv) insurance; v) quality assurance; vi) environmental, physical climate risks and adverse weather conditions); vii) performance bonds; warranty periods; viii) liquidated damages; ix) claims and disputes resolution; x) EPC specific sector risk matrix; xi) supervising the construction activities and engage independent engineering consultants as required.**

- **The Operations & Maintenance (O&M) Contract deals with the activities for maintaining the project at a certain performance level. The performance or availability level that the O&M contractor guarantees is one of the critical components next to the scope of work, price and insurance where applicable to mitigate against financial losses in the event of operational disruption. The independent engineer is required to review the following: i) the design of the suite to key documents. The independent engineer is broadly required to: i) review of the drawings and specifications; ii) review of the project management plan; iii) review of inspection and monitoring of construction works including the climate resilience measures; iv) conducting tests on completed works including completing competitive tendering; v) reviewing inspection and testing results of O&M as well as the climatic conditions. The independent engineer will be required under the agreement, the costs of any works or services and/or their reasonableness. vii) determining whether the project is in compliance with the terms and conditions of the contract when performing any duty or obligation; viii) assisting the parties in resolution of disputes; and undertaking such other duties as may be agreed by the parties from time to time.**

- **The Financing and Shareholders' Agreements are a set of legal documents that deal with the terms and conditions of the debt and equity finance, sponsor support, cash waterfll and security.**

- **Financing for infrastructure projects can take a number of forms but is typically financed on an owner's balance sheet by way of corporate finance or on a limited or non-resource basis through project financing. A key difference between the two approaches lies in how financing is structured (i.e., whether it is debt-based, equity-based, or a combination to leverage or preserve the owner's equity; among other reasons) alongside the equity owned by the shareholders. Debt-based financing typically involves the use of debt instruments generated by the project alone (additionally secured by available assets and guarantees), while corporate finance leverages the companies' balance sheet, not the cash flows. ## B

### Key point in project finance: The cash flows from the project alone must be sufficient to service debt (e.g., project debt without corporate guarantees). The magnitude of the cash flow that is deemed sufficient depends on the risk profile of the project. The process of structuring a project finance facility involves many complexities and often costly conditions, the covenants imposed by lenders put a degree of inflexibility into the financing structure, which makes it difficult to adapt to changing circumstances or to take advantage of opportunities in later years. The use of equity from the ICFR and ACF is designed to remove this complexity time lag and rigidity, and thereby increase the probability of a project reaching successful operation.

## 5. For the implementation of Outcomes 2 & Outcome 3 (Sections 2.1.4 both AFC and ACF will be co-executing entities). AFC will be the executing entity for all other activities under Outcome 2 and Outcome 3. ACF will implement the activities in partnership with ACF (fund management) and GCF (funding). The relationship between AFC, ACF, and GCF is expected to be signed between AFC and the GCF. AFC will hold and manage the grant resources and make a direct payment to ACF for implementation of the project activities. All projects under Outcome 2 & Outcome 3 must be approved by AFC and ACF and will sign a technical assistance agreement (TA Agreement) in this regard, which will cover both Outcomes 2 & Outcome 3.

### B.2 Governance Structure of the ICFR

The ICFR is the sole shareholder of ACF. In its role as AE and sole shareholder of ACF, AFC will oversee the project implementation process and ensure that all activities are in line with the GCF’s Strategic Plan and AMLO/IT policy. ESS policies such as the ESMP are passed down to ACF, the Fund level and portfolio company-level. Furthermore, as the Accredited Entity it will require and ACF and the ICFR to support the fulfillment of its obligations under the Agreement with GCF. The AE will be responsible for ensuring that the GCF will ensure that any Subsidiary Agreement it enters into with ACF or the ICFR on any fund management services, including but not limited to the Fund Management Services Agreement (FMSA) and AMA and will take appropriate action and/or exercise remedies available to the Fund documentation to comply with such (pending) breach of the applicable provisions under the AMA and/or FMSA to protect the interests of GCF.

In addition, it will dedicate full-time team members to monitor EEs’ compliance with FAA and AMA obligations. The AE will also be responsible for ensuring that ACF complies with all relevant reports, to dispute GFCD processes for the Funded Activity. It will assess achievement of program, targeted outcomes, and results as per logframe, project implementation plan, and final evaluation reports as per GCF’s requirements. The GCF will enter into Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) with the AFC, which will detail key implementation arrangements and other terms and conditions for the Funded Activity. The AE will also pass down AMA policies to ACF and the ICFR. These key terms will be summarized in the Term Sheet.

### B.3 Legal and Capital Structure of the ICFR

It is envisaged that the ICFR will be a newly formed Mauritius law limited partnership without legal personality (LLP) structure, and its general partner will be the General Partner, a newly formed limited liability company wholly controlled by AFC (via ACF) as the Accredited Entity. The General Partner will delegate investment decision-making authority to ACF pursuant to the Management Agreement.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 64

The capital of the ICRF will be appropriately divided into two tranches so as to attract multiple investor classes.

* **Tier 1 Interest** or "Junior Interest" will hold a junior equity position in the structure of the ICRF, which will absorb a portion of risk throughout the ICRF, acting as the principal entity to attract commercial capital and provide liquidity.
* **Tier 2 Interest** or "Senior Interest" will rank in a senior equity position. Investors in the Tier 2 Interest will benefit from first loss capital protection provided by investors in the Tier 1 interest. The Tier 2 Interest shall target institutional investors who are willing to accept adjusted returns, which would not have participated in the Fund without capital protection offered by GCF.

AFC acting as the EE will invest GCF's fund commitment in the Tier 1 Interest of the ICRF by absorbing high-risk and low-return investments. The AFC will also be responsible for structuring the ICRF to de-risk and mobilize commercial investors (including AFC’s commitment from the Fund) into its own balance sheet resources under the Tier 2 Interest. It is expected that GCF (via AFC) will be the sole investor in the Tier 1.

AFC (for its own account) intends to invest an amount equal to USD [50m] in the Tier 2 interest.

## Management Agreement

ACP and the general partner of the ICFR will enter into a management agreement (the "Management Agreement") to govern the relationship between ACP and the general partner of the ICFR. In its capacity as manager for the ICFR, ACP shall be responsible for performing the following duties: managing the portfolio companies; making investment decisions on behalf of the ICFR; identifying and analyzing investment opportunities; negotiating the terms of investments; preparing documentation for relevant transactions; and exercising all other powers necessary or incidental to the performance of its duties. ACP will otherwise participate in the management or control of portfolio companies, including taking all actions necessary or convenient to enforce, amend, compromise, or modify any agreement, contract, or other instrumentality governing such company.

The General Partner shall have the power to appoint and remove the ACP as manager for the ICFR. The General Partner may also directly participate in, consult regarding, or review the management and business of the portfolio companies; provided that nothing herein shall be construed to limit the powers granted to the General Partner under its capacity for Portfolio Companies, exercising voting, consent, or approval rights in relation to shares and other securities held by such company. The Management Agreement will be governed by a Mauritian law and any disputes in relation thereto will be resolved through arbitration at the seat of arbitration.

Side Letter AFC, ICFR and the General Partner: Accredited Entity is proposing to enter into a Side Letter (represented by the general partner as executing entity) and the General Partner. Neither the Private Investment Fund nor the Subsidiary Subscription Agreement or a document substantially similar thereto, nor any other agreement between the Private Investment Fund and the General Partner, shall be deemed to constitute an amendment of the Side Letter. The Side Letter will be governed by English law and any disputes in relation thereto will be resolved through arbitration at the seat of arbitration.

Side Letter AFC, ICFR and the General Partner: Accredited Entity is proposing to enter into a Side Letter (represented by the general partner as executing entity) and the General Partner. Neither the Private Investment Fund nor the Subsidiary Subscription Agreement or a document substantially similar thereto, nor any other agreement between the Private Investment Fund and the General Partner, shall be deemed to constitute an amendment of the Side Letter. The Side Letter will be governed by English law and any disputes in relation thereto will be resolved through arbitration at the seat of arbitration.

### 4.7 Governance of the ICRF

It is envisaged that the Fund will be registered in a Mauritius, a leading Private Equity jurisdiction in Africa, set up and ultimately managed by the ACP. The legal nature of the Fund is subject to final due diligence following the registration of the Fund. The ICRF will have two branches: an equity branch and a debt branch carrying different risk/return profiles, as described in greater detail in the Term Sheet. The ICRF may be structured as a single entity or as separate entities. If the ICRF is to be structured as a single entity, it will have a general partner with limited personal liability in Mauritius (LGPL structure) which will include a subsidiary of ACP as the General Partner and the Investment Committee. The General Partner will manage the Fund on behalf of the investors. If the ICRF is to be structured as separate entities, only ACP (through the General Partner) may participate in the management of the LP. The remaining partners will have no role in the management of the LP and shall not have the power to bind the LP. The indicative governance of the Fund is described below:

**Advisory Committee (AC):** The ICRF will have an AC, which is composed of United Partnerships (LPS). The Advisory Committee will be comprised of members from investors selected by ACP in its discretion. The Advisory Committee may also include other independent experts as determined by the AC. The AC approves decisions of interest and/or consents to waivers of the investment policy agreed with the investors.

**The General Partner:** The General Partner will be responsible for the management of the assets of the Fund, including but not limited to: (i) managing the day-to-day operations of the Fund; (ii) performing all marketing functions related to the Fund, including investigating, analysing, structuring, and negotiating potential investments in accordance with the investment policy agreed by investors; and (iii) identifying and evaluating opportunities. The General Partner holds the decision-making power and has the exclusive authority to make decisions on behalf of the Fund. The General Partner is also attributed to avoid conflicts or any other situation that may affect the performance of their duties.

**ACP:** The General Partner shall appoint ACP to perform the day-to-day management and operation of the LP, including portfolio management and risk management pursuant to a Management Agreement to be entered into between the General Partner and ACP. At the level of ACP, the following governance structure is in place:

- **Board of Directors:** ACP's Board of Directors is comprised of senior manager of ACP and independent directors. The Board is chaired by the ACP CEO and is responsible for oversight of ACP’s operations, finances and conflict management.
  
- **Audit Committee:** The independent members of the Board will form the Audit Committee, which assists the Board in fulfilling its oversight responsibilities for the integrity of ACP's and the ICRF financial statements, the independent auditor's qualifications and the performance of the independent auditor.

- **Conflict Management Committee:** ACP will establish a best-practice procedure for handling actual or potential conflicts of interest. The Conflict Management Committee will be comprised of members forming from the Conflicts Committee, which reviews how conflicts of interest are managed, including steps taken to mitigate them.
  
- **Investment Committee:** Although ACP will leverage ACP services for investment sourcing, the ICRF will be managed by dedicated ACP teams and the decision to participate in any investment opportunity will be made independently. The Investment Committee will be responsible for approving investments on behalf of investors. The Investment Committees will include selected members of the Board of Directors of ACP, the Executive Management Committee of ACP, and any external independent members appointed by ACP.


---
**Page 76**
---

In addition, the following governance structure or policies will be set up:

- **Risk Committee:** Prior to any final IC meeting, the Risk Committee is required to confirm approval of the deal including sign-off on legal, financial, technical, ESG, climate and other diligence items. If actions are required pre-signing or post-completion, then the deal team prepares a Corrective Action Plan, completed and signed off by the Risk Committee prior to final IC approval. Approvals and Corrective Action Plans are recorded prior to final IC by the deal team.

- **Internal Compliance Committees:** ACP will establish policies, procedures, and governance structures to identify, assess, mitigate, monitor, and report on compliance risks associated with the Fund’s activities on ACF’s existing applicable policies. It prioritizes adherence to the highest standards of corporate governance, including but not limited to: anti-money laundering and anti-corruption; prevention of money laundering issues from anti-bribery and anti-corruption, prevention of sexual harassment at the workplace, anti-money laundering and anti-corruption compliance, and equal opportunity for employees.

- **Corporate Compliances:** All fund-related compliances will be outsourced to a law firm. For the Fund, Investment Manager and Portfolio companies, corporate secretarial compliances will be outsourced to a Company Secretary appointed by ACF in accordance with ACF’s existing policies.

- **Investment Approval/Investment Process:** The Investment Manager will apply a three-tiered approach to investment and divestment approval. The investment team together with the respective risk and internal compliance committees performs an initial review of the investment opportunity, which is then forwarded for Investment Committee approval. ICF only invests in projects that meet eligibility criteria of the GCF.

- **Internal Compliance Processes (Anti-Money Laundering and Anti-Corruption Policy):** ACP takes into consideration the potential risks of money laundering and corruption in local projects by developing robust anti-money laundering and anti-corruption policies and assessing potential risks already in investment opportunities. The policy will be based on ACF’s existing AML policy.

- **Anti-Money Laundering:** ACP will have an anti-money laundering policy, which will be designed as per local legislation and industry best practices. Any such policy will be based on ACF’s existing AML policy. This policy will include a risk assessment process for money laundering risks in projects, including the check which investor poses money laundering risk. Transactions that are suspicious in nature (the source of funds or moving large cash investments) shall be considered as part of the anti-money laundering policy.

- **Anti-Corruption Policy:** This policy, to be based on ACP’s anti-corruption policy, will be applicable to ACP and all personnel employed by it or acting for or on behalf of ACP wherever they are located such as any third-party service providers. The policy will include a risk assessment process for corruption risks in projects, including the check which investor poses corruption risk. It will also include compliance and sanctions, diligence procedures, where such procedures are “gate-checking” requirements to ensure that there is no corruption risk associated with the project. ACP will have processes and mechanisms for heightened compliance review. IDD Business Integrity checks are undertaken by independent third parties who report the results to the Head of Operations along with the relevant Deal Contracts for review.

### 4.8 Track record of AE and EE: AE was established in 2007 to be the catalyst for private sector-led infrastructure investment across Africa, while EE is a public-private partnership institution as a pre-investment development facility. ACP’s approach combines institutional expertise with a focus on financial and technical advisory, project structuring, project development and risk capital to address the financing gap in Africa. The two entities have been successful in developing and investing in high-quality infrastructure assets that provide essential services in the core infrastructure sectors of power,


---
**Page 77**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 73

natural resources-heavy industry, transport, and telecommunications. To date, the Corporation has invested over USD 10 billion in projects in 35 countries across Africa. AFC is committed to investing in projects that will provide substantial benefits to a region or sector on the long term and will be able to leverage additional infrastructure investments in the process. The Corporation aims to mitigate the short-term social, economic, and environmental impacts. Where possible, the projects will utilize local resources and technologies, and where possible, they will be co-financed by other development partners.

Over the past several years, AFC has been radically rethinking its approach in response to the ever-growing needs of Africa's growing population.

AFC is now one of the leading infrastructure investors in renewable energy on the African continent. In terms of AFC's track record, its renewable energy platform (Letisia) and portfolio include 1.4 GW of operational capacity and 2.5 GW under development. The Letisia platform has been a key driver that led several award-winning renewable energy projects in Africa such as the 26MW Cabelecida wind farm project, generating electricity for over 70,000 households; the 138 MW Kafue hydropower plant; the 60MW wind power project; and the 450 MW Mphanda Nkuwa hydropower plant. AFC also owns and operates the country's first renewable energy plant, 44MW Hydro Power Plant at Simbawane (Simpangwe) and 430MW Nachitja hydroelectric plant in Cameroon.

The Corporation has invested $1.28 billion in the Transpo sector. As logistics such as ports and roads which are important for trade and economic activities such as flooding, sea level rise, precipitation, high temperatures, etc., As a response, AFC's strategy is to integrate climate resilience as part of its programme on infrastructure investments. The Corporation has been carrying out an extensive review and analysis of the occurrence of various climate threats and the potential impacts. The Takoradi port in Ghana, which was built by AFC in 2013, is one example of a project that has been designed to be more resilient to climate stresses such as sea-level rise, storm surge, temperature, among others. In the road sector, AFC also plays a key role in the development of the Trans-African Road Bridge (TARBI) which is a US$1 billion project under construction on the Abidjan-Bouake highway, a 6.4-kilometer highway and 1.9-kilometer bridge connecting Abidjan's residential district directly with the commercial district.

Rather than simply providing funding for individual projects, AFC now seeks to take a wholesale approach to infrastructure investments by investing in every step of the value chain through financing ecosystems that enable project competitiveness and further alleviate high social, economic, and environmental risks that governments face when they lack the necessary local capacity. Nowhere is this more evident than in AFC's track record of delivering projects at the highest rated institutions in Africa – an A329 rating from Moody’s – AFC can deliver on ambitious projects, manage investments, and pursue the highest levels of corporate governance, transparency and sustainable development. By leveraging its unique position as a multilateral development bank with high investment grade credit status, member countries can de-risk development projects and access capital at a cost they would be forced to pay if they were to seek funding from other sources. AFC has also established a robust pipeline under management by 2023 to address the urgent and important mandate of developing and financing climate-resilient infrastructure in Africa. The Corporation is committed to supporting sustainable economic growth of the African continent for enhanced productivity. AFC has sought to address the wider infrastructure divide by originating, structuring, and executing projects in Africa (Figure B-4.8).

## International institution Established by Treaty

AFC is an international multilateral institution established by treaty between independent sovereign states.

### AFC Capital Partners (ACP) EE track record.

AFC Capital Partners (ACP) is the wholly owned asset management subsidiary of Africa Finance Corporation (AFC) – a leading infrastructure solutions provider on the continent. The ACP's inaugural launch in 2017 was designed to leverage the scale and reach of the AFC’s extensive experience across Africa and a track record with more than US$2 billion in climate investments. ACP aims to provide institutional investors with a unique access to attractive and de-risked investment opportunities in low carbon infrastructure projects, leveraging the depth and breadth of AFC's investment track record on infrastructure projects with comprehensive climate considerations.

Considering all the above, it is evident that:

- The scarcity of domestic public resources of locally-controlled governments in many African countries to meet the need for traditional investments in infrastructure constrains their capacity to finance climate-resilient infrastructures.
- The high upfront cost of these investments modifies the risk profile and reduces the appetite for this type of investment, and
- The need for GCF investment in the ICRF capital replenishment (to increase the size of the fund and mandate to finance resilience), to facilitate the development of enabling measures and de-risk overall investment to attract private sector.

The rationale above justifies the need for bold responses to make ICRF a viable solution to market barriers and deliver on impacts that are core to the ICRF replenishment thesis.

With Africa being the most vulnerable continent to climate change, mainstreaming climate change is a key requirement for the long-term viability of its infrastructure. Financing Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) in Africa will require an additional $13 trillion by 2050 (which includes USD 291 billion for adaptation) over the next decade. Yet, there are limited capital flows to climate-resilient infrastructure and private sector finance to bridge this gap by removing current market barriers that limit a greater flow of capital in low carbon and climate-resilient projects in Africa.

Removing these barriers will help address concerns about the cost and the bankability of infrastructure projects in general, which are also relevant for projects that will need to be climate proofed. These include, among other things, high real and perceived risks associated with these investments, weak institutional capacity, and limited access to finance. Creating a supportive enabling environment will be critical to driving more climate-resilient infrastructure investment.

A particular challenge for funding climate-resilient infrastructure is that many of the benefits may be hard to monetize, particularly for protective infrastructure such as flood defenses. An optimal risk-sharing mechanism should therefore be developed and agreed upon at the outset of a project. This determination is typically undertaken at the outset of the project, during the project conceptualization and design phase. The resilience agenda brings a new dimension in this: "given that the need to consider how the allocation of climate-related risks will affect investors' incentives to manage these risks.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 77

Within this context, public finance providers can use various tools to allocate risks effectively and bridge the bankability gap for climate-resilient infrastructure. These tools include:

- **Project preparation support**, in the form of technical and financial assistance to project owners or concessionaires. This is particularly important given the potential additional complexity of considering climate resilience.
- **Blended finance** can be used to support investment in climate-resilient infrastructure. In this context, GCF can play an instrumental role by mobilizing institutional capital at scale by improving the risk-return profile of investments in climate-resilient infrastructure through innovative financial instruments and mechanisms. For instance, blended finance can use credit enhancement instruments such as first loss concessional equity and insurance co-risks physical and commercial risks.
- **Expanding the role** of entities that integrate resilience are likely to play a key role in this regard by providing a standard for integrating climate risk into financial actors. A particular area of interest relates to the potential of insurance that could be developed for climate-resilient infrastructure.

- **Insuring new and existing Infrastructure against future risks due to climate change** could be an anchor for long-term financing, as it would provide a mechanism by which project finance insurance would need to reflect climate resilience as a potential avenue to internalize resilience into the project financial structure and annual financing costs. However, this depends on the availability and commercial viability of such products

## Climate Insurance
Improve the credit rating of infrastructure projects by reducing climate change risk and commercial risks.
With the support of GCF, ICFR will provide insurance products to mitigate residual climate risks.

Africa needs to reduce its massive infrastructure deficit to realize its full development potential, achieve structural transformation and market integration. The main constraint is the continent’s limited domestic resources for financing infrastructure projects. This results in a significant infrastructure investment gap or deficit with the need for additional billions to make these infrastructures climate-resilient and ensure they have the capacity to withstand extreme weather events. The GCF has been working on the enhancement of Africa's legal and regulatory framework for investment in climate-resilient infrastructure, promoting the development of an appropriate necessary concessional finance, and reducing efficiency gaps prevailing in existing infrastructure structure.

Private Sector, principally financial institutions, have demonstrated an increased appetite for infrastructure investments that are aligned with the objectives set by the green economy and sustainable infrastructure structure. Private equity infrastructure funds have also been increasing their participation and relevance in this space. The growth is attributable to the rising opportunities in physical assets induced by the strengthening of global demand for climate-resilient infrastructure, which has become a key driver of investment. This plays an important facilitation role by becoming more and more involved in projects at the early stage of development. The regulatory framework for private sector investments in Africa has been strengthened over time to ensure that appropriate environmental and social considerations have been accounted for. The regulatory framework is also designed to provide adequate protection against climate change risks, thereby ensuring the resilience of current and future infrastructure modifies the risk profile of the investment and creditworthiness of the project. This makes it possible to attract private sector investments necessary to de-risk this type of investment and lock in other strategic investors, including institutional investors.

Climate change has destroyed various physical assets or disrupted the service delivery of infrastructures, thus creating an obstacle to achieving the necessary economic and development goals. Thus, there is a strong need for infrastructure projects that are climate-resilient. This will help reduce the risk of failure, increase infrastructure maintenance costs, shorten asset life, and increase their rate of depreciation. Additionally, it will also reduce the cost of insurance premiums. The proportion of infrastructure assets from institutional investors into infrastructure are low—approximately 1 percent out of total asset allocations. Moreover, there is a lack of awareness among institutional investors about climate change in developing countries, especially in Africa. Infrastructure projects require high amounts of finance at the project conception stage and over their entire lifecycle. This makes it difficult for private sector investors to raise funds needed to make infrastructure climate-resilient. Those high costs of capital will often render infrastructure projects financially unfeasible.

Concessional finance is increasingly needed from the GCF to reduce risks sufficiently to blend other public and private sources of capital and accelerate deployment of investments in climate-resilient and more sustainable infrastructures. In addition, GCF concessional finance is needed to support risk assessment and mitigation measures that are required for infrastructure projects to withstand extreme weather conditions to reduce the risk across the infrastructure project lifecycle.

B. 8. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The ICRF programme is designed to remove market barriers and mainstream climate resilience as “THE NORM” in the design, financing, construction, operation and maintenance of targeted infrastructure assets in the built environment. ICRF is the first of its kind climate resilient infrastructure fund on the African Continent. The programme will create a sustainable pathway for scaling up investments in climate resilience infrastructure projects. These include, among other things, high risk and perceived risks associated with these investments, weaknesses in the enabling environment, weak capacity at all levels to manage climate change related risks, lack of adequate insurance coverage, poor project preparation, lack of standards in building climate resilient infrastructure. The programme will create a sustainable pathway for scaling up investments in climate resilience infrastructure projects. These include, among other things, high risk and perceived risks associated with these investments, weaknesses in the enabling environment, weak capacity at all levels to manage climate change related risks, lack of adequate insurance coverage, poor project preparation, lack of standards in building climate resilient infrastructure. The programme will create a sustainable pathway for scaling up investments on scale. ICRF will create a sustainable path to blending public and private capital to unlock investment opportunities in climate resilience infrastructure projects. This will be achieved by providing sources to increase bankability and cover the incremental CAPEX that is relevant to the climate-resilience upgrade of targeted infrastructure assets. The programme will also provide support for capacity building on operational management integrating resilience options throughout the life cycle of the targeted infrastructure asset.

In this way, ICRF ensures that commercial investors can meet their investment hurdle rates. The benefits and risks of the ICRF investments will be captured through monitoring and evaluation process during the projects’ life cycle. The benefits will include savings in operations and maintenance (O&M), disaster risk reduction, increased resilience to climate change impacts, reduced insurance costs, and use of climate risk insurance for long-term assets.

Components 2 & 3 have [10] year life span which is deemed sufficient to capture its impact on improving the enabling environment. It will be supported by a range of ICRF activities including support of national infrastructure regulatory framework including construction codes system, and climate data and standards preparation. The programme will also provide technical assistance for capacity building in terms of skills, and data will be made available for programme countries. ICP’s network of investors, climate think tanks and centres of excellence, and even the GCF Climate Network (C-Net) through community of practices will be engaged to support the development of a robust enabling environment for investment in climate data for climate-resilient infrastructure development in Africa. Component 3 implementation plan underlines the importance of building an enabling environment for investment in climate change risk data collection and analysis model. As a recent member of Coalition for Climate Resilience Investment, ICP is committed to supporting the coalition’s efforts.

The desired outcome of the programme is to establish climate resilient infrastructure as a distinctive CBI Asset Classes. With GCF’s co-investment ICRF acquires to deliver a proof of concept that will provide comfort to commercial investors and other stakeholders in Africa to invest in climate resilience infrastructure projects. The desired outcome will be positively reinforced by the creation of enabling environment driven by an informed policy making, knowledge creation and sharing and climate data infrastructure.

Exit: The ICRF fund will exit its portfolio investments in sub-projects through strategic trade sales to strategic investors. Any proceeds from such sales will be reinvested into other projects. From the investment exits will be reforged to GCF and other investors in accordance with the term sheet. After a successful implementation of the programme, ICRF will have demonstrated its ability to unlock private capital for climate resilience infrastructure assets at scale. The fund will have demonstrated the investment case and long-term viability of integrating climate resilience in infrastructure projects. This will be achieved by demonstrating that it is possible to deploy climate risk mitigation measures on a large scale for this new climate resilient infrastructure asset class.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/program against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

**D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

Potential of the project/program to contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas.


The aim and the purpose of the proposed Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF) is to increase climate resilience in Africa by protecting infrastructure assets from climate events such as rising temperatures, changing rainfall patterns and rising sea levels as well as to provide access to markets, energy and trade. The ICRF will also contribute to improved livelihoods.

By providing concessional financing, ICRF will primarily cover the adaptation and resilience costs necessary to catalyze funding for interventions to adapt infrastructure to climate change in four targeted sectors and create market for: Climate Resilient Transport and Logistics; Climate Resilient Energy systems. Climate Resilient Water and Sanitation; Climate Resilient Urban Infrastructure.

By promoting successful adaptive approaches to improve the long-term resilience of infrastructure, ICRF aims to provide a substantial contribution towards increased resilience for Infrastructure and built environment in Africa. The targeted countries are 15 African countries with GCF NOLs, most of which are least developed countries (LDCs). The total value of infrastructure assets expected to be protected against climate events is about USD 2 billion. The programme is also targeting the urban poor who represent a significant proportion of the population in these countries representing 8.5% and 20.1% vis-à-vis the total population of ICRF countries. Targeted beneficiaries are those living in vulnerable areas, including informal settlements, slums, peri-urban areas, etc., exposed to climate change, related service disruption associated with potential adverse economic impacts. It is expected that the programme will contribute to the unlocking of long-term climate-resilient infrastructure to a significant degree.

ICRF investment decision-making is based on mandatory climate risks screening and assessments including through consideration of local and infrastructure specific climate stresses and prioritization of resilience and adaptation measures for projects / programmes. By systematically mainstreaming climate change adaptation into project design, ICRF will contribute to the achievement of long-term sustainability in infrastructure. Long-term sustainability can be built from-the-start as a core consideration of infrastructure finance. Such good practices will contribute to the unlocking of long-term climate-resilient infrastructure to a significant degree.

Investing in the resilience of infrastructure (in the targeted African countries with GCF NOLs) is the core investment thesis of the ICRF. This way, ICRF will contribute to bridge the Africa infrastructure gap in a climate resilient manner. The programme will provide access to services and facilities for people living in and from rural areas, as well as access to markets, energy and trade. By doing so, the programme technology will improve people’s livelihoods through enhanced access to a reliable, safe, and affordable infrastructure base.

D2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Baseline: Climate change presents significant implications for infrastructure. Current design standards for infrastructure building are based on outdated, historical climate data; and today, future infrastructure planning is being made with a climate that does not yet exist. Infrastructure assets are sensitive not only to the existing climate at the time of their construction, but also to climate variations over the next 50 years. As such, the design standards for new infrastructure projects will be in place well into the middle of the century. In the face of climate change, planning, financing, operation, maintenance, and management of infrastructure need to be revised. Existing infrastructure stock constitutes a significant proportion of global greenhouse gas emissions. Most of it is located in vulnerable zones – most of them constructed in coastal zones in Africa; it is already vulnerable to today’s extreme weather. Climate change will make these vulnerabilities even more pronounced, with the potential for increasing others. To increase the resilience of both new and existing infrastructure, countries must be prepared to plan, finance, build, operate, maintain, and manage their infrastructure differently.

ICRF's paradigm shift potential: The paradigm shift lies in the programme’s holistic approach to building resilience to climate change by integrating strategies focused on climate adaptation, climate analytics, risk prevention, and disaster risk reduction. ICRF will introduce a new product to the market that can be monetized and upscaled: climate resilient infrastructure as an asset class. The integration of the specific elements described in the programme’s design and implementation will provide a unique opportunity for investors to access a new asset class with significant potential for the scaling up of activities based on the premise that following ICFR’s intervention, a successful project can be replicated elsewhere at scale.

ICRF will contribute to Systemic Resilience through:

- Improving government prioritization of national resilience infrastructure and developing a wide range of tools and resources to support governments in their task of early warning systems infrastructure delivery of enhanced capacity-building to create building codes, meteorological agencies, and technicians. ICRF aims to deliver actionable outcomes that can be used from the country level up to the international level. The programme will also provide technical assistance to the development of more effective NAPs, NDCs, national strategic plans and country programming.

- Infrastructure Asset Design & Structuring: ICRF develops a framework for the integration of climate change adaptation into infrastructure asset design and structuring in order to account for historical climate patterns to inform their asset designs. Often design is based on minimum regulatory requirements or best practices that are not necessarily aligned with the potential future climate. This results in investors to exceed these regulatory requirements due to limited or no knowledge of the potential climate risks (ICRF 1). Develop a framework to quantify the economic, social and financial benefits of incremental investments in resilience and use this GCF’s catalytic financing as a substantial and critical incentive for the financial sector to invest in climate resilient infrastructure.

- Financial Innovation and mobilizing funding at scale: The ICRF fund will blend commercial and concessional equity capital to mobilize institutional investors at scale for climate resilient infrastructure. The combination of public and private capital will be used to provide both technical assistance and the mobilization of institutional investors, while the commercial investors will provide equity required for the construction of projects. The GCF’s catalytic financing will be provided in a manner that will only support the building of climate resilient infrastructure projects and the overall return objective of the Fund will be achieved through the successful completion of these projects. Any proceeds generated by GCF will be subordinated to the commercial equity tranche – hence providing a partial risk protection to institutional investors. The ICRF will also seek to raise capital from institutional investors such as permanent investment funds (PIFs) and sovereign wealth funds (SWFs) where investors have the necessary long-term resources to scale up climate investments from “billions” to “trillions” at a

African pension funds have been expanding in several countries across the continent, offering a viable option for long-term financing opportunities. PwC (2015) estimates that pension funds' assets-under-management in 12 African markets will rise to about USD 1.1 trillion by 2030, from USD $76 billion in 2017. The largest growth is expected in South Africa (81.7%), Nigeria (76.6%) and Botswana (47.3%). South Africa holds over USD 207 billion in assets under management, with the majority of this being pension funds. Since significant regulatory changes were implemented in 2006, pension funds have managed to accumulate over USD $30 billion in assets under management. However, despite their potential as a major source of long-term financing for infrastructure projects, there is a very limited amount invested in infrastructure. # D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Describe the wider benefits and priorities of the project/program in relation to the Sustainable Development Goals

Climate resilient Infrastructure development sits at the center of all development pathways and is closely linked to economic growth, environmental outcomes, and well-being of populations, as such investing in climate-resilient infrastructure is a key priority for many countries. In particular, it is critical for African participating countries: both climate and development objectives by promoting sustainable investments in resilient infrastructure that can withstand the impacts of extreme weather events and changing climate. ICRF will promote sustainability based on rigorous due diligence, capital discipline, strategic planning, and long-term financial viability. The project/program will be implemented with a view to also safeguarding affordability of the assets. ICRF principally contributes in participating countries by providing concessional financing to support the development of resilient infrastructure, focusing on developing resilience of infrastructure and 9+ calling for upgrading of existing infrastructure.

- **Target 6**: Develop quality, reliable, sustainable and modern energy access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all
- **SDG 8**: Promote sustained, inclusive and sustainable economic growth, full and productive employment and decent work for all
- **SDG 10**: Reduce inequality within and among countries
- **SDG 11**: Make cities and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable
- **SDG 13**: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts
- **SDG 16**: Promote peaceful and inclusive societies for sustainable development, provide access to justice for all and build effective, accountable and inclusive institutions at all levels

By providing concessional financial support to ICRF, the GCF will contribute to Target 9 by investing in: Facilitate sustainable and resilient infrastructure development in developing countries through enhanced financial, technical and institutional capacity building. The project/program is consistent with and provides contribution to SDGs 6, 8, 10, 11, 13, and 16.

## Knowledge sharing: ICRF advocates for the delivery of climate risk databases to be available as an open-source public good in support of the region's sustainable economic and social development as an overarching priority.
