**Page 91**
---

ICRF presents a real potential to contribute to sustainable development with impacts on all dimensions of sustainability as co-benefits to the core adaptation results expected from its implementation: Economic, Environment and Social including consideration for gender equality, health, safety and wellbeing.

**Economic co-benefits:** Proven a central factor in the productivity of firms, the wellbeing of local population, and the overall economic development of countries, infrastructure is essential for the achievement of economic development. ICRF will provide a substantial contribution to the economic development and sustainable prosperity of beneficiary countries by improving access to markets, increasing competitiveness through better transport and accessibility of a country's infrastructure and related services. As such inadequate infrastructure is an obstacle to economic growth, ICRF will contribute to the creation of jobs in the construction sector and other infrastructure, ICRF provides clarity on long-term climate-resilient development strategies to avoid the lock-in effect of investments that are not resilient to future climate change impacts and thus reduce potential losses in the face of climate hazards. The Programme will also promote business development and create jobs by supporting the private sector through investment in multi-use infrastructure projects for the benefit of society. At least 572,364 new direct jobs are expected from the implementation of ICRF in its beneficiary countries with additional 78,085 indirect jobs (conservative estimates).

**Environmental co-benefits:** ICRF aims to generate substantial environmental returns. To achieve this, the Programme will ensure that all projects are designed and implemented to deliver both direct and indirect social and impacts such that they result from the implementation of individual sub-projects. Project level environmental assessments and associated action plans will be developed for each project. The Programme will also develop a set of general principles of action plan with necessary safeguards will create a blueprint for the market wider adoption as positive examples of good practice in infrastructure development. To ensure that ICRF projects deliver their substantial environmental performance, project sponsors will be encouraged to incorporate environmental and social safeguard policies into their own policies and procedures (e.g., World Bank Group’s Environmental and Social Framework) and other national environmental requirements. For example, all sub-projects shall integrate application of environmental best practices in infrastructure construction, improving the sustainability and management of key ecosystem services vital for human well-being such as water supply, flood protection, food production, and ecosystem services preservation, consistent with the ICRF Environmental and Social Framework with specific emphasis on gender equality. The Programme will also ensure that all projects are designed to maximize the investment process with regards to taking environmental considerations into account including through ongoing monitoring of project performance against agreed environmental targets. This will include due diligence, at the onset of the post-investment phase and as part of asset management and monitoring. The programme targets to integrate resilience measures through nature-based solutions in at least three (3) subprojects.

**Social co-benefits:** Infrastructure disruptions frequently cause or exacerbate many types of social impacts per person, including loss of life, injury, displacement, and economic disruption. These impacts can be substantial and disproportionately large; when an infrastructure breakdown can it cause injuries, loss of properties and even result in human fatality. ICRF will increase safety by deploying climate resilient infrastructure that is designed to withstand the effects of extreme weather events and other natural hazards, reducing climate change disasters and prevent losses protecting local populations. ICRF interventions will be prioritized for areas most vulnerable to climate change impacts and where there are high levels of poverty. These projects in turn result in improving people safety and wellbeing and raising the quality of life of the populations of the targeted countries.

**Gender and Social Inclusion:** Promotion of gender equality: ICRF will be implemented in a gender responsive manner. The preparation of an in-depth and comprehensive gender assessment and associated action plan is required for each project. This will ensure that the benefits from infrastructure development and social inclusion returns as co-benefits. ICRF implementation will effectively support reduction of gender inequality through the investment portfolio: ICRF will increase women’s empowerment by increasing women's access to and control over resources, including land, water, finance, technology, information and services; it will customize its gender action plans to the local context of each project and systematically advocate and raise awareness on gender equality issues. The Programme will ensure that all projects are designed to encourage the inclusion of women through all activities and will ensure that women are benefitting in each

ICRF targets [19] African countries of which [11] LDCs under this Programme are all highly vulnerable to climate change and its impacts. The infrastructure needs in these countries are massive given the limited fiscal space. Hence, African countries need to build strong institutions and enabling factors including fiscal incentives and other regulatory incentives.

ICRF responds to the needs of its beneficiaries countries and their population on several fronts: the need to build capacity and improve access to climate information; the need for updating existing codes and practices incorporating state-of-the-art scientific knowledge; the need to mainstream climate change concerns in planning and approval processes for infrastructure development.

D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Please describe how the beneficiary country takes ownership of and implements the funded project/program.

Country ownership is pivotal to the ICRF design and implementation. Considering the innovative approach underpinned by its focus on national ownership, engagement with a wide range of stakeholders is central to the sustainability and success plan of ICRF and in line with AFC investment strategy. Over the 10 years of the ICRF, each country will be responsible for developing a National Climate Resilience Strategy (NCRS). AFC due diligence process always provides due consideration to engagement at national level as illustrated by the example of Kenya and Ghana. The NCRS is a key document that sets out the country’s vision and commitment to commit the design and implementation of ICRF follows an open, inclusive, and comprehensive process. This approach ensures that all stakeholders are engaged in the development of the NCRS and acknowledge the ICRF approach as a first of its kind when it comes to ensure regular consultations and engagement with NDAs and providing them with the opportunity to lead the engagement with key national stakeholders. The NCRS will be used to guide the design and implementation of the ICRF pipeline.

ICRF aligns to pan-African, regional, and national priorities. It is consistent with the Africa 2063 vision, the AfCSP Union Program of Infrastructure Development (UPID) and is consistent with Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) thematic areas, themes and priorities identified by the participating countries. The ICRF will be implemented in a manner that ensures alignment between the NCRS, project design and technical assistance to make African countries’ infrastructure master plan resilient to climate change. Most African countries have developed or are developing their NDCs and NDC implementation plans and documents Two illustrative examples from Kenya and Ghana. Kenya plans to mobilize funds from local (10%) and external (90%) sources. The project will be implemented in two phases: risk analysis and vulnerability assessment, design of building materials to enhance the resilience of roads to climate change Ghana has developed a climate resilient infrastructure roadmap, well aligned with the ICRF programme, in recognition of the need for a national approach by a country to address the country’s climate risks and development prospect.

AFC engaged collaboratively with all participating countries during the organization and development phase of this funding proposal. AFC engaged with NDA and wider stakeholders of the target countries to enable them to understand the structure of the ICRF programme, the choice of the financial instruments, confirm their alignment with the national priorities and ensure that they are in a position to engage with the project and programmes. The 19 countries that have delivered their non-objections letters (NOLs) have fully followed this process. This has resulted in the development of a comprehensive NCRS for each country. These were established and documented their respective NCL issuance procedures, and the delivery of the NCL is the achievement of a constructive engagement and collaboration with the countries, setting a foundation for effective implementation of ICRF projects. The NCRS will be used to guide the design and implementation efforts to embed climate resilient at the core of infrastructure financing. AFC’s infrastructure projects are largely implemented in partnership with governments and other stakeholders. This is consistent with AFC’s approach to work with governments across the project life cycle. In the context of the ICRF, AFC will work with relevant national government counterparts to ensure that the project is aligned with climate resilience into the Public-Private Partnership (PPP) project framework (as described in section B3.2).

The ICRF has developed a comprehensive country engagement strategy that goes well beyond the issuing of non-objections letters and NCLs. The key element of this strategy is to ensure that the ICRF design process as part of the PPP implementation is that in targeting a wide spectrum of stakeholders in each country from both public and private sector. More than 500 institutions have been targeted and received the project summary document (PSD) of the ICRF. The PSD has been used to engage with these institutions on the NCA resulting from the stakeholder engagement (please see stakeholder consultation report as part of the PPP implementation).

D.4. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 2000 words, approximately 1 page)

The CIFG program will promote climate resilient infrastructure investments in targeted African countries through adopting de-risking mechanisms and integration of data-driven climate risk analysis into investment decisions. The program will support the development of a comprehensive framework for building institutional and market capacity, regulatory framework through TA for CRI planning and implementation. CIFG will provide technical assistance (TA) to governments and private sector entities on climate and other public capital, regulatory standards, policies and tools for building climate resilient infrastructure.

* **Transitional requirements** for CRI through standards and policies, TA for public and private sector and innovative climate insurance products for CRI will be supported by the CIFG grant and create a paradigm shift in the way infrastructure is financed. The program implementation RIR below market rates, and as a result cannot attract commercial investors to fund the projects. This makes it necessary to use concessional funding from CIFG. The CIFG concessionary funding comes with an upfront incremental cost that will be covered by the project itself. The CIFG concessionary costs for de-risking CRI investments, which makes the projects climate-resilient and bankable for medium- and long-term financing, are expected to be lower than the market rate. This is due to best practices and policies to maximize sub-project results, increase efficiency and financial sustainability. This will serve as a proof of concept that can easily be replicated and upscaling by the private sector.

* **Incremental costs** for CRI will be covered by CIFG concessional equity and will deliver the following intended benefits:

    * higher quality of the desired infrastructure result (less costly operation and maintenance (O&M), less revenue losses, lower risk of stranded assets)
    * better sustainability (assets longevity and avoidance of stranded assets risk),
    * additional economic benefits (increased revenue for vulnerable people and communities,
    * increased productivity).

By incorporating upfront incremental cost, the sub-projects will avoid financial losses from infrastructure disruptions causing revenues losses, higher O&M, and additional capital expenditures in the future as a result of climate change.

* **Mobilization** at the sub-project level by CIFG: ICFP equity and mezzanine financing will unlock financing at scale targeting ~12.6 – 13 leverage ratio at the sub-project level, equivalent to US$ 1.55 - $480 million per project. The total mobilization cost for each sub-project is estimated to be equal to an average mobilization ratio at the sub-project level by CIFG of S$7 (US$1.155 + S$3 489) or US$2.6 million per project. This will result in a total mobilization cost of $2.6 million per project, resulting in a leverage ratio = ~5.2x.

* **Total mobilization ratio at the ICFP fund and sub-project levels is envisaged as >7.x to be achieved by catalytic junior equity by the CIFG; a very strong mobilization ratio for a private sector adaptation programme: US$ 130 million on the Fund level + 1.32 billion on the sub-project level) / US$ 25 million on the Fund level = >7.x.

ICF’s use of proceeds will ensure maximum cost efficiency based on the practices and policies of the executing entities. All contracts will be awarded in competitive bidding procedures covered by AFC procurement rules. The CIFG equity share will remain consistent with market prices, independent of the cost estimates provided in this funding proposal.

E.T. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, max. 1 page)

ICRF programme-level monitoring, reporting, and evaluation will be conducted by ACP in compliance with the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework, the ICRF Programme Implementation Plan (PIP) and the GCF Annual Workplan and Evaluation (AWE). The ICRF Program Manager will be appointed by ACP, who will be in charge of programme implementation, data monitoring, reporting, analysis, and evaluation. ACP will develop and quarterly work plans to ensure the efficient and optimal implementation of the programme, working in coordination with ACP Senior Management Team.

In case of encountering any delays or non-systematic events, ICRF Program Manager will inform ACP Senior Management Team, programme countries (DACs), GCF Secretariat and other relevant stakeholders. The ICRF Program Manager will be responsible for ensuring that the programme is implemented in accordance with the PIP and the GCF Annual Workplan and Evaluation. ACP will be to implement M&E Plan through coordination of stakeholders to ensure that appropriate support, interventions, and corrective measures can be undertaken as required.

Throughout the programme monitoring, reporting and evaluation of the programme’s results.
ICRF Program Manager will provide regular updates on M&E plan implementation and annual reports. ACF have internal regulations and policies including those based on EMSP, Gender Action Plan for ACP for programme implementation and compliance. ACP and ICRF Program Manager will coordinate with the GCF Secretariat to ensure that the ICRF Programme is in line with the GCF’s M&E framework.

The ICRF Program Manager will coordinate with the DACs and other relevant stakeholders on M&E of the ICRF programme level.
The methodologies for M&E may vary depending on the outcomes. The methodologies are more submission to GCF for each year of the programme lifespan: five years for Outcomes 2 and 3 and twenty years for Outcome 1. The methodologies of M&E will be standardized according to the best practices in ACP and ICRF Programme level. The ICRF Program Manager, ACP Senior Management Team, and the DACs, as appropriate, will provide objective input to the annual APR report for GCF. The ICRF Program Manager will ensure compliance of the APR with the M&E plan in the Annex 11 and meeting the reporting deadlines.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

| Selected Risk Factor | Failure to deliver on the ICRF impact potential |
| --- | --- |
| Category | Technical and operational |
| Probability | Low |
| Impact | Medium |

Description:
Due to climate change uncertainties, there is a risk of lower results associated with ICRF impact potential, e.g., providing access to resilient infrastructure and protect the most vulnerable groups, lower technical and financial viability of the assets, and delays in project implementation that might delay repayment and decrease investors' confidence.

Mitigation Measure(s):
- Robust ICRF Climate Assessment Methodology, technical and climate change risk due diligence will ensure through projects development to ensure socioeconomic and financial success of ICRF investment.
- A comprehensive ICRF impact assessment will provide a holistic approach to risks and opportunities, allowing to ensure targeted impact.
- Ongoing M&E of the projects will focus on identifying red flags and providing remedial solutions early.

Selected Risk Factor | Limited access to financing at the fund and project levels
| --- | --- |
| Category | Technical and operational |
| Probability | Medium |
| Impact | Medium |

Description:
Delays in raising capital at the fund and project level and not meeting Fund commitments due to complex negotiations or low appetite from the private sector.

Mitigation Measure(s):
- Concessional funding from GCF and parametric insurance scheme will unlock private capital for ICRF projects.
- ICRF financing of the full project cost (including costs associated with operations) allows to optimize transactional cost and negotiation process.
- Unconditional access to ICRF financing and commitments from a confidential list of investors even at the early stage.
- ICFM with solid track record in infrastructure financing provides access to even greater network of investors on a regional and sectoral levels.
- Liquidity risks will be mitigated by five years cash flow planning and ongoing liquidity test.

Selected Risk Factor | Risk of misalignment
| Category | Technical and operational |
| Probability | Low |
| Impact | Medium |

The ICIF is a junior infrastructure equity fund which will invest in climate resilient infrastructure projects. These projects are subject to various project risks which may result in capital losses for investors in the fund.

GCF is taking a high risk by providing a junior equity position in the ICIF fund to catalyze the participation of other investors and to leverage the scale and expertise of AFC. The capital commitment of GCF has a high-risk profile, therefore, it may not be returned in full in case of unforeseen circumstances.

Mitigation Measure(s)
All parties have an established track record as a pre-eminent infrastructure solutions provider in Africa with unrivalled experience in identifying, developing, executing, and delivering transformational infrastructure projects. AFC has delivered over 100 projects worth $25 billion in the last decade, including 3000 MW of power infrastructure in over 35 African countries. ACP, the Asset Management subsidiary of AFC, will co-invest ICRF funds alongside AFC. ACP will leverage the scale and breadth of AFC's investment track record and proven ability to deliver projects on time and within budget. ACP has a strong credit rating (Aa2) assigned by Moody’s and has been profitable since inception 15 years ago. This reflects the robustness of its investment process, which is based on rigorous due diligence and risk management practices. ACP will co-invest with AFC in eligible projects, therefore, will apply the same rigor in its investment process to mitigate potential risks.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environment and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Executive Entity has developed a comprehensive Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) for the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund. The ESMF has been designed in compliance with the IFC Performance Standards and the GCF Policies and Standards. In addition to this, the Executive Entity and Accredited Entity have prepared Resettlement Policy Framework and Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework, and Biodiversity Management Framework in line with GCF ESF requirements, as well as other relevant policies and frameworks to match requirements for GCF Category A programmes. Please refer to Annex 6 for details.

The ESMF is presented under Annex 6 of this Funding Proposal developed to ensure that the projects to be financed by the ICRF are designed, implemented and monitored in a sustainable manner so that it ensures that GCF ESF policies are passed down from Accredited Entity (AE) to Fund (Fund level) and then to the project level. The ESMF is also aligned with the IFC Performance Standards, GCF Policies and specific frameworks to address topics: Resettlement, Indigenous Peoples Rights, Biodiversity. Stakeholders consultation, gender and social inclusion.

The main objective ICRF ESF is to ensure internal environmental and social governance risks prior to investment decisions are identified and institutionalized the monitoring mechanisms at an investee level to supervise ESG performance during the term of the ICRF’s investment. As per ESF documentation, the following steps will be taken to identify, assess, manage, monitor and mitigate:

### ESG Integration in Investment Process

1. **Screening Stage:** The project in the ICRF pipeline will be screened for “eligibility” to the AE and GCF ESF Policy.
2. **Conduct Environmental and Social due diligence of projects that passed screening stage:**
   - The Executing Entity’s team shall assess potential E&S risks of proposed project prior to the ICRF’s investment.
3. **Incorporating investor company’s obligation to comply with AE and GCF Environmental and Social policies and policies:**
   - The ESAP developed during the due-diligence stage will be part of shareholder agreement between ICRF and the respective investee company, therefore the investee will be legally required to implement ESAP.

The ICRF programme will invest in category 11, 12 and 13 projects. Therefore, the stakeholder engagement plan (SEP) Framework in Annex 6 also reflects an importance of fully and effectively engaging with Indigenous peoples affected in the design, development and implementation of the strategies and activities

To be financed by the programme, while respecting their rights. The Executing Entity will disclose on its website and provide EAS information for each sub-project under this programme in accordance with the Disclosure Policy.

In case, where projects to be financed under the programme will involve land acquisition, resettlement, we will apply the Resettlement Policy Framework which has been developed as per GCF Resettlement Policy Requirement and is included in Annex 6. A Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) will be developed for each project, to ensure transparency and meaningful participation of affected communities and interested parties. Stakeholder engagement and consultations will be conducted throughout the project cycle. This will include discussions of project design and implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and lessons learned. The SEP, along with other EAS instruments, will be subject to public consultation and disclosure requirements as per GCF policies. The SEP will be treated as a living document to be regularly updated as needed during project implementation.

### SEAH Considerations

In accordance with the IGF Board Decision B-2M-2021/18 adopted in September 2021, the IGF ESMS incorporates the risk assessment and management on sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment (SEAH) as part of the EAS due diligence for the proposed programme and sub-programme activities.

#### PS 1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts

As part of the social risks, issues of sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA) and gender-based violence (GBV) may arise within projects. The IGF ESMS will include a Gender-Based Violence Action Plan to assess, manage, and monitor the risks of gender-based violence (GBV) and sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment in ensuring labour and working conditions effectiveness. This will also be applied to the risks related to the sexual abuses, sexual exploitation, and sexual harassment issues in ensuring labour and working conditions effectiveness. The IGF ESMS will include a Gender-Based Violence Action Plan (GBVAP).

#### PS 2: Labour and Working Conditions

The PS 2 seeks to protect the fundamental human rights of workers. It is applicable to all direct project workers, project contract and subcontractor workers as well as project supply-chain workers. The compliance requirement will include provision of clear information and documentation on working conditions, including wages, hours, and other terms and conditions.

The PS 2 in the contexts of IGF will also seek to strengthen the Grievances Redress Mechanism (GRM) to address any complaints or concerns related to labour and working conditions. This will be applied in ensuring labour and working conditions effectiveness. Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse (SA), sexual harassment (SH), including the exploitation of children are included in the list of "Designated Categories of Offences".

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Summary of the gender assessment and project/program-level gender action plan that is aligned with the objectives of GFIP's Gender Policy

The Infrastructure Climate Fund (ICF) will mainstream gender considerations in its investment projects to ensure women’s economic participation in the target sectors and create income-generating opportunities for women in project countries. Proposed interventions under the Gender Action Plan (GAP) include: (i) increasing women’s access to climate change mitigation and adaptation actions, and (ii) minimizing gender-related risks in climate change actions.

Empowerment of women is a key component of ICF projects. The ICF will ensure that women are included as beneficiaries in all project activities, including the design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and digital infrastructure. Under the ICRF approach, issues related to female employment and access to climate resilient infrastructure services will be effectively embedded across the implementation of sub-projects.

The project team will develop a gender action plan (GAP) that includes specific strategies and methodologies for continuous project monitoring and impact assessments, collect and present sex-disaggregated data, and ensure that women’s voices are heard. The GAP will also include an awareness raising campaign to promote women’s access to climate resilient infrastructure services.

The initial program level gender assessment outlined several issues for women outlined below:

These issues will be addressed through the following strategies: improving access to education, building capacities and generating knowledge on the program's differentiated impact on men and women. The ICF undertakes an initial gender assessment of the ICRF programme and elaborated a gender action plan.

#### i. Female employment and skill gaps in the target sectors

The main barriers to women’s participation in the target sector include social norms that confine women to unpaid household and care work, occupational segregation, lack of access to education and training opportunities, and limited access to credit and financial environments. This gap will be addressed through skills building and, outreaches as well as the promotion of women-run businesses.

#### ii. Female employment in the areas of implementation of target projects

Women, if employed, are often paid less than men for similar work. The ICF will ensure that, where possible, the socio-economic needs of the local communities will be assessed and actions empowering women prioritized. This includes ensuring that women’s voices are heard during project design and implementation.

#### iii. Gender-based violence

Women routinely experience sexual harassment and intimate partner violence in target countries, and the risk of gender-based violence may increase during the realization of infrastructure projects. The ICF will ensure that all project activities include measures to prevent and response approaches and the adoption of internal prevention and response mechanisms in compliance with the GFIP’s Gender Policy. This includes ensuring that women are included as beneficiaries empowering local women and men and aim to create a safe environment at the project site and in communities.

#### iv. Women access to climate resilience infrastructure services

Among project countries with lower electrification rates and under-developed grids, women suffer from time-poorly


---
**Page 136**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL v3.0 | PAGE 132

## Increasing issues, as they bear the responsibility of food preparation, as well as fuel and water collection. Climate hazards disrupt and damage transmission lines limiting access to electricity among poor households, including female-headed households (FHH). Improving access to climate resilient energy sources can help FHHs reduce their vulnerability to climate change impacts by increasing their income, improving access to education, and increasing female labor participation and incomes. Similarly, climate stressors such as flooding, sea-level rise, and droughts can disrupt transportation links such as ports, roads, and ports, leading severe economic losses that may significantly impact FHHs.

For these reasons, the IE will (i) pay attention to women’s and men’s differentiated needs in the design of climate change adaptation projects; (ii) ensure that gender equality is considered when designing women's voice is heard and included in decision-making processes; (iii) undertake outreach and capacity-building initiatives to increase women’s knowledge about climate resilience in target sectors, and (iv) ensure that women are involved as stakeholders in project-level activities.

The ICRF will follow a systematic approach and provide cross-support to projects interventions with a focus on data collection, policy engagement, project level actions and capacity-building activities. At the national level, the following implementation approach will be followed:

### 1. Data Collection and Initial Assessments
- Build upon existing country gender diagnostics and action plans;
- Document lessons learned from previous ICRF projects in the selection of sex-disaggregated data;
- Conduct stakeholders’ consultations involving female participants to understand men and women differentiated needs, and facilitate an understanding of the differentiated impact of the ICRF project on men and women in initial assessments through the involvement of gender and sector experts (Environmental and Social Impact Assessment, Country Gender Action Plans etc.).

### 2. Identifying and Implementing Relevant Actions
- Identify and implement relevant interventions to narrow gaps in terms of employment and climate change adaptation;
- Prioritize employment including capacity building and programs for women to gain hands-on experience in the target sectors;
- Implement preventive measures to mitigate gender violence or sexual harassment in the implementation of sub-projects.

### 3. M&E and Knowledge Management
- Monitor results throughout the project cycle to assess impact on men and women and allow for real-time correction during implementation.
- Expand the knowledge base by documenting lessons learned from prior projects, knowledge production, and dissemination.

### 4. Strengthening Female Participation and Voice During Stakeholder Consultations
- Organize stakeholders’ consultations throughout the project cycle. Ensure effective participation of women in all stages of the project cycle including planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation.
- Use local collectives and involve female facilitators/local women leaders, as well as women representatives from the utility company whenever possible;
- Organize gender-sensitive and inclusive stakeholder consultations that recognize consultations and focus groups at a convenient time for women; and create partnerships with women’s groups and non-governmental organizations to ensure women are involved in all aspects of the project sustainability.

The full gender assessment and project-level gender action plan is provided in annex B.

G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately (1 page))

*Due Diligence: ACF will be responsible for establishing KYC due diligence processes including anti-money laundering and other evaluations of sponsors according to each respective institution's internal policy requirements at ICRF. ACF will be responsible for the financial management of all funds of the ICFR through its own internal policies, procedures, and controls.*

*Distribution: The distribution of funds will be made by ACF in accordance with the terms of its investments team and the implementation of the financial operations team.*

*Distributing Agent: The Distribution Agent shall have the discretion to determine when to cut out the financing agreements. These will be subject to the fulfillment of all conditions precedent as agreed by the ACF and the Borrower.*

*Supervision and Portfolio Management: ACF will ordain a dedicated portfolio management team for each investment, which will be responsible for monitoring and reporting on the performance of its investments. There are a set of quarterly, semiannual, and annual reporting requirements, part of which also require that the Borrower provide certain information to ACF. The Borrower must ensure it is compliant with all the monitoring and reporting requests as stipulated in the AMA and be agreed in the Funding Agreement: ACF will ensure that its reporting requirements to ACF are fully met.*

*Procurement: All procurement of goods, services, and works for the Corporation's individual business units and services for general use, i.e., the procurement of specialized services that support investment operations and projects, shall be conducted by ACF. This includes the selection of suppliers, contractors, consultants, and conduct of due diligence on all suppliers, contractors, consultants, and services for general use. In the procurement of specialized services that support investment operations and projects, ACF will ensure that the Borrower complies with the applicable laws and regulations of the country in which it is located.*

*Product or Service: relative to the Corporation's individual business requirements. Potential vendors must comply with the following standards: Anti-Bribery/Anti-Corruption and Anti-Money Laundering. Thereafter, ACF will undertake a systematic pre-qualification to assess the suitability of all potential suppliers, contractors, consultants, and services for general use.*

*Audit: The framework will be subject to ACF's and ACF's audit policies and will be audited by external independent auditors as required under the terms of the ICFR. This includes the submission of disbursements of funds for each investment as well as repayments following best international standards.*

### G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.

With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, will not be disclosed to the GCF and that it will be subject to a confidentiality policy.*

Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

* full text of the funding proposal of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidential treatment under the GCF's confidentiality policy; and
* redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by the Africa Finance Corporation regarding the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF)

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the programme titled the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF) in Cameroon as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Africa Finance Corporation to us on 12 October 2021.

The undersigned is the only authorized representative of Mr. Paul Tchawa, the National Designated Authority of Cameroon.

Pursuant to GCF decision 8.09/01, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our objection to certain programmes as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our objection, it is implied that:

(ii) In view of the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in contravention of the relevant laws and regulations.

We also state that our national process for ascertaining non-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed. We also state that this objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made public on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

Pr Paul Tchawa

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP205

## Proposal name: Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF)

### Accredited entity: Africa Finance Corporation (AFC)

### Project/Programme size: Large

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1. Project description
The funding proposal is for an adaptation programme that seeks to support the least-FAV [sic] African countries, as identified in the World Bank's re-projection letter (N02), by providing resilient priority infrastructure assets and services: Benin, Cameroon, Chad, Cote d'Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Gambia, Ghana, Guinea, Kenya, Mali, Mauritania, Namibia, Nigeria, Rwanda, Sierra Leone, Togo and Zambia. The project will establish a new fund called the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF), which will invest in what are referred to as "climate-resilient energy systems" (e.g., Solar Power Plants, Wind Farms, Bridges, Railways, Airports) [40-50 per cent of the portfolio]. "Climate-resilient Energy Systems" include "climate-resilient infrastructure assets and services" (e.g., Water Supply, Waste Management, Telecommunication and Digital Infrastructure") [5–10 per cent]. The funding request from GCF is US$ 2.4 billion, with a total investment of US$ 3.6 billion. Of this amount, US$ 1.8 billion will be invested as equity junior to ICRF, and US$ 1.49 million will be used as grant finance split between the World Bank and GCF for project preparatory assistance, monitoring and evaluation, and project management costs.

#### 2. The ICRF programme will be dedicated to the development of investment in new climate-resilient energy systems and climate-resilient infrastructure assets. At least 50 per cent will be dedicated to new investments in CBI assets (including greenfield, expansion and growth projects). The remaining 50 per cent will be invested in existing CBI assets, upgrading infrastructure assets to be made climate resilient through climate adaptation solutions. As a joint equity position, the GCF equity de-risk ICRF by taking on as much as the first 30 per cent of any loss incurred from the project portfolio. The target beneficiaries are the most vulnerable (80 per cent of the total population, of which 50 per cent are women), and 144 LLS countries will be eligible for funding under ICRF. Of these, 27 countries have at least one female minister in government (of whom 36 per cent are women) of the selected NCD countries. Subprojects will be implemented over a period of 10 years using a total investment of US$ 3.6 billion. The GCF equity de-risking at the fund level will no longer be required. ICRF will be managed by Africa Finance Corporation (AFC), with support from the World Bank and GCF. The AFC team will be responsible for deal origination, due diligence and execution, and operational management and exit of ICRF portfolio companies.

# Context of climate vulnerability: historical climate

Based on the feasibility study, climatologically, more precipitation has been recorded within the latitude band of 20° S and 20° N for 1979–2013 than in any other period since 1854. The RCM ensemble from the Regional Climate Model archive and an ensemble of three regional climate models (RCMs) from the Africa domain of the Consortium for Research on the Distribution of Climate (CORDEX) are used to assess historical climate change. The RCM ensemble shows a good pattern correlation of 0.85, although with a root mean square error of 12 mm/day. The RCMs show that precipitation has increased over most land areas, especially around the Sahara desert region. Although modelled minimum and maximum temperature have a root mean square error of 1.43 °C and 2 °C, respectively, there is good agreement between the models in terms of trends. The ensemble shows an increase in precipitation trend observed and modelled mean surface temperatures. Unlike precipitation, which exhibits a positive correlation with all RCMs, temperature has a negative correlation with some RCMs and model, where trends could be positive or negative depending on the subregion. Decreasing trends in precipitation are observed over parts of West Africa (WAF), Central Africa – South (CAF-S), Horn of Africa (HORN), and East Africa (EAF). The decreasing trend is more pronounced for temperature.

# Climate change projections: Model projected precipitation and temperature

According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change Sixth Assessment Report (AR6), common regional characteristics exist in Africa. Some changes are that the mean temperature has increased by 0.5 °C since 1950, with a rate of increase of 0.23 °C per decade from 1850–1950. Equally, the rate of surface temperature increase has been more rapid on the continent than in other regions globally. The observed warming is attributed to both natural and human-induced climate drivers, such as anthropogenic emissions and land-surface modification. Furthermore, sea level around Africa has increased at a higher rate than global mean sea level due to the influence of local factors, such as changes in ocean circulation patterns, contributing to increases in the frequency and severity of coastal flooding in low-lying areas, and to coastal erosion. The projected increase in temperature is expected to be more pronounced over critical infrastructures situated around the coastline.

| Region | Projected change in mean annual temperature (°C) |
| --- | --- |
| Africa | 1.5–4.0 |
| Sub-Saharan Africa | 2.3–4.8 |
| North Africa | 1.6–3.9 |

### Goal statement/impact: paradigm shift and Theory of Change
The TCF is built on the premise that a blend of blended finance, technical assistance, and capacity building in CRII and climate risk assessments and regulatory frameworks for CRI are strengthened and complemented by an innovative insurance mechanism to support a resilient infrastructure pathway. BECAUSE vital infrastructure will be more resilient to climate hazards, vulnerable people and communities will have better access to critical services such as water for livelihoods and other social services.

### ICF design structure encompasses three outcomes:
- **Outcome 1:** Blended finance is mobilized at scale to accelerate CRII investments in the beneficiary countries. This component will help to increase the resilience of infrastructure assets to withstand climate hazards and for improved access of vulnerable people and communities to critical services such as water, sanitation, health care, education, food security, power supply, ports and logistics, special economic zones, telecommunications, and digital infrastructure. The blended finance component will also overcome institutional barriers on limited capital flows to infrastructure investments in Africa due to investors’ perception of high risk (i.e., risk allocation and financial instruments mismatch, low return on investment, lack of market liquidity, and the perception that there is limited insurance from climate risk insurance, low appetite from the private sector).
- **Outcome 2:** Technical assistance is mobilized at scale to support the CRII. Improved capacity for scaling up CRI in Africa for infrastructure programme assets made resilient and able to withstand climate hazards and for improved access of vulnerable people and communities to critical services such as water, sanitation, health care, education, food security, power supply, ports and logistics, special economic zones, telecommunications, and digital infrastructure.
- **Outcome 3:** A strengthened regulatory framework and innovative climate risk parametric insurance (CRII) is mainstreamed for the long-term viability of CRI investments in Africa. The technical assistance component will help to scale up the programme’s paradigm shift effect in the programme countries and the region. This component will also support the development of a robust regulatory framework, with favourable investment policies and fiscal incentives on country and regional levels based on the lessons learned from the pilot phase. The implementation of this work is a crucial milestone for attracting investments from the private sector in resilient infrastructure in the programme countries. A feasibility study on parametric insurance will be conducted.

### Adaptation impact
- **Triple resilience dividends:** As per the funding proposal, ICF, if established and able to invest according to its mandate, will aim to deliver robust critical infrastructures in the identified sectors of transportation, energy, economies and telecommunications; infrastructure


---
**Page 164**
---

projects will deliver triple resilience dividends for the relevant country. The first dividend is the avoidance of loss of life from disasters, minimization of disruptions, and promotion of effective recovery. The second dividend is the unlocking of economic potential by investing in resilient infrastructure. The third dividend is the creation of a more cohesive society through interlinked communities, ecosystems, infrastructures and investments along the value business chain.

In for private sector stakeholders, ICFR, if established and managed effectively, could lead to the proper mainstreaming of climate adaptation in private sectors' investment; an important aspect of this will be the development of dedicated climate assets. The implementation of climate measures in the development of infrastructure projects will lead to the operationalization of a new financial market linking the delivery of climate finance for adaptation with the creation of new climate class I/II assets. This will enable investors to lead to an increased awareness in the private sector of how the physical impacts of climate change will affect private investments, revenues, costs, operations, assets and liabilities. Valuation leading to the development of dedicated climate assets (Type 1) and development of Type 2 assets, will enable the private sector to diversify its investments in dedicated ICFR class assets; investment in infrastructure projects that are climate sensitive and where climate-related risks are managed through the implementation of climate measures will be encouraged. This will therefore cover any residual risk.

From the perspective of the financial or capital market sector, ICFR will lead to the creation of new climate class I/II assets. If ICFR is successful in delivering the establishment and operation of a true climate-resilient asset class (climate proofing [Type 1] and development projects that are climate sensitive and where climate-related risks are managed through the implementation of climate measures [Type 2]), then it will be possible that the experience of the ICFR will enable the following:

* The emergence of a new financial mechanism linking the delivery of climate finance for adaptation with the creation of new climate class I/II assets. This will lead to true climate-resilient investments (projects that only exist because of the need to address a climate vulnerability).

Reforms on fiscal incentives for investment in climate-resilient infrastructures by beneficiary governments; these could be in the form of tax holidays, faster regulatory clearance, subsidies (government, agency), climate-focused infrastructure mobilization initiatives;

Lessons learned from operationalizing climate measures to manage climate-related risks will be shared with private sector investors, financial institutions and corporations in compliance with the mandatory recommendations under the Task Force on Climate Financial Disclosure.

### 1.2 Risks to adaptation

12. Weak baseline assessments: While this funding proposal was first submitted for consideration at the time of the meeting of the Board (38.1), the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) raised the following issues in relation to the proposal's weak country and sectoral baseline assessment:

* The TAP had recommended an in-depth assessment of the four (4) identified target sectors (i.e. Transport and Logistics [Ports, Roads, Bridges, Railways; Airports]; Energy Systems, Economic Development and Finance [Electricity, Natural Gas, Oil, Water, Fossil Fuels]; Agriculture and its ancestors (e.g. annex 2b) had not properly assessed the target countries' domestic and regional markets on finance, capital markets, insurance and climate finance markets. Moreover, while it was acknowledged that both natural disasters and man-made events and extreme events had impacted each country included in the proposal and its key sectoral infrastructures, no evidence based reports were presented showing the extent of losses and


---
**Page 165**
---

damages in terms of monetary cost, lives affected, property, infrastructure, businesses, livelihoods, climate-related budget expenditures, or as a percentage of gross domestic product, or even from a portfolio management perspective of infrastructure assets held by the major financial institutions. In addition to this lack of information on the economic impacts, it had also not provided a clear context on vulnerability covering each country’s vulnerable people and communities. While the reports did provide some useful information about climate change, it did not show what their several sectoral barriers, issues and challenges were, nor how the proposed climate-resilient solutions would address them.

(i) Despite the abundance of reports of assessments of infrastructure assets were lacking. Various annexes and sections mentioned the impact of climate hazards on infrastructure assets, but they did not provide a clear context or analysis of the vulnerability of these assets. The country climate analysis, however, failed to establish at which climate thresholds these infrastructures begin to be adversely impacted. For example, in one of its examples, the report for Benin states that “the coastal areas and low-lying islands were particularly vulnerable due to increasing temperatures, heatwaves, droughts, excessive or reduced rainfall, and sea-level rise.” However, no analysis has been performed to show how climate parameters had impacted economic zones.*

(ii) The answers to the questions in the proposal (e.g., annex 2a: an assessment of the selected case studies on climate-related infrastructure assets in 19 KFIP NIS countries) gave brief overviews per country of the supposed losses and damages due to climate change. However, no analysis was provided as to how these figures corresponded to actual losses and damages were provided or cited. For example, for Benin, the report states that “the total economic loss from climate change impacts on infrastructure sector are estimated at 130,000,000 FCFA (279,054 US dollars). Among which, 80% of these losses are attributed to coastal zones (i.e., sea-sweeper).” The most affected departments were Borgou, Atacora and Donga.*

(iii) As to the baseline information providing the context on the vulnerability of the people, economy and infrastructure in each country, as well as the economic impact of the economy and people of the countries in question, similarly, little information or assessment was provided. For example, the 2015 World Bank report “Assessing the impact of the country’s key infrastructure” report was also quite limited. On Gabon, for example, the assessment on climate in was given just in two sentences (i.e., “However, with the exception of coastal areas and low-lying islands, Gabon is not as vulnerable as cities on the lower coast such as Port-Gentil or Mayumba, is indispensable.” The effects of sea-level rise are expected to be more significant for these coastal zones, impacting the economy of the country and more particularly the populations of this region.)

14. With the revolution of the funding proposal for B35, the All has made good efforts to address these issues. The wealth of information generated for the 19 countries has been provided in annexes 2a (country climate analysis), 2b (climate change impacts on infrastructure) and 2c (adaptation solutions). Annex 2a provides a summary table showing how current and the future climate change will seriously exacerbate negative impacts on these countries’ key infrastructure assets (e.g., roads, bridges, ports, airports, etc. infrastructure). The ITP observes that annex 2aa now has information on observed and anticipated changes in climate variables for each country. Annex 2b provides a summary table for each country on “Climate change impacts on infrastructure type” and “Adaptation solutions” for each combination of major climate change hazard and infrastructure type. The quality of these sections varies between countries, but taken altogether, they provide a strong sense of the


---
**Page 166**
---

potential for investment in specific technical solutions as part of building or retrofitting infrastructure to be durable and resilient to these hazards. Annex Zb summarizes the same information for each country. Annex 25 “Africa Infrastructure Climate Risk Mapping,” is an impressive tool that provides a comprehensive overview of different types of assets—airports; roads; seaports; wireless infrastructure, including radio masts and towers; wind power plants; hydropower plants; transmission lines; bridges; dams; and economic zones—and including 23 investment projects currently in AFCEP’s pipeline. The report provides a summary table for each country by asset type and includes maps of the assets’ locations, wherever data is available. It also supplies summary information and maps per country on exposure to the main relevant climate-change-intensified hazards.

16. In addition to the infrastructure climate risk gap, there was also an initial phase of a wide baseline analysis of the climate-related finance gap on infrastructure. As shown in general, the assessment used the depth of the existing financial commitment as a proxy for what is needed normally and/or as a fraction of the total cost of infrastructure, to help build climate-resilient infrastructure. The climate-finance gap, even from the viewpoint of an infrastructure asset requiring adaptation, was estimated at 10–25% of the total cost of the infrastructure with national contribution (NIC), identified its financing gap for mitigation, but the adaptation financing gap was not defined. This is because there were no reliable estimates available to define the climate-finance gap for adaptation. The funding proposal write-up had defined the incremental cost of climate-resilient infrastructure as 10–25% of total costs needed to make infrastructure climate resilient based on the World Bank’s estimate.

Making infrastructure assets climate-resilient requires incremental cost in the range of three (3) to seven (7) percent of the total cost of infrastructure, depending on its location and chosen resilience interventions. This represents a significant increase compared with the current level of investment, which has a small impact and depending on its asset, its location, and chosen resilient interventions. This represents a significant increase compared with the current level of investment, which has a small impact and depending on its asset, its location, and chosen resilient interventions.

18. The incremental cost range is based on the World Bank’s analysis and works undertaken previously by the Global Commission on the New Economy and Climate regarding the costs associated with climate-resilient infrastructure construction with low-emission development pathways below 10 per cent of total investment cost.

Outcome 2: A natural indirect effect more resilient due to access to safe and climate-proof infrastructure assets in 17 African States with 10 LGCs, which are the most affected by the climate change impacts. The reference to people was also missing in other parts of the project design continued in the proposal.

A case in point was the ICFR’s climate resilient impact assessment methodology (CRIAM). In a nutshell, CRIAM was ICFR's guide on how to screen and identify climate risks, vulnerabilities, and adaptation opportunities. It included an asset-based vulnerability identification measures based on these processes. As presented in figure 1 below, there was a “climate risk screening” process that identified the following:

The figure shows a flowchart with two main parts. The first part, "Part 1: Engaging communities and identifying climate risks," includes steps such as "Identify the asset base," "Screen for climate risks," and "Identify adaptation opportunities." The second part, "Part 2: Assessing climate risks and impacts," includes steps like "Assess climate risk," "Identify adaptation measures," and "Implement adaptation measures."

The flowchart highlights that under part 1, the climate risk impact assessments focused on the asset for subtopics 3a, 3b, and 3c. Then under subtopic 3d, the focus was on the ecosystem services. The figure also notes that beneficiaries (value chains, other assets) appeared if terms like "asset" and "asset ecosystems" were used in the context of vulnerability identification.

The proposal's single focus was on infrastructure and vulnerable people and communities. When asked what mitigation ecosystem services would be most affected by climate change, the AE responded as follows:

- The targeted infrastructure projects would most likely impact ecosystems as well as ecosystem services.

The ICF is designed as a fund to support investment in climate resilience of all the infrastructure it will finance. The aim and purpose of the ICF are to de-risk and scale up investments in resilient infrastructure as all infrastructure types have a recognized vulnerability to climate change impacts. In order to ensure that the ICF is well-targeted, considering the lifespan of the infrastructure AFC invests in today, GCF and AFC have agreed on a set of criteria for determining which projects will be eligible for funding under the sub-projects in which ICF may consider investing. Climate risk screening to define the climate-related investment opportunities is an important part of this process.

Climate risks and future climates are a central part of the eligibility criteria. GCF concessional funding will primarily cover incremental costs for adaptation measures to make infrastructure assets resilient to climate change impacts. The ICF aims to leverage its concessional funding to attract private sector investment in ICF and thereby in climate change in line with Article 2, paragraph 1 (b) of the Paris Agreement. In order to ensure that projects are fit-for-beneficiaries, number and value of assets made resilient to climate change. During the original design phase of the ICF, AFC worked closely with GCF to define the criteria for concessional funding. With the goal of making infrastructure resilient to climate change, ICF is a 100 per cent adaptation program. None of the GCF mitigation result areas will apply to ICF (ICF investments are not expected to generate any significant co-benefits in terms of carbon emissions). With the goal of making infrastructure resilient to climate change, ICF is a 100 per cent adaptation program. None of the GCF mitigation result areas will apply to ICF (ICF investments are not expected to generate any significant co-benefits in terms of carbon emissions).

Therefore, mitigation and ecosystem are not eligible under the ICF program or the ICF fund.

While the funding proposal was mainly focused on the ICF, its adaptation results areas also included the most vulnerable peoples and communities, as well as infrastructure and built assets that are particularly exposed to climate change. The ICF has been mainly focused on. Resulting in limited or general screening on infrastructure assets alone, neglecting the broader context of how these assets interact with and affect vulnerable people and communities' access to the climate-resilient infrastructure, as discussed in paragraph 21 of the previous version. This is a key difference between the independent TAP and ICF approaches that needs to be addressed. As mentioned in paragraph 21 of the previous version the examples and narratives in the funding proposal are not representative of the full range of potential co-benefits between the independent TAP and ICF approaches that needs to be addressed.

In with the revision of the funding proposal for B35, the AE has addressed these issues. The AE has revised the climate risk screening methodology as described in the operations manual where the methodology has been further detailed following a system approach, including impacts on people and communities, infrastructure assets, ecosystems, livelihoods and interdependent built infrastructure. These two chapters of the operations manual clearly address the co-benefits between ICF and TAP. The AE has also revised the climate risk screening methodology as described in the funding proposal, section B14.8 Climate Risk Impact and Investment Assessment Methodology: Guiding Principles. Furthermore, this has been reflected in annex 2z6, Climate Assessment Methodology for Infrastructure Sites. This new approach is more detailed with respect to the infrastructure sites. The climate risk impact methodology is now set out in much more detail, with the specific focus on co-benefits between ICF and TAP.

See Chapters 6 and 7 of the operations manual, annex 2z6.

# Climate Risk Assessment Methodology

## Overview of the Process

The figure below illustrates a simplified version of the ICF Climate Risk Assessment Methodology.

| Step | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Step 1: Scoping and Initial Assessment** | Identify key climate risks, vulnerabilities, and opportunities. |
| **Step 2: Climate Risk and Impact Assessment** | Assess potential impacts on project objectives and financial viability. |
| **Step 3: Mitigation Strategy Development** | Develop strategies to reduce identified risks and enhance resilience. |
| **Step 4: Implementation and Monitoring** | Implement mitigation measures and continuously monitor their effectiveness. |

## Weak Technical and Operational Capacity on Climate

In the first submission of the funding proposal for B34, the independent TAP assessed that the AFC and ICF Team had weak institutional capacity to address climate change issues. The TAP noted that there was a lack of experience in the team at the technical competence of the AFC in climate issues.

The following are some key points from the TAP assessment:

- Weak technical and operational capacity on climate.
- Lack of experience in addressing climate change issues.
- Need for further training and development to enhance institutional capacity.

multilateral development bank (MDB) finance reporting guidelines?”. As to the request for demonstration of, or documentation on, how climate had specifically impacted AFC’s portfolio through the years, AFC had still to provide credible evidence-based data; considering that AFC’s portfolio was not large enough to be representative of the MDBs’ overall climate impact. It was believed to be the most impacted type of infrastructure, the independent TAP hoped that it would have been very straightforward and easy to show using AFC’s own portfolio, but no data or figures were provided to show this.

Thus, in response to the question of climate finance accounting, although AFC had been maintaining a separate account for climate-related projects, it was not possible to provide an answer with a “no”. AFC could have easily cited those of its energy projects that had involved 95 per cent mitigation and 5 per cent adaptation. The TAP noted that AFC had also provided information on the total project cost, and that this was reflected in the Joint Report on Multilateral Development Banks’ Climate Finance for another similar report, but it did not. The point of the question was to assess its level-based approach to climate finance accounting. It was agreed by all parties that it is extremely important, as ICFD would be tasked with accounting for the climate-related finance and investment flows in the context of the 2015 Paris Agreement. This would require a close alignment with the ICFR and the independent TAP needed to understand if ICFD were capable of differentiating climate change adaptation from mitigation projects, as well as other types of projects.

Similarly, ICFD should be capable of accounting for climate-related finance costs for project components such as solar power plants, wind farms, hydroelectric dams, nuclear power plants and thermal power plants. To provide another example, in building an economic zone, would ICFR, under the framework of the ICFR, be able to account for the climate change adaptation cost of building as climate finance? AFC had mentioned that it was familiar with the MFI approach to climate finance, but it had not demonstrated how it has mainstreamed this in its operations. With AFC’s portfolio being small and not representative of the MDBs’ overall climate impact, the AE must ensure that proper skills, experience and technical capacities are available at the climate governance level to support the implementation of the ICFR. This would also help to ensure climate-related goals, objectives and strategic interventions in GCF were ensured and safeguarded.

The AE also revised the B318 funding proposal to include additional information on climate risk screening and resilience measures in sections 7.2 and 7.5. In terms of finance accounting, the AE has provided a detailed response on how AFC could mainstream the inclusion of climate-resilient infrastructure, and will only count as adaptation finance the incremental cost of adaptation measures in the ICFR info projects. The AE also emphasized that GCF concessional funding would be available to support AFC’s technical capacity to contribute to financing the cost of adaptation measures.

25. Weak climate-resilience indicators. In the first submission of the funding proposal for B34.8, the independent TAP assessed that it included weak climate-resilience indicators. As presented in Table 1, the indicators were not clearly linked to climate change and/or were not clearly climate-linked or not clearly stated. Specifically, for airports, indicators that were not clearly climate-related included: total number of aircraft movements; total amount of cargo tonnage; total amount of cargo tonnage; total number of passengers annually; broken down by mode (i.e., domestic and international); total number of passengers annually; broken down by mode (i.e., domestic and international); and transfer passengers, including transit passengers). The indicators on insurance that were not clearly elaborated included: reduction in insurance premiums; current/expected impact on commercial aviation; downtime of telecommunications infrastructure; and improved range and quality of weather forecasts. For flood risk management, the indicators on insurance that were not clearly elaborated included: reduction in insurance premiums or downtime of telecommunication infrastructure led to an improvement in insurance premiums or downtime of telecommunication infrastructure. While the following metrics were not explicitly linked to climate change, they could be considered as indirect indicators of climate change (e.g., from the climate-resilient bond initiative). The independent TAP observes that the AF has done well in this area by including a number of climate-related indicators for both materialized development benefits, variable sector assets (e.g. from the climate-resilient bond initiative) and GHG emissions.

1.1.3 Mitigation co-benefits

The funding proposal also considers mitigation to be a co-benefit; there are no mitigation co-benefits that clearly identify that "climate change is an emergency that demands rapid and far-reaching action to cut the global carbon footprint and build resilience to more extreme weather events". The funding proposal does not clearly identify that climate change mitigation would be a co-benefit and the cost of carbon reduced from these projects as an externality the value of which is considered in the project's values of service.

As per the proposal, GHG emissions are assessed and monitored under environmental safeguards where GHGs are considered as environmental risks (i.e. pollution). However, the GHG emissions monitored are those attributable both directly and indirectly from purchased

electricity exceeding 100,000 tons annually. The framework document, however, does not qualify these GHGs include methane (CH4), nitrous oxide (N2O), perfluorocarbons (PFCs), hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs), sulphur hexafluoride (SF6) and nitrogen trifluoride (NF3). It also does not qualify these GHGs as being subject to the Kyoto Protocol or reported as part of the disclosure of the ICRF investments.

The independent TAP acknowledges that a funding proposal for an adaptation project. However, it is important to note that this applies only after the lifting of an infrastructure project once the project is fully operational, especially in the case of road and transport projects. At the moment, the GHG emissions associated with these projects are not included in the global average but the transport sector, for example, is already a major source of GHG emissions as it accounts for 23% of total global emissions. The TAP also notes that the project would form part of the project risks to be mitigated so as to (i) prevent a carbon lock-up or stranded assets in the future; (ii) align the infrastructure projects with the Paris Agreement objectives of the ICRF, and (iii) provide an opportunity for countries to access funding from mechanisms that seek projects that are aligned to the Paris Agreement, thus increasing the attractiveness of ICRF.

By doing so, this assessment of GHG emissions will also promote transparency and enable potential strategic partners to address the risk of such emissions. The independent TAP also sees this as a way to help countries to meet their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) under the Paris Agreement, and for the NDC countries to achieve their Paris Agreement commitments, and for the AE to optimize the potential for mitigation opportunities in infrastructure projects. This will also help to reduce GHG mitigation commitments under the Paris Agreement no matter how small their GHG emissions.

Overall, based on the above, the impact potential is mainly high depending on how the above-mentioned issues are addressed.


### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

As per the funding proposal, if the ICRF mandate is implemented correctly, the ICRF will support a structural paradigm shift in the African infrastructure space through the systematic integration of climate resilience into new and existing infrastructure projects design, construction, and maintenance to strengthen resilience to climate change. However, building climate-resilient infrastructure requires a significant paradigm shift from traditional approaches that focus on increasing autonomous adaptation measures and may call it a 'build back better' scheme. For countries that have already developed their policies, plans, or strategies for climate-related investments and expenditures, the reference to climate-proofing these critical infrastructure based on available resources is not new. However, for countries with limited resources, there are no existing policy, engineering, non-engineering or management measures to manage the most critical risks in their priority sectors.

In the case of Africa, "where most of the infrastructure is yet to be built,"21 the timely entry of ICRF to introduce the development of climate-resilient infrastructure offers a good opportunity for countries to build resilience into new and existing infrastructure. As per the funding proposal for B34, the independent TAP found it hard to determine that degree of paradigm shift that would be required in order to achieve this objective. The independent TAP also found the funding proposal to describe the baselines of each of these infrastructures. However, as discussed above, with the addition of new baseline assessments as part of the resubmitted

### Document B.35 (ex. annexes 2b and 2b), much greater clarity is provided on the potential paradigm shift

#### Section 36: Within the framework of the climate finance and investments, the independent TAP finds that the proposed creation of a dedicated funding mechanism for climate resilience and adaptation within ICBF could be considered as an innovative financing model. The insurance component or finance can be safely categorized as climate resilience or adaptation investments, separate and distinct from other types of investments such as mitigation (e.g., renewable energy projects) or generic green bonds. There is the potential for ICBF to squarely address the issues of RAI investment in infrastructure and the need for a dedicated funding mechanism for climate resilience and adaptation. The ICBF will provide clarity on the definition of a climate-resilient asset class, as differentiated from other types of investments such as mitigation or generic green bonds that may impact investments. If successfully managed and implemented, ICBF could be the first vehicle of its kind to completely reorient the way climate adaptation investments are modeled and implemented in infrastructure projects. A key role of ICBF has the potential to become the model financial mechanism facilitating the delivery of private sector climate finance for adaptation and resilience.

#### Section 37: The innovative financial model formulated under ICBF will also enable the much needed transformational impact on the insurance industry, as well as the broader economy. The financial model adopted demonstrates how investments should take into account the impacts of climate. The ability to integrate ICFP’s approach by leveraging other funding for NDCs’ NDC needs is promising.

#### Section 38: Within the framework of climate risk management, the initiatives to conduct a feasibility study on insurance component under B.34 are considered as an important step towards taking the first steps toward a paradigm shift on climate infrastructure risk management as per the funding proposal submitted for B.34. The ICBF will provide a dedicated funding mechanism for managing the risk of climate extreme events in the infrastructure sector. While there is a parametric insurance market in place, it is not well developed outside Africa and Asia. There is no parametric insurance for infrastructure on the African continent. Therefore, from that perspective, and it can be assumed that infrastructure offers the next opportunity to diversify the risk profile of the insurance industry.

#### Section 39: In the first submission for B.34, the AE stated that: “The insurance component of the ICBF program will be a pioneering pilot in private sector dedicated climate resilience and adaptation investments in African countries. Therefore, we are not crowding out other the industry players, but rather it providing a demonstration effect supporting the development of the insurance market in Africa. The AE also noted that this is an important step toward transforming the insurance industry as well as the finance industry as this will risk the climate-sensitive components of the targeted infrastructure projects. The ICBF will provide a dedicated funding mechanism for managing investments in the targeted countries based on the integration of science-based data, metrics and standards, as well as adequate legal frameworks. Also, the introduction of new construction code for infrastructure projects in Africa is expected to have a transformational impact in establishing CRI as a new distinct asset class.”23

#### Section 40: However, after extensive consultation with GCF, the AE decided that the insurance component has been scaled down to conducting a feasibility study. The AE has stated that:

After extensive consultation with GCF, the AE decided that the insurance activities under the programme will only consist of USD 1 million in technical assistance to undertake a


---
**Page 175**

feasibility study that will support the development of potential parametric climate risk insurance products (fit-for-purpose for African infrastructure) in collaboration with insurance companies and various stakeholders. Therefore, the insurance premium finance is not included as part of the ICFR funding proposal and will be mobilized through a separate donor programme. That said, the AE is planning to partner with other donors (on a best effort basis) to mobilize this financing for the implementation of parametric climate risk insurance after the study and design phase.[^1]
