### 1.2 Risk to paradigm shift

The success of ICFR as a game changer will greatly depend on:

(i) how judiciously it screens potential subprojects for true resilience components using an integrated approach that not only focuses on assets, but also on the vulnerable people, communities, critical ecosystems, climate-sensitive livelihoods and infrastructure, and their interdependencies;

(ii) how prudently it will account for the climate-related finance for each project; 

(iii) how it will mitigate the GHG emissions that may arise, including from the development and operations of these ICFR infrastructure assets.

Overall, the AE is advocating for the prioritization and demonstration of technical capacities of the AE on climate investment assessments. The paradigm shift potential can be assessed as high.

### Economic development
Infrastructure development sits at the heart of all development pathways and is closely linked to economic growth, environmental outcomes, and well-being. It is a key enabler for poverty reduction, inclusive growth and development because of its constructive impact on multidimensional aspects of society. By promoting climate-resilient infrastructure, ICRF will support countries in developing their own development strategies to avoid the lock-in of fragile infrastructure and stranded assets.

### Social, gender-sensitive development, and environment
By promoting climate-resilient infrastructure, ICRF will contribute to social, gender-sensitive development by helping to generate jobs, facilitating access to employment opportunities and contributing to the development of skills and capabilities. It will also help to improve livelihoods, well-being of workers; and protect the interests of displaced individuals and communities, including women, children, youth, people with disabilities, and indigenous peoples (IPs). The benefits of the infrastructure to be promoted by ICRF will be that it will be inclusive and accessible to everyone, particularly underserved groups such as women and children. Additionally, ICRF will promote gender equality.

### The funding proposal has also prepared an indigenous peoples management framework
The IPMF is a tool for managing projects with IPs in the project area. The IPMF sets out the principles, rules, guidelines and procedures for properly identifying and assessing the potential positive and negative impacts of the programme's activities on IPs. It identifies the measures that will be taken to ensure that IPs are not adversely affected by managing impacts on IPs will be met by the programme. The IPFM further recommends impact mitigation strategies and provides guidance on how to manage relationships, agreements, arrangements and appropriate costs.

### Gender and social inclusion assessment
A gender and social inclusion assessment and action plan has been prepared. It is noteworthy that ICRF has achieved the goals set in 60-70 per cent representation of women in senior positions in ICRF and ACP (i.e., on boards and committee meetings).

ICRF will support projects with a clear economic case for promoting environmental principles in infrastructure investment. In addition, ICRF will support low-carbon investments, hence contributing to reducing CO2 emissions. ICRF only submitted a one-and-a-half page document entitled "Environmental and social safeguards".

### Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

1. **Infrastructures**, which ICFR intends to deliver, are in general always needed by African countries. In the first submission of the funding proposal for B34, the independent TAP raised the question of whether these infrastructures were indeed the priority needs of the NOL countries.
2. The needs of the country: ACF stated upon receipt of the funding proposal for B34 that its portfolio, which mirrors the ICFR target portfolio, had the implied approval of the NOL countries. However, the TAP raised a question about whether these infrastructures were arguably true, this assertion was weakened by the absence of a specific statement of concern or need from the NOL countries. The TAP also questioned how these infrastructures met their priority needs.
3. This, given the absence of a baseline assessment on the impact of each country's infrastructure, whether these were indeed the priority needs of the NOL countries could not be determined.

### Needs of the vulnerable people and communities: Similarly, infrastructures to be delivered by ICFR, depending on where these will be built, may, directly or indirectly, render some communities more vulnerable. For example, a new road might connect a rural community which lacked a bridge connecting it to its schools and markets, whereas loss of this infrastructure could have been avoided if the road had not been built in an area prone to heavy rains intensified by climate change. It could be that there could be positive co-benefits from an infrastructure investment, or negative impacts, or even maladaptation that undermines the resilience of a community. In such cases, it would be important to assess whether infrastructures would be needed, or the extent to which they would be able to address the vulnerability of communities and their ability to adapt to climate change in light of their local and specific contexts. Given then the weak baseline assessments on each of the countries' infrastructure needs, it is not clear that these infrastructures would enhance the resilience of the people and communities.

### Needs of the private sector investors: There is always a need for an assessment which includes the potential economic, social, environmental, financial, safety, liquidity,

# Need for climate risk insurance by infra stakeholders

In the first submission for 13.4.2021, it was noted that there is a lack of comprehensive climate risk management tool, especially for the residual risks of the proposed infrastructure projects. The question was raised whether any form of insurance or other financial protection would be needed in the target region where the NOL countries are located, in the absence of an in-depth market assessment of the insurance sector. The answer was unknown as were the needs of the NOL countries. When asked about the level of insurance penetration and quick overview as to the penetration rate of insurance in the target countries, the AE responded:

- Insurance penetration is very low in Africa, with an observed downturn over the recent past years.
- In 2020, Africa's average insurance penetration was around 2 per cent (from a 7.29 per cent average for OECD countries).
- The average insurance penetration rate of NOL countries was only 1.35 per cent and 4.46 per cent in OECD countries.
- As an illustration, see Figure 2 on insurance penetration rates by country in Africa (source: Climate Resilience Fund [CRF] - Deloitte Africa Insurance Outlook (2022)): penetration rates ranged from as low as 0.52per cent to a high of 13 per cent.

This response gave the independent TAF a glimpse of the insurance market in the target countries and confirmed that there was more to evaluate. Due to the lack of an in-depth market assessment of the insurance sector, it remained to be seen whether this would indeed be the solution needed.
- This could lead to uncertain without knowing and understanding which climate risk management solutions were currently available and planned by international insurers or reinsurers, including AEC's responses, Africa Risk Capacity (Africa Catastrophe Insurance), Swiss RE, and the World Bank's own experience in this area and any other potential lessons or best practices.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

### Scal: High

Leveraging GCF capital to de-risk investments. The GCF USD 240 million funding will be used primarily to fund the incremental costs of adaptation and resilience and/or risk-investment projects, which are expected to leverage an additional USD 510 million in co-financing per project level. The USD 240 million will be matched by USD 510 million coming from ACF (USD 50 million), and other institutional investors (e.g., pension, sovereign wealth funds, insurance companies).

Through the GCF catalyst first-loss capital position, the ICFB investment objective is to leverage private sector co-financing of USD 240 million in order to accelerate investments into climate-resilient infrastructure projects in AEC. The ICFB’s approach to de-risking investments is detailed in the operations manual (annex 21 of the funding proposal). Among GCF’s key tools for de-risking are: a) enhanced risk assessment and impact assessment methodology (see paras 17 and 18 above), b) innovative climate insurance, c) co-financing with other development partners, d) technical assistance for the conduct of a feasibility study on climate insurance, and other policy dialogue and frameworks development in the ICFB targeted sectors.

See ACF response to the independent TAP question on AFC-ICFB dated 16 January 2023.


---
**Page 182**
---

### Mobilization at the subproject level by GCF: ICF equity and mainstream investments will mobilize USD 1.32 billion (USD 50 million of GCF equity + USD 1.27 billion of debt financing), equivalent to USD 1.55 - 1.48 billion additional debt financing, bringing the total project value at the subproject level to USD 6.9 billion. At the same time, the mobilization effect at the subject level by GCF is 5.2x (USD 1.55 + USD 1.48/USD 0.30 = USD 1.32 billion average mobilization at the subproject level) = USD 1.32 billion x USD 24.5 million total GCF funding = ~5.2x.

### Total mobilization ratio at the ICF fund and subject levels is envisaged to be >7.2x as to achieve the target of USD 6.9 billion project value, which is equivalent to a private sector adaptation programme (USD 510 million on the level) + USD 3.2 billion on the subproject level.

### Incremental cost of the ICF will be covered by the GCF concessional junior equity (with a 3 per cent internal rate of return) thus allowing private sector capital to hit target hurdle rates of at 15-20 per cent, which is significantly lower than the 27.4 per cent average hurdle rate compared to standard infrastructure project investments (the demonstration cases suggest 5.2–
### High cost will deliver the following intended benefits: (i) higher quality and more resilient infrastructure; (ii) reduced risk of climate-related asset damage; (iii) less revenue disruptions due to the ability to withstand disaster climate-related weather events, including floods, droughts, heatwaves, storms, and wildfires; (iv) additional socioeconomic benefits (increased safety for vulnerable people and communities, increased productivity).

### It is based on an integrated approach of providing technical assistance for project preparation (e.g., climate risk, vulnerability and adaptation options assessment), doing a feasibility study, preparing the investment proposal, and developing a set of climate-related risk standards and policies. And GCF concessional equity (with 3 per cent first loss protection for investors) can effectively de-risk the investments among institutional private sector investors (including commercial banks, insurance companies, pension funds, development financial institutions and foundations), and enhance the development of climate resilient infrastructure.

### Cost-effectiveness and cost-efficiency

The ICFB will be invested in a total of 15 projects, at an average of USD 32.72 million per project (USD 490 million in total). The cost per year for a period of 30 years, GCF cost of funds is at 3 per cent (Tier 1) while Tier 2 investors’ (AFD, other investors) hurdle rates are at 12.5 per cent. GCF investment of USD 246 million will be matched by USD 3.1 billion from the subject level. At a leverage ratio of 1:13 and an internal rate of return of 7.8%, the payback multiple, the value of the equity that investment holdings for 30 years will have generated in GCF returns is 24.

# Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel Assessment Findings

## Proposal name: Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF)

## Accredited entity: Africa Finance Corporation (AFC)

### Impact potential

AFC is committed to mobilizing blended finance at a scale and accelerating investments in high-impact climate-resilient infrastructure projects to reduce the risks associated with projected climate change impacts on critical infrastructure, thereby enhancing the resilience of communities and related beneficiaries on the African continent. The ICRF is targeting 350,365,061 direct beneficiaries and 144,115,789 indirect beneficiaries. We appreciate and concur with the ITAP assessment which ranks the impact potential High.

### Paradigm shift potential

The program will champion and mainstream a novel approach to integrating physical climate risks into the design of new climate-resilient infrastructure assets which are planned, designed, built and operated in a way that anticipates, prepares for, and adapts to changing climate conditions. Resilience assets can be designed and constructed with an understanding of future climate change conditions. By enabling the mobilization of Blended capital at scale, ICRF will on one hand invest in the resilience measures needed for long-lasting infrastructure investments and future climate risks; but also on the other hand, it will increase the availability of investment opportunities for resilient infrastructure. ICFR concessionality will be crucial to enable the financing of adaptation measures and projects that are not commercially viable at market rates. The program seeks the mobilization of institutional investors such as pension funds, insurance companies, and sovereign wealth funds in Africa and globally.

The AE appreciates and concurs with ITAPs assessment of ICRF’s paradigm shift potential.

### Sustainable development potential

We are in agreement with the ITAPs assessment regarding the sustainable development potential. ICRF presents a real potential to contribute to sustainable development with impacts on all the dimensions of sustainable development, including economic, social, and environmental aspects for its implementation: Economic; Environment and Social including consideration for gender equality, health, safety and well-being. AFC is committed to developing projects that will contribute to the following SDGs: 7,8,10,11,3 and 16.

### Needs of the recipient

AFC agrees with ITAP’s assessment regarding the needs of the recipient. The origination and development of ICRF have been informed by an in-depth analysis and understanding of the needs of the particular communities that will be impacted by climate change. This understanding has further been reinforced by the demonstrated evidence of the critical need for resilient infrastructure on the


---
**Page 187**
---

### Country ownership

Country ownership is pivotal to the GCF design and implementation. We agree with the FEAP assessment on country ownership as we believe that the NCR countries are very much aligned with the target sectors of the ICBF. AFC engaged collaboratively with all participating countries during the original design phase of the program, including a series of workshops held in each country to engage stakeholders of the target countries to enable them to understand the structure of the ICBF program, the choice of the financial instruments, confirm with them how the program aligns with their NDCs, their national development plans, policies, strategies, and programs.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

We are in agreement with the ITAP assessment of efficiency and effectiveness. AFC has demonstrated its ability to deliver high-quality projects that meet the needs of the countries by providing robust case studies with financial models incorporating effects on climate change under different climate scenarios. We will ensure that the GCF financing is effectively deployed across the various target sectors of the ICBF.

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Current and future infrastructures in Africa face risks associated with climate change which is often not a key consideration in infrastructure planning, design, financing, commissioning, and construction. Climate-resilient infrastructure would help provide the quantity, quality, and accessibility of infrastructure and the associated services to connect people and enhance the quality of life.

The Africa Finance Corporation (AFC) wholly-owned Capital Partners is establishing the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF) to offer tailored financial products (concessional equity and performance-based debt) that will help mitigate the risks posed by climate change and other physical hazards on the physical infrastructures in the selected African nations. The ICRF will be supported by targeted technical assistance and policy interventions to ensure a long-term and systemic approach to addressing these risks.

This report provides an overview of the Gender and Social Inclusion Risks that might be associated to the implementation of the ICRF in the target countries, with the view to propose an action plan to ensure a gender-responsive implementation of the ICRF.

The proposed targets 19 countries in Africa and is structured around three (3) components:

- **Component 1 – Financial instruments to reduce market failure and attract investment in Climate Resilience:**
- **Component 2 – Strengthening capacity, systems, and networks to improve understanding of current and future risks;** and
- **Component 3 – Policy interventions to support investments in Climate Resilient Infrastructure.**

This Gender and Social Inclusion Assessment and Action plan has been developed using a participatory approach to complement intensive documentation review to understand the national and regional context for gender and social inclusion, as well as the specific risks associated with ICRF investments and takes into consideration the potential for additional risks associated to climate change and specific gender considerations.

This report is structured in seven (7) parts:

1. General presentation of the ICRF
2. Connection between climate-resilient infrastructure and Gender considerations
3. Analysis of gender and socioeconomic indicators at country level
4. Gender disparities to infrastructural access and services, resulting from the survey in ICRF countries
5. Considerations of other social inclusion dimensions in infrastructure
6. Considerations of gender and social inclusion entry points in the program implementation
7. Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## II. ACP INFRASTRUCTURE CLIMATE RESILIENT FUND (ICRF)

### 2.1 Investment Objective

The principal objective of the ICRF is to deploy capital to support the development and financing of climate-resilient infrastructure projects in Africa and to achieve medium-to-long-term returns for investors. The Fund will invest in a diversified portfolio of infrastructure projects, including ports, roads, bridges, rail, telecommunication, clean energy, and logistics projects) with the objective of making these assets more resilient to the impacts of climate change while being in accordance with the Paris Agreement.

### 2.2 Investment Themes

ICRF will focus on infrastructure core sectors including:

i. Climate-resilient transport and logistics (including ports, roads, bridges, airports, etc.)

ii. Climate-resilient energy systems

iii. Climate-resilient economic zones

iv. Climate-resilient telecommunication and digital infrastructures

### 2.3 Investment Strategy

ICRF will invest in greenfield and brownfield infrastructure acquisition and growth equity financing combined with concessional capital from international and multilateral sources like the Green Climate Fund (GCF) that have committed significant pools of capital to climate resilience financing for emerging markets, including Africa. ICRF will seek to identify and pursue investment opportunities that fit its climate mandate and investment criteria, hence benefiting from AFC’s strong track record in identifying and managing climate risks along with related resilience and adaptation considerations to incorporate ICRF investment decision-making process.

ICRF will hold a significant or majority interest in the Project Companies in which it invests and will seek to have an equity stake of approximately seven to ten years of the respective Project Company's lifecycle. ICRF will invest up to 20% of its committed capital in project development. ICRF will target projects that exhibit the following characteristics:

1 The Fund will not invest into fossil-fuel-based energy solutions, and this covenant will be included in the exclusion list


---
**Page 195**
---

I. Ability to generate sustainable positive cash flows and offer returns commensurate with the risk associated with the investment.

II. Strong governance, deep pockets, solid experience, and track record, as well as technical expertise in the relevant sector and region.

III. Credible, experienced, and independent EPC and O&M contractors.

IV. Offer a strategic competitive advantage: Projects with manageable or predictable market dynamics, as well as proven and stable or growing demand from credible off-takers or a broad and deep marketplace.

V. Demonstrate alignment with Paris Agreement Goals (commitment for emissions reduction) and the avoidance of financial flows to low emissions and climate resilience), as well as a commitment to best practice ESG standards.

VI. Projects that have all relevant permits/licenses/concessions and authorizations or have a clear and realistic plan to secure all relevant permits and authorizations in a timely manner.

VII. Sound and transparent governance.

VIII. Predominantly exhibit equity characteristics.

### III. CONNECTION BETWEEN CLIMATE-RESILIENT INFRASTRUCTURE AND GENDER CONSIDERATIONS

The impact of climate change on infrastructure is experienced across the globe. However, in Africa, the level of exposure of critical infrastructure to climate change is dire as the continent records some of the most extreme climatic conditions. Unfortunately, several existing and upcoming infrastructures are not built with climate resilience.

In addition, there is a strong relationship between climate change and environment-based livelihoods, which relates to gender. Climate change also affects the allocation of tasks, resources, and time in various ways for men and women, especially in Africa. For example, climate change increases the burden on women who are responsible for fetching water, cooking food, managing household level and caring for children and other vulnerable people. Women mainly undertake these tasks, thus limiting their time for other economic and social activities. In addition to others borne out of the socio-economic status of women in Africa, such as lack of access to education, health care, and technology, these factors make women especially vulnerable to climate change. When infrastructure alleviates these burdens, women usually remain at risk of extra labor time when such infrastructure endures climate hazards that impact its performance.

There is a strong relationship between the climate, the resilient-climate infrastructure and gender considerations.

Climate change affects the allocation of tasks, resources, and time in various ways for men and women, especially in Africa:

For example, it increases water burden connected to the provision of food, water, and energy which are essential for life and leads to fetching for children and other vulnerable people. Women mainly undertake these tasks, thus limiting their time for other economic and social activities. In addition to others borne out of the socio-economic status of women in Africa, such as lack of access to education, health care, and technology, these factors make women especially vulnerable to climate change. When infrastructure alleviates these burdens, women usually remain at risk of extra labor time when such infrastructure endures climate hazards that impact its performance.

The impact of climate change on infrastructure is experienced across the globe. However, in Africa, the level of exposure of critical infrastructure to climate change is dire as the continent records some of the most extreme climatic conditions. Unfortunately, several existing and upcoming infrastructures are not built with climate resilience.

Climate change disproportionately impacts people across the board. Generally, marginalized populations group, including women, suffer more of the negative impact of climate change. It is noteworthy that women and girls are 14 times more likely to die during a disaster, as “natural disasters often exacerbate existing gender inequalities and increase women’s vulnerability to their fatal impact.” A World Bank report, Shockwaves, notes that women are most vulnerable during disasters. This increases their hardships during floods and their risk of contracting waterborne diseases.

Furthermore, the disproportion in household activities translates to a disproportion in income-generating labor hours. As a result, women are more likely to experience loss of housing and lack


---
**Page 198**
---

Page 194

access to services and relief. In addition, the female population is at far greater risk of physical and sexual violence during and after disasters.

Women are more vulnerable than men to job losses in times of crisis. Women only account for 39 percent of global employment and about 54 percent of overall job losses during the COVID-19 pandemic2. As workplaces closed, the earnings of many self-employed and hourly wage female workers were cut off completely. The loss of income is particularly acute among women living in poverty because the pandemic affected sectors where female employment is high3. Women’s loss of income often has long-lasting effects and compounds the vulnerability of children as well. Women lost 10 million jobs during the COVID-19 crisis, compared to men who lost only 5 million4.

The social and cultural norms in the program countries impose additional constraints on women; the division of labor in the household is gendered, this responsibility on women limits them from engaging in income-generating activities or pursuing further education. In a context where energy and transport infrastructure are heavily impacted by climate change, women would require even more time to access these infrastructural services using conventional means that are less efficient and time-consuming.

Women lack access to knowledge and climate information generated by climate information and early warning systems (CIWES) relevant for preparedness, adaptation, risk reduction, and disaster risk management. Most of the CIWES in the program countries are gendered; this is partly due to women’s low access to media and telecommunication infrastructure through which climate information is disseminated. The lack of these infrastructural services also means that women face the least access compared to men and such infrastructure is also exposed to various climate stressors.

Although most countries today have a national gender policy or strategy, gender is often considered an after-thought, and gender policies are usually linked in their implementation. Paternalism and lack of political will, religious roadblocks, coupled with scarce resources impede the effective implementation of the gender-responsive legal and regulatory framework. Through communication and capacity building activities, this program aims to build the capacity of local governments implementing activities that consider gender equality and equity. This program will draw lessons from the successful experiences in other countries and implement them in the program countries to ensure that women have full access to resilient infrastructure and infrastructural services.

To ensure that the program reduces the gender disparities in the implementation area, the output in establishing new infrastructure or strengthening existing ones is more resilient to climate change should translate to fewer burdens and more economic and social access for women.

However, gender disproportion is observed in critical sectors such as employment, politics, and education. This stresses the need to ensure a conscious gender plan in the program activities in response to gender disparity.

### IV. IN-DEPTH ANALYSIS OF GENDER AND SOCIOECONOMIC INDICATORS AT COUNTRY-LEVEL

#### 4.1 Benin

##### Demographics and human development

With an area of 114,736km² and an estimated population in 2020 of 12,200,588 people of whom 50.7% are females, Benin is a West African country bordered by Togo (West), Burkina Faso and Niger (North), Nigeria (East) and the Atlantic Ocean (South). The country's population age structure is very young: 77.64% of its people are under 15 years of age and 48% under 15 years of age. The population growth rate is high (3.9%) and the total fertility rate is 5.2 children per woman. The majority of the population is rural (55.4%) and cotton is the main export product contributing 13% to GDP.

Benin has adopted a national gender policy and action plan to combat gender-based violence and poverty, and to promote women's empowerment. The situation for women in Benin's social status has been improved, according to national statistics. In 2019, the Africa Gender Equality Index shows a generally high level of gender equality in Benin (0.78). However, Benin is still below average on the overall scores; scores in the social and economic domain are close to parity (0.776 and 0.728 respectively) but low in the area of representation and empowerment (0.145). Benin's HDI value for 2019 is 0.545, which places it among the lower-middle income countries showing a gender gap index of 0.613 (countries and territories and a Gender Inequality Index (GII) value of 0.61), ranking 148 out of 182 countries in the 2019 index.

##### Education

Education is highly important in Benin and central government is redoubling its efforts to make education for all a reality in this sector in Benin. It was observed that, in Benin, 59.4% of women have no level of education, 24.9% of women had primary education, 14.2% secondary education and only 1.3% have completed tertiary education or higher. The gender gap index for Benin is 0.68 in the area of access to all for primary education and in the first cycle of secondary education where the first cycle gender parity index (GPI) was 0.97 in 2019. The second cycle GPI was 0.53, while the tertiary GPI was 0.39 in 2019. The gender inequalities observed in secondary, technical, vocational and higher education have impacted negatively on women's participation in employment and as a result, on their incomes.

##### Employment

# Health

It was noted from the recent Demographic and health survey (2017-2018) in Benin that antenatal health care coverage for women was higher in urban than in rural areas (90% compared to 79%). The contraceptive prevalence rate is also higher among women with a secondary education level or more, at 46%, and for those at the higher education level, the use of contraceptive methods is higher. The fertility rate has decreased from 5 children per woman over the period between 2007 - 2017 period. Access to health establishments is more difficult in rural areas than in urban areas because of distance, inaccessibility and lack of appropriate facilities.

## Energy, transport, and telecommunications

The production and reproductive risks assigned to women expose them to the negative impacts of climate change; it is important to factor into the climate change impact adaptation and mitigation measures, the different needs of women and men to provide an appropriate and sustainable response to the challenges posed by climate change. Women are more dependent on firewood as a primary energy source. In terms of access to employment and entrepreneurship in this sector, men dominate; which sustains gender inequalities despite the existence of a legal and institutional framework for gender mainstreaming.

Women are the most concerned regarding energy for cooking, unlike men who are more interested in other uses (lighting, comfort, ICT, productive energy, etc.). Energy used for cooking and preserving food in Benin is mainly wood (80%), which is also the main cause of indoor air pollution and respiratory diseases (as a result of an pollutant in houses due to cooking with traditional biomass). According to ERH (2019), the average consumption per household is 3.5 kg/day, which corresponds to different combinations of energy types for cooking: wood and charcoal (23.5%) and coal-and-gas (76.5%). The average consumption of natural gas is 8.9 kg/household) and the proportion of households using improved stoves was only very low (17.4%).

Energy demand is growing rapidly, with a national electrification rate that is still low, but has increased from 13.0% in 1996 to 58.1% in 2015 with an average annual increase of 7.7%. In urban areas, the rate now stands at 84%, and it is estimated that by 2025 this will reach 90%. The national consumption for electrical energy has increased by 10.4% annually, from 105 MWh in 2006 to 192 MWh in 2014. In terms of electricity access, the coverage rate is estimated at 38%, and the number of households connected to the grid (using improved stoves) was only very low (7%). The 2015 ERH indicates that in 2015, 1,654 localities were electrified out of the 3753 localities in Benin, a coverage rate of 43%.

However, Benin has a relatively large hydroelectric potential capacity of at least 400 MW. For the main sites identified and evaluated in the NDP (2018-2025) namely Adjagpoa 147 MW on the Mono River, Kéffou


---
**Page 204**
---

Page 200

Dogo 108 MW, Vissa 79.2 MW, Berthel 42.4 MW, Mélouro 30 MW and Gougâh 29.4 MW on the Ouémé River. In the context of climate change, where predictions indicate a probable increase or decrease in renewable water resources, the hydropower production could be affected.

Since 1958, Benin has been implementing a policy of seasonal reposition to rural feeder roads, first for the transportation of cash crops (maize-dominated) and second, for other agricultural products. In 2014, 43% of the national road network was considered to be in poor condition whereas only 57% were in good or very good condition.

The current government has crafted an ambitious program for strengthening the country's transport infrastructure. The program aims at improving access to markets and reducing costs associated with production basins, reduction of transport costs to increase economic growth and reduce poverty.

Concerning ICT, data showed that 51.8% of Benin’s women have a mobile telephone compared to 79.3% of men in the same age group (2014). According to the National Strategy for Information and Communication Development Plan, there is an entrepreneurship promotion program for women involved in growth sectors and for young men and women in certain sectors of the digital economy and green economy. However, this program has not yet been implemented on a large scale. The government encourages women in semi-urban and rural areas to invest in this sector. The sector's inclusive development should entail specific measures to take this aspect into account.

### Sociocultural

Polygamy is the most widespread form of social organization in almost all of Benin’s ethnic groups. The continuation of cultural practices that are degrading, to women appears to be linked to the banalization of gender inequality and the lack of awareness among women about their rights (and/or their partner and/or spouse) for important decisions in their lives prevent their full empowerment. Despite the Code of Persons and Family which states that monogamous marriage is the only type of marriage recognized, polygamy remains widespread in Benin. According to a survey conducted by the Ministry of Population in 2014, two out of five women aged between 15 and 49 years old and in a relationship (38%) said they were living in a polygamous union and about one out of five men (22%) said they were polygamous.

### Policy and Legal Framework

Various legal and regulatory texts have been promulgated to promote and protect women’s rights, and gender equality among whom:

- The United Nations Charter adopted at 1945 enshrined the principle of gender equality.
- The Universal Declaration of Human Rights which enshrines the legal recognition of rights 10 December 1948.
- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (Optional Protocol) adopted on 3 September 1979.
- The Convention against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment. 26 June 1987.
- The Convention on the Rights of the Child 2 September 1990

Bénin has also ratified almost all the international instruments promoting women and gender. Despite a legal framework that is in place, there are still gaps between the letter and spirit of these texts and their application by different actors remain a challenge because of habits and resistance from socio-cultural models.

On this basis, several guidance documents have been prepared for women's promotion and gender, including the National Gender Promotion Policy, the National Policy for the Promotion of Women in Agriculture and the National Action Plan to combat Gender-Based violence.


Gender based violence

Statistics show that 76% of violence against women aged between 15 and 49 years of age is physical. 44% sexual, 86% verbal or psychological and 7.3% concerns genital mutilation[1]. Bénin has established a legal and institutional framework to combat gender-based violence. The national gender-based violence prevention center of 3 Integrated Center for GBV Survivors are available to support and care for GBV victims[2]. In order to achieve an effective change in mindsets and behaviors, the law must be further strengthened in this area and efforts must be made to raise awareness among the population.


Access to Finance

The financial inclusion has generally improved in the West African Economic and Monetary Union (UEMOA), standing at 0.020 in 2020 against 0.01 in 2019, on a scale of 0 to 1, according to the annual report delivered to the subject by the Central Bank of West African States (BCAC). The index stood at 0.447 in 2018 and 0.356 in 2017[3]. In Bénin, the financial inclusion rate is 29% for the rural population region. Account ownership in Benin increased overall, from 38 percent in 2017 to 49 percent in 2019.

According to the Global Findex report (inclusive 2021), the growth in account ownership since 2011 has not been spread equally throughout society. The young, the unemployed and those with low or no education, remain less likely than men, the rich, and the educated to have an account. This gender gap is also reflected by the fact that women are more often excluded from financial services.

The spread of mobile money accounts has created new opportunities to better serve women, poor people, and other groups who traditionally had been excluded from the formal financial system. Though this trend is positive, it does not necessarily mean a reduction in the gender gap for account ownership gap. In country like Benin there is a statistically significant gender gap for account ownership overall, including both financial inclusion and mobile money accounts.


Poverty

Monetary poverty has slightly declined from 40.1% in 2013 to 38.5% in 2019 (INEG). Note on Poverty 2019: In 2018, it was noted that men's average monthly income was one and a half times higher (CFAF 31,49) than women’s (CFAF 20.30) and that they spent more time than women on remunerated activities resulting in higher hourly incomes[4]. Monetary poverty is highest in rural areas [44,2%]. It was also noted

There had been an improvement in the living conditions of poor households, reflected in a 13-point drop in monetary poverty between 2015 (29.4%) and 2019 (24.1%). Although the improvement in monetary poverty was significant, it did not translate into an overall improvement in living standards or an improvement in their living conditions in 2019. The place of residence, gender, level of education of the household head are the determining factors on the level of household poverty[2]. Therefore, poverty affects women more than men and is higher among female-headed households.

In 2015, monetary poverty affected almost as many male-headed households (40%) as female headed households (39.7%). On the other hand, the incidence of non-monetary poverty is higher in female-headed households (68% compared to 55% for male-headed households). The difference between these two groups was even more pronounced in 2015 in households where the head had no education: it was 17 points higher than in those where the household head had completed secondary or higher levels of education. In contrast, the difference between women and men who had completed higher education is even more pronounced (37 points). The trend is even more pronounced in terms of non-monetary poverty where the differences are between 22 and 37 points higher between household heads with no education and those with secondary or higher levels of education.

Berin has a very low level of women’s participation in political office. From 1991 to date, women's presence in the National Assembly has never exceeded 10% (5 legislators out of 28). Following the most recent legislative elections (April 2019), only 6% of the National Assembly members were women. At the latest municipal elections in May 2020, despite the signing of an Equality Charter on February 24, 2020, only 3 out of 57 elected mayors are women (5%). In 2019, 8 out of 67 elected mayors were women. As regards executive power, women's participation in government was low (varying from 9% to 24%) between 1991 and 2017 according to data from the Government’s website[3]. In 2021, there are only 5 out of 23 ministers. I.e., however, it was observed that since 2011, women have occupied on increasing number of positions in public administration, especially in the areas of education and training, the Ministry of Civil Service and Labour, Trade and industry, and the digital economy. This is a major development since historically, they were mainly responsible for the social sectors such as education, social affairs and health.

According to the 2021 annual report on gender statistics in public administration in Berin published by the Ministry of the Civil Service and Administrative Reform[4], the proportion of women in positions of responsibility in public administration has increased from 35% in 2019 to 38.7% in 2021, with a slight increase for men (from 65% to 61%). Women hold 35% of ministerial positions and 40% of the positions of responsibility held by women, a percentage of 9.25%. The majority of these women occupy positions related to statutory jobs. 130 out of 178 positions in public administration are occupied by women (73%). Women hold only 6% of the positions of responsibility in the Ministry of Civil Service and Labour, Trade and Industry. Also, in the energy sector, only 10% of positions of responsibility are held by women.

# 4.2 Cameroon

## Demographics and human development

According to world-development-indicator databases, Cameroon’s population is estimated above 20 million people of which 49.98 percent is women. Its current population is expected to increase to 50 million by 2030 and then 89.62 million by 2099 Despite negative net migration, Cameroon's population growth rate is 2.5%, adding over 600,000 people to the population every year. Cameroon has a total fertility rate of 4.7 children per woman, which is above the replacement level of 2.1 and zero to 14 years old. Cameroon’s Human Development Index value for 2019 is 0.563—-which put the country in the medium human development category—positioning it at 133 out of 189 countries and territories in 1990, and 79th out of 189 countries and territories in 2019. The average number of years of schooling increased by 2.8 years and expected years of schooling increased by 4.1 years.

In Cameroon, 29.3 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women, and 32.7 percent of adult women work compared to 56.0 percent of men who work. The female-to-male ratio is 1:1.8 for female to male counterparts. For every 100 live births, 529.9 women die from pregnancy-related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 105.8 births per 1,000 women of ages 15-19.

## Education

The disparity between women and men in education has declined in general, but the gender gap is wider at the higher levels of education. According to the World Bank data, the primary net enrolment was 97% for boys and 87.7% for girls (for 2017); primary completion rate was 68.3% for boys and 62.35% for girls (for 2017). The secondary net enrolment was 45.5% for boys and 39.5% for girls (for 2017).

The National Gender Policy Document explains the reasons for the gender gap: some families do not value education as women’s roles and jobs would not create much income; poor families provide priority to boys’ education because they believe that girls will be married off early, and they need clothes and learning materials less; inculcates domestic allocation and agricultural work to girls; some girls are withdrawn from school due to early marriage or pregnancy; some families are reluctant to send girls to school fearing violence at school or on the way between home and schools; and the toilets for girls are inappropriate at school.

The enrolment rate increased after making the primary education free of charge. However, according to the World Bank data, the number of teachers in public schools is insufficient compared to the number of pupils and adequate learning materials are not available. The National Gender Policy Document explains that the ratio of female teachers to classrooms to decrease the number of pupils per class, increase by salary of teachers to motivate them, increase the number of new teachers, and distribution of basic teaching materials to schools.

The National Gender Policy Document explains that in the formal employment tradition, men accounted for 53% and women accounted for 47%. The ratio was 116 for men and 145 for women (in 2009). Women are concentrated in the traditional female skills such as homemaking, dressmaking, and secretary.


---
**Page 208**
---

# Employment

According to the Human development report 2020, female participation in the labour market is 71.1 percent compared to 83.1 for men in Cameroon. According to the National Gender Policy Document, people with disabilities are also discriminated against and face many challenges in accessing jobs for women. The public institutions aim to make women's share at least 30%. The higher are the positions, the smaller is the women's share. Women account for 44% of the lowest job rank (category D: non- skilled workers) but only 12% of the highest job ranks (category A: senior managers and directors). In the private sector, 1.8% of male workers and 0.7% of female workers are in managerial positions. The average monthly salary for men is 35,496 FCFA compared to 20,000 FCFA in the public sector. According the World Bank data, Cameroon's estimated annual Gross Domestic income per capita was 3,341 dollars for men and 2,264 dollars for women (about 68% of men in 2014). Regarding child labour, 8.8% of children aged between five and fourteen years old were working. The percentage of children in labour is 12.6% in the rural areas and 2.4% in the cities.


# Health

The latest World Bank data shows that, maternal mortality ratio slightly declined from 564 in 2014 to 529 in 2017. The proportion of pregnant women receiving prenatal care increased from 62.8% in 2014 to 63.8% in 2017. The maternal mortality ratio has decreased by about 10% over the same period. The reasons for the high maternal mortality ratio include late decisions of the patients to seek medical assistance, lack of access to health facilities, poor quality of care provided at the health facilities; and physical damage to young pregnant women and young mothers (National Gender Policy Document). The national average of contraceptive prevalence rate in 2018 was 19.3%. It is reported that the use of modern methods of contraception has increased from 5% in 2014 to 17% due to the contraceptive prevalence rate, and that women with lower educational attainment or income level tend to be reluctant to negotiate with the husband or partner for the use of contraceptives. Child health indicators during the three years (2016-2018) in the infant mortality rate reduced from 54 to 48.3; under-five mortality ratio reduced from 101.3 to 72.2.


# Energy, transport and telecommunications

According to the National gender policy document 2011-2020 there is a great need of resilient infrastructure in Cameroon. The rural areas generally lack good roads or have none at all. Motorcars and motorcycles are the most common means of transportation for people living in these areas. The flow of agricultural produce from rural to urban areas is thus hampered and women are unable to market their produce. They are compelled to carry crops over long distances or cram them onto bicycles, which they cannot afford. This makes it very difficult for them to do so; they receive heavy post-harvest losses and the country's food security is undermined.

World bank data (2019) showed that 43% of the population have access to electricity Despite the efforts made by the government in electrifying residential areas, in rural areas only 24% of households have electricity, against 93% in urban areas. In the Information and Communication Technologies (ICT) sector, it can be noted that in urban areas, women have taken ownership of ICTs through local social programs as Spopulation 100 000 women

# Socio-cultural

According to the "National Gender Policy Document 2011-2020" by the Ministry of Women's Empowerment and Family (Ministère de la Promotion de la Femme et de la Famille; MINPROFF), while gender equality is a priority, women still face significant challenges. The policy acknowledges that in most countries, there exists a traditional division of labour in which men take on productive and public roles while women take reproductive and domestic roles. Traditionally, women's life is mainly in the domestic sphere and they are responsible for household chores such as cooking, cleaning, childcare, and managing the family budget. Women also manage households in the country. Women marry early: about 50% of women get married for the first time by the age of 18. The average number of children per woman is high (4.9), reflecting the important role of women, and it leads to the high fertility rate; it was 4.5 in 2019 (World Bank data).

The National Gender Policy Document explains that the perception about the roles, attitudes and behaviors of men and women are different between urban areas and rural areas. In a country where time is limited, it points out differences between women in the cities and those in the rural areas, and between those with high educational attainment and only basic education. Women in the rural areas and those with low educational attainment tend to have a perception of gender division of labour that is more rigid than terms in the cities; in rural areas, but the perception of gender division of labour, in which a man is the main breadwinner and the woman takes domestic responsibilities, exists life in the rural areas. Women's roles are often seen as secondary or supplementary to those of men.

# Policy and Legal framework

The National Population Policy Decree in 2012 aims at universal primary education, improvement of literacy of men and women, and reduction of gender disparities in all areas in the economic and social development. The Poverty Reduction Strategy Paper (PRSP) in 2003 is engaged in the promotion of gender equality in all areas. The National Gender Policy Document 2011-2020 was developed based on the country's long-term development plan called "Cameron's long-term vision by 2035". The Vision 2035 aims at promoting women's empowerment and increasing their substantial participation of women and men in development.

There is an Inter-ministerial Committee of gender at the central level of the country, which is headed by the Prime Minister. It is responsible for overall coordination and supervision of implementation of the National Gender Policy. It holds an annual meeting. Under this Committee, there are three Technical Committees: one on the monitoring and evaluation of the implementation of the National Gender Policy; in collaboration with the gender focal points of the ministries, civil organizations and development organizations, the Technical Committee has discussions with the stakeholders to ensure that the policy is implemented effectively. The second committee is responsible for monitoring and evaluation of the implementation of the National Gender Policy. The gender focal points are responsible for gender mainstreaming of each ministry's policies and project. Cameron adopted several international conventions on women's rights in 2013, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Declaration on the Protection of Women and Children in Emergency and Armed Conflict adopted


---
**Page 210**
---

Page 206

In December 1974 which outlaw all forms of repression and cruel and inhumane treatment of women and children, including impalement, torture, shooting, mass arrest, collective punishments;

• The Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women of 20 December 1993, which commits countries to eliminate violence against women.

• ILO Convention No. 3 adopted in 1919 and ratified by Cameroon on 25 May 1970 on maternity protection; ILO Convention No.89 on Night Work of Women, revised on 9 June 1946;

• The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), adopted in 19 July 1954 which guarantees women their right to vote and be eligible in all elections without discrimination.

• The 1937 Convention on Nationality of Married Women, which grants the latter the power to take her husband's nationality.

• The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), adopted on 18 December 1979 and its Additional Protocol of 4 October 1999. The Convention obliges States to promote equality between men and women in all spheres of public life, including political participation.

• Resolution 1355 of the UN Security Council which provides an opportunity for women to participate in the prevention and resolution of conflicts. This resolution was supplemented by Resolution 1820 of 19 June 2008 which recognizes rape as a crime against humanity, whose perpetrators are prosecuted at the International Criminal Court.

Gender based violence

Since the adoption in 2011 of the national gender policy (2011-2020), the Cameroon's government has focused on promoting a fair and equal society between men and women with a view of ensuring sustainable development. The government has set itself several objectives, including those contained in the National Gender Policy (2011-2020) aims of contributing to the reduction by half, the prevalence of various forms of gender based violence by 2020. Overall, gender-based violence whether physical, sexual or emotional is not lessened and it is estimated that one out of three women in Cameroon has experienced some form of violence during her lifetime. This violence affects women much more than men, and are more witnessed in the Centre region (excluding Yaoundé). Specifically, physical violence against pregnant woman remains a concern, especially urban areas. According to the 2018 edition of the National Demographic and Health Survey, 35% of women aged between 15-49 years old have experienced some form of violence during their lifetime. The partner/spouse is the main perpetrator of this violence among both women and men. The percentage of girls who got married early stood at 12% in 2018. There still efforts to be made to promote the implementation of the Community Health Practices (CHPs), notably through community-based violence-in prevention and fighting against these practices.

Access to Finances

### Poverty

The country’s general poverty rate is estimated at 39%. Poverty has a strong regional dimension concentrated in the Far North, North, Adamoua and East regions. Cameroon's northern regions are situated in the Sahelian band, where communities are the most deprived as a result of years of successive droughts and floods. The poor are also characterized by their lack of access to basic services such as education, health care, water supply and sanitation. This situation is compounded by continuous cycles of shocks and stresses. Cameroon has a Gender Inequality Index value of 0.563, ranking it at the bottom among countries in Sub-Saharan Africa. Women spend an average of two hours more per week than men on unpaid domestic work. As these tasks require a significant investment of time each day, they are particularly affected by the lack of access to income-generating activities. This inequality is also reflected in the fact that women have less control over their own earnings and over household resources, partly explains why women are more affected by poverty. As a result, nearly 83.2% of these women cannot access credit for any purpose.

### Sherpa Participation in decision taking

It is generally observed that when women contribute to household income that they often participate in decision-making as regards to its management. The empowerment and participation in decision making also increases the likelihood of women’s involvement in family planning, especially if there is consensus between spouses on the management of household resources and an decisions regarding women's health care. According to the 2018 DHS result, in Cameroon women are less autonomous as compared with men when it comes to managing their own income. The proportion of women who control at least half of their earnings has increased from 35% in 2016 to 47% in 2018 compared to 2011 (25%). Moreover, the proportion of 15-49 year olds who own and use a bank account increases with the level of education. This proportion rises from 1 percent among women with no education to 43 percent among those with higher education. For men, the proportion increases from 2% to 54%.

In fact, the installation or extension of the electrical network in the country will allow access to rural areas favorable to the development of basic infrastructure including education and health. This will increase access to information and communication services especially for women.

At the local and community level, traditional gender languages assume that if women are disempowered (through lack of capacity and capability), they will not challenge male dominance. As at the administrative and political levels, women's participation in household decision-making is very low. Also, women have limited access to credit facilities and other resources for their economic activities from women in rural areas because of the mobilization of certain actors in the fight against gender inequality, however, this does not mean that women are not involved in these activities.

These are the posts of: Minister of Posts and Telecommunications of Cameroon; Managing Director of CAMTEL (Cameroonian Telecommunications Company) and Orang's Zone Director and Spokesperson for the Middle East and Africa zone.

# 4.3 Chad

## Demographics and human development

According to the World Development Indicators (WDI), in 2020 Chad's population was estimated at 16.42 million inhabitants of which 50.1 percent were women, 76.5 percent live in rural areas, and 48.5 percent are youth under 15 years of age. However, there are still significant gender disparities: it ranked 160th out of 162 on the Gender Inequality Index in 2019. Chad is also affected by internal and external population displacement, driven mainly by conflict, with an estimated 348,750 refugees (of which 55.5 percent are female) and 24.3 percent of school-age (WFP: Chad country strategic plan 2019-2023).

### Education

According to data from the 2019–2020 School Statistical Yearbook of the Ministry of National Education and Civic Movement (MNEPC), many provinces of the Sahel belt have the lowest basic education rates in Chad. These include Borkou (18.8 per cent), Ennedi-Ouest (24.6 per cent), Ennedi Est (46.6 per cent), Batha-Geneina (30.2 per cent), Hadjer-Lamis (32.1 per cent), Batna (34 per cent) and Wadi-Fira (48 per cent).

The highest enrollment rates for girls are recorded in the provinces of the city of Ndjamena (13.1 per cent), Logone Occidental (17.6 per cent), Mandoul (118.4 per cent), Mayo-Kebbi Ouest (114.7 per cent) and Mayo-Kebbi Est (109.5 per cent). The lowest enrollment rates are recorded in the provinces of Tibesti (73.8 per cent). According to the School Statistical Yearbook, the rate above 100 per cent means that there is a lack of capacity to accommodate students under current conditions, all six-year-old students in the primary first level.

Thus, according to the 2019-2020 Statistical Yearbook, out of 58 girls enrolled in primary school, barely 37 will complete their studies. The provinces with the lowest enrollment rates are Tibesti (46 per cent) and 24 per cent, which is a particularly worrying situation. Only the provinces of the city of Ndjamena (92.1 per cent), Mayo-Kebbi Ouest (77.3 per cent) and Logone Occidental (51.3 per cent) stand out.

### Employment

According to the World Bank, female participation in Chad's labour market is 63.3 percent compared to 77.5 for men. Women have restricted access to productive assets, credit, land ownership, and the opportunity to rent land. Land inheritance customs often discriminate against women, and men decide to use harvested crops and income. Only 23.3 percent of women take part in decision-making about agricultural production, while only 18.7 percent are involved in trade and commerce activities. Unemployment is higher among women – 24.7 percent compared to 18.7 percent for men – and the female unemployment rate is 56.9 percent.

The male labour force participation rate is 65 percent, remaining largely stable since the economic crisis, while the male rate steadily decreased from 80.5 percent in 2002 to 77.4 percent in 2016.


---
**Page 214**
---

Page 210

According to the results of the Health Development Assessment report the maternal mortality is estimated at 174 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2019 and only 24% of births were attended by health personnel whose birth aid is urban area and 13% in rural areas. Life expectancy at birth in Chad in 2019 was 55.7 years for women and 52.8 years for men. The average age at first marriage is 16.4 years (INE, 2019a: p.7). In 2013, the prevalence of HIV/AIDS among women in Chad was 1.5% compared to 0.5% among men and 2.8% for women (INEB, 2014b:p.77). The prevalence rate of HIV/AIDS in the general population aged 15-49 years fell from 3.3% in 2003 (National Empowerment Survey of 2003) to 1.8% in 2013 (Survey of measurement of reproductive health in the female population offering antiretroviral care (UNAIDS) and 1.5-1.9% (World Development Indicators 2013).

### Energy, transport, and telecommunications

The Food Security and Vulnerability Survey report in Chad (FVS 2000) report demonstrated that wood is still the primary fuel source for cooking by over 90 percent of households. This proportion is slightly higher in rural areas (73%) than 75% for urban areas. In rural areas, the burden of ensuring the household supply of wood and other firewood falls on women who are responsible for preparing meals and feeding their families. Women have to walk to find wood and thus, their time on this chore is in addition, using wood for cooking exposes women to risks of injury and respiratory and other health problems (République de Tchad, Document de Politique Publique 2017).

Chad has a road network of approximately 40,000 km. Due to its size, the density road traffic is low and highly variable across regions. The average speed on roads in Chad is only about 35 km/hour. In some areas, the distance between two villages can be more than 40km in the Sudanian zone. During the season rainy weather part of the road network becomes impassable which increases the cost of transport and further isolates certain regions.

In the Chadian context, education women are in science innovation and technology according to a survey by Internet Society Chad (2017).

### Socio-cultural

The division of household responsibility is still highly gender-biased: women are expected to take care of children and cooking while men are responsible for income generation. In many rural areas, family members have no access to land and property; customary practices are still in place and male-oriented. These realities mean that women have fewer opportunities to participate in income-generating activities. For instance, while they perform the majority of agricultural work, they do not own or control the resources necessary for production rather than commercial. This situation is evident in rural areas where women constitute up to 40 percent of the country’s population.

### Policy and Legal Framework

The Ministry of Social Affairs, National Solidarity, and Family oversee the implementation of gender policies at the national level. The main policies in this area are:

- Constitution adopted in 2005: recognises gender equality
- ITPA Labour Code
- National Population Policy and the 1995 Policy for the Integration of Women in Development
- Law N°38/97/RSF recognises that men and women should be granted equal employment opportunities.
- Law N°46/04/PM promotes the education of girls
- National Gender Policy (NGP) 2016


---
**Page 215**
---

The national development plan for 2017-2021 emphasizes social protection, gender-based violence prevention, economic empowerment and livelihood capacity strengthening, and formulating a national gender strategy (with) their counterparts in Chad.

The 2018 National Gender Policy outlines the Government's strategy to ensure that gender inequality and violence will be eradicated by 2030. Women are included in decision-making processes and natural resource management.

• systematically integrate the gender dimension at all levels: planning, budgeting, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of development strategies, policies, and programs;
• develop a communication strategy for changing mentalities and behaviors;
• promote equal and equitable access to essential social services and decision-making spheres.


| Gender based violence |
| --- |
| Gender-based violence and sexual violence are pervasive and are aggravated by conflict and displacement. ACCORD estimates that 40% of women in Chad have experienced physical or sexual violence, while intimate partner violence is estimated at 29% (DHS-MICS 2014-2015). The child marriage proportion stood at 87% and the female genital mutilation is estimated at 38%. In Chad, a large number of women and men support the practice of female genital mutilation. According to the World Bank, 96% of women in Chad believe that female genital mutilation is a requirement of their religion. This proportion decreases with the level of education of the woman: from 100% among women who have not completed primary school to only 35% for those who have reached the highest level of education (4E). |
| At the same time, almost 3 out of 10 women (29%) believe that the practice of female genital mutilation should be maintained. This proportion is higher among women with no education (39%) than among women who have reached the highest level of education (4E). |


Access to Finances

Chad has implemented three national strategies to promote access and usage of formal finance by the poor and women specifically in remote areas. These are the Shelf (Stratégie Nationale d'Accès à la Finance) in 2009, the PAFT (Programme d'Aide au la Finance Inclusive au Sahel) for the period 2010-2014, and the PAQUIF (Programmation pour l'Accès aux Services Financiers et l'Inclusion financière) from 2015 to 2019. The Shelf strategy was implemented in 37 communes of Chad between 2009 and 2014, while the PAFT strategy covered 68 communes.

However, the financial system in Chad remains one of the least inclusive in the region. The Global Finances report indicates that only 5% of women have access to formal finance compared with 37% for men. Only 1 out of 10 women has a bank account. According to the World Bank, only 11 percent and 5 percent of women own a mobile phone or make banking and a bank account, compared to 28 percent and 19 percent of men, respectively in 2021.


Poverty

In Chad women do not have access to the same work opportunities as men, or when they do, are more likely to work part-time. As a result, women are less productive and earn less than men. All this leads to substantial gender gaps in earnings and productivity, which decreases women's bargaining power and voice and their ability to negotiate their working pay. Twenty-three percent of Chadian households are headed by women.

# Unequal Participation in decision making

In Chad, the lack of a legal framework establishing parity or quotas makes it difficult for women to gain access to public office and political positions open for men. To increase women in politics, but it is hard to find women in decision-making positions such as the President or Secretary General of a party. With some exceptions, women are more present and garner support for participation especially during elections.

In charge of social affairs or the family, women should be noted that women are more active in civil society organizations. The Association of Women Lawyers of Chad in cooperation with the female caucus of the National Assembly has been working to increase the number of women in public office.

According to the National gender policy (2011), in Chadian communities, the perception of male and female roles is a basis of the unequal participation in decision making. Men take on roles of decision makers in public affairs, while women are responsible for family management and strengthening of social relations (baptisms, weddings, funerals, visits to parents and patients).

# 4.4 Côte d'Ivoire

## Demographics and human development

Côte d’Ivoire has an estimated population of 25.8 million in 2019 (31.7% being male and 68.3% female), and the sex ratio is 102 men for every 100 women. 70.2% of the population live in forest areas, and 49.5% in rural areas. In terms of electricity, 4,500 localities were electrified in 2017 compared to 2,800 in 2011, representing a growth rate of 61%. The fertility rate has remained high for the past decade, with an average of 4.6 children per woman, with differences between rural (5.0 children) and urban (3.4 children) areas. Côte d’Ivoire is one of the countries in Africa where the population growth rate is highest, and it ranks third in the world: poverty is declining sharply, from 44.3% in 2015 to 37.4% in 2016 (UNDP)[1]. Life expectancy in the country is on average 68 years for men and higher for women (55 years) than for men (52.7 years). Total mortality rate is 414 per 100,000 live births, while the birth rate for adolescent girls (15–19 years) is estimated at 132 per 1,000 live births over 2015-2020.

## Education

Although the government effort, the country strategy document (SCP 2018-2025), combined with the review country portfolio performance in 2016, December 2018, highlights that significant differences remain between men and women in terms of access to education, almost one woman for two (51%) and just over one for three men have completed primary school. Concerning secondary education, only half of girls complete upper secondary education: 33% of men have at least completed primary education, compared to 21% of women. Concerning university education, the proportion of women is 40%, compared to 58% of men who have completed higher education. The average length of schooling in 2019 was 4.2 years for women compared to 6.2 years for men.

According to the World Bank, in Côte d’Ivoire, the literacy rate for people over 15 years is 64%. 51.6% of women are concerned and that of men 68%, Only women hold 13.3 percent of parliamentary seats.

## Employment

According to the World Bank, women’s participation in labour is 48.2 percent compared to 65.3 for men in 2019. Women are more represented than men in the informal sector and in agriculture, which are the main economic activities. This gives rise to the need to give greater power to local institutions while at the same time building their managerial capacity to launch viable projects and to speed up the access of people of the local level to quality services. Côte d’Ivoire’s diverse regions bring important assets.

## Health

According to the health systems assessment for Côte d'Ivoire report (May 2020), communicable, maternal, neonatal, and nutritional diseases are the leading causes of disability and death in Côte d’Ivoire, representing 83 percent of the disease burden. down from 75 percent in 1995. Côte d'Ivoire’s strong urbanization rate has led

---
**Page 218**

Page 214

Introduction of unhealthy lifestyles has also led to a rise in the burden of noncommunicable diseases resulting from a dual burden of disease taxing an already fragile health system. Neonatal deaths, HIV/AIDS, and lower respiratory infections were the top three causes of disability-adjusted life years (DALYs) lost between 1990 and 2017; neonatal disorders, malaria, and malnutrition were the top three causes of disability-adjusted life years (DALYs) in 2017. Even though the disease burden has been transitioning toward more noncommunicable diseases, the majority of deaths are still caused by infectious diseases. The leading causes of death for children under five years old are neonatal disorders, HIV, lower respiratory infections, and malaria; HF, TB, and malaria continue to cause a significant proportion of deaths among adults aged 15–49.

Energy, transport and telecommunications

Côte d'Ivoire has four primary sources of energy: hydroelectricity, oil, natural gas, and biomass. Provision of primary energy in Côte d’Ivoire amounted to 11.1 million tons of equivalent (teq), of which biomasses (8%), crude oil (39%), natural gas (12%), and hydroelectricity (7%). Côte d'Ivoire has four hydro units (plants) with a total installed capacity of 504 MW, generating an average annual production of 6.5 billion kWh of electricity in 2018. The country had an installed capacity of 390 MW at the end of 2017, and it is expected to reach 504 MW by 2020. In 2017, Côte d’Ivoire produced 6,857 GWh of electricity, of which 87.2% were exported to neighboring countries (mainly Ghana). The country has a total installed capacity of 390 MW in 4 hydroelectric plants located in specified areas (2014). However, only an estimated 25% of households have effective access to electricity. Concerns about the sustainability of hydropower are increasing as the country faces water shortages and the source of energy consumed amounts to 70% of total energy consumption in the country. Households have reduced their domestic loads for cooking which are not energy efficient. Access to energy for productive uses remains limited.

According to Côte d’Ivoire’s 2019 and 2020 National Business Group, the country has made up its 486 km of roads including 357 km of paved roads (of which 10% are national roads) and 4.3 km of bridges. The extension of motorways and rehabilitation are underway to ensure better quality of roads and facilitate the movement of goods and people in the country. Côte d’Ivoire has a total length of 2,587 km of railway lines; it is one of the region’s leaders, in particular ranked 2nd West African transport frontier behind that of Lome. Since 2012, 1.7 billion Euros have been invested in the development port. The overall traffic of all ports of Abidjan was 38 million tons in 2019 (of which 45% are imports and 55% exports). The Port of Abidjan handled a total traffic of 2019: 16,991 ships and tons exports 34,567,840 tons. A second container terminal (TEC) of 1,100 linear meters was inaugurated in 2019. The Port of Abidjan is the largest port in West Africa in terms of capacity of transshipment and overall traffic volume. The port of San Pedro is the 2nd port of the country, located at the south-western coast of Côte d’Ivoire. It handles a total traffic of 10 million tons (of which 58% are imports of cocoa exported) and diversifies its activities with the export of other materials raw materials such as rubber, cotton oil or oil palm.

Policy and Legal Framework

In the international level, Côte d’Ivoire has ratified most international conventions, including:

• The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1995 has also participated in all the major meetings leading up to the Beijing conference. I

• The convention on environment, peace, and security in Africa.

• The Solemn Declaration of African Heads of State and Government on Gender Equality in Africa was adopted in July 2004.

The country has committed to implementing the recommendations of international and African conferences including those of Mexico (1986), Cairo (1994), Beijing (1995), the International Conference on Population and Development, ICPO, 1994), Beijing+5 (2000), the African Women’s Decade Program 2010–
