# Gender-based violence

According to the National Ministry of women, children and family, 3,405 cases of GBV were declared in 2020 (including 697 cases of sexual violence). The victims are mainly young girls aged between 18 and 24 years old. In physical assaults, its marital forces caused 2,119 deaths of resources, opportunities or services and 817 psychological or emotional abuse. Of these declared cases, 81.9% of the victims are women and 64.7% of sexual violence is targeted against children.

# Access to Finance

The Government of Ivory Coast adopted, in February 2020, a National Financial Education Program (PNEF). This program is centered around building the skills of Ivorian populations, in order to prepare them to access financial services and use them wisely. According to the Financial inclusion Insight (2018), women have markedly lower levels of financial literacy than men: 54% for women compared with 67% for men. Women have formed a formal institution and 2.5 times more likely to be informal non-bank financial institution than men.

# Poverty

The National Development Plan For 2018-2023, its strategic objectives include that in Côte d'Ivoire women affect both women (47.4% of them are poor) and men (45.5% of men are poor) even if the latter contributes slightly more to this poverty (30.4% of the poor are men and especially in Abidjan where 33.1% of the poor are men and only 28.9% of women). The average income of a woman is 57,600 CFA francs per month compared with an average household salary of $22.8. Poverty affects both households headed by a man (44.4%) and by a woman (43.5%) even if in Abidjan the poverty rate for women is higher than that of men (39% against 31%). Data from the National Institute of Statistics shows that the average income of women in Côte d'Ivoire is 39%, lower than that of men.

# Unequal Participation in decision taking

In Côte d'Ivoire, only 12% of parliamentary seats are held by women, following the March 2021 legislative elections. The Ivorian government has therefore decided to implement a series of measures with the Ivorian government and partners for the establishment of gender-sensitive socio-economic infrastructure and the participation of women in grassroots decision-making bodies and the establishment of lines of credit dedicated to women entrepreneurship, within the framework of the initiative to promote access to finance for women in Africa (AFAWA).

# 4.5 Djibouti

## Demographics and human development

The Republic of Djibouti’s population was estimated to 988,000 inhabitants in 2020, of whom 47.3% were women. The total fertility index dropped from 4.2 in 2000 to 2.6 in 2020 with an estimated population growth rate of 1.4%. The population is highly urbanised and very young. Djibouti City is home to 75% of the total population, making Djibouti the second most densely urbanised country in Africa after Angola. The median age was 18 years old (2020). The population aged between 15 and 64 years old represents 67.27% (of 47,385 of whom are women), while 8.97% is aged 55 years old and older (46,315 of whom are women). The median age is about 20 years old. Therefore, there is a high dependency ratio in the country.

The country is characterised by a high scale of internal migration and immigration as result of climatic changes resulting in recurrent flooding and droughts. This causes the nomadic or semi-nomadic population to move from their traditional areas to urban centres. In addition, people living in conflict-prone countries also leave their countries for economic, political or insecurity reasons due to conflicts or wars so people migrate from one country to another. As a result of this migration, net migration in the country dropped from 1.9 in 2015 to 1.2 in 2020.

According to the UNDP report the Human Development Index (HDI) score for Djibouti in 2019 was 0.524, which also placed the country in the low human development category and ranked it 164th out of 189 countries. The HDI score increased from 0.37 to 0.52 between 1995 and 2019.

Between 1995 and 2019, Djibouti’s HDI increased from 0.351 to 0.524, an increase of 49.3%. The average length of schooling for women was 6 years in 2019 compared with 7.8 years in 2000 and 8.8 years over the same period.

Despite this progress, Djibouti’s HDI score in 2019 remained below the average of the Sub-Saharan region (0.54). The country has a high proportion of young people: 63% are aged between 15 and 24 years old. Over half of all households were headed by women, with 78.5% of these being single-headed households representing 18.8%, 21.1% of which were in urban areas and 18.0% in rural areas (Human Development Report 2020).

## Education

In recent years, the Djibouti’s national education is seeing the number of students in general and technical secondary schools increase. However, this has not been accompanied by a significant reduction to perpetuate women's empowerment. Though the literacy rate has slowly rose from 43.7% in 2011 to 58.9% in 2016, it still remains low compared with other countries in Africa (from 58.8% in 2009 to 68.6% in 2017). This drop is due to the slow rising enrolment (net enrolment rate) (NER) from 65.7% in 2011 to 68.3% in 2017 and retention (GER) [from 68.8% in 2011 to 78.9% in 2017]. The impact of investment on the education sector has been limited, with a decline in the number of teachers per student from 35.4 in 2009 to 28.5 in 2016 and a decrease in the retention of rural girls (GER [from 21.9% in 2011 to 40.7% in 2017]), but this has not sufficiently affected girls' dropout rates. The failure to achieve parity in primary education has a side effect on the

# Employment

The steadily growing number of young people entering the labor market toughens competition in a context where the resources and assets of women/girls are unevenly distributed from the start. This situation is particularly acute for women who have completed secondary education (62% of women vs 38.75%) than for the role for women (from 68.6% to 43.4%), between 2003 and 2017. The major capital-intensive infrastructure projects implemented in Djibouti since independence have not been accompanied by a concerned women. The formal private sector employs 35% of women, mostly in trading and hospitality sectors. Women are underrepresented in the public sector (48%), especially in the informal sector, 74.3% of jobs are filled by men. The female employment rate/female population ratio remains at 15.6%, despite a labour market participation rate of 30%.

According to the World Bank (2021), Djibouti has made progress in terms of women: CIEs for 1142 businesses (15.3%) registered in the directory*. According to the AIBD country gender profile 2020, e-commerce, tourism and handicrafts are growth sectors in which women are encouraged to participate. But many obstacles remain: lack of access to credit, lack of information on market opportunities, financial obligations, cumbersome administrative procedures, failure to connect with the networks (international trade fairs, markets, regional and international business networks). Other constraints include: (i) limited access to land for agricultural activities, especially in rural areas; (ii) limited access to the formal and informal sectors, and (iii) access to transport making it impossible for women to travel primarily by public transport. The lack of infrastructure is also a major obstacle to women's access to basic infrastructure projects implemented or ongoing. The latter creates new jobs for nationals, e.g., 25,000 jobs during the construction of the electrified railway, 250,000 jobs planned for the new industrial free zone lane, 150 to 200 direct and indirect jobs for the construction of the new site power plant.**


# Health

Djibouti's health outcomes have improved in recent years. For example, between 1990 and 2016, Djibouti's life expectancy at birth increased by 10.4 years according to the human development report in 2020. But some underlying challenges remain and have been exacerbated by recent pandemics. The number of births has increased from 35,789 in 2016 to 38,000 in 2019. The maternal mortality rate (MMR) dropped by 239 points from 307 to 248/100,000 live births but remains very high compared with the regional average of 50/100,000 live births. The proportion of women who give birth in a health facility has increased from 6% (down from 29.7% in 2013), with no difference in incidence between girls and boys*. The lack of access to basic water, sanitation, and hygiene (WASH) services is another concern**. Only 47% of the rural population have access to improved drinking water sources compared with 85% for urban areas. Only 23% of pregnant women receive four or more antenatal care visits, and only 34% receive any form of postnatal care. Reducing

# Energy, Transport, and Telecommunications

In the energy sector, data from the Djibouti Household Survey (EDAM-4-S) shows that in 2017, 57% of households had access to electricity. Depending on the gender of the household head, the difference between urban and rural areas is significant: women-headed households have a lower rate of access than men-headed households. However, the difference is more pronounced when it comes to access to energy affecting women increases whenever it is combined with area of residence and socioeconomic status. The high cost of electricity automatically deprives poor households from having access to this service.

In urban areas, 80% of women-headed households have access to electricity compared to 95% for men-headed households. In rural areas, the difference between urban (70%) and rural (63%) households is even greater in 2017. Fewer women-headed households have access to it (8% compared to 41% for men). 5% of urban households have no access to lighting compared to 19% of rural households.

In terms of transport infrastructure, although there are no available data on the impacts of new ports and roads projects on women and men in terms of job and/or business opportunities. It is obvious that they may benefit from these infrastructures. The Djibouti Road Network (DNR) project, which was designed to strengthen the national economic integration. EDAM4-S data provides an overview of the major road infrastructure in Djibouti. The DNR project aims at improving access to roads and increasing access on them. One-third of households (33%) cover 5 to 10 kilometers to reach them. Under a third (30%) cover over 10 kilometers. To do so, only 17% of households use public transport. It is worth noting that women are more likely than men to have access to roads and transport: only 42% of women are adults (2018 country gender profile).

As a lending for six months major submarine fiber optic cable, Djibouti has an opportunity to expand its telecommunications network in order to ensure sustainable and progressive growth. According to the World Bank, only 1% of the population used fixed internet in 2020, and the digital gap remains large. Among the richest 25% of the population, 89% of households own at least one mobile phone, compared to a 15% share among the poorest 25%. The average number of mobile subscriptions per inhabitant represents over 12 % of per capita gross national income. As a result, Djibouti ranks 13th of 173 countries in terms of mobile penetration and 80th for fixed internet access.

Household inequalities to the detriment of those headed by women were identified in the area's internet access (25% compared to 25% for those headed by men), mobile phones (47% vs. 78%), and at least one PC (19% compared to 30%). Women-headed households are more likely than men-headed households not have the same opportunities for internet access (25% compared to 23%), mobile phones (20% compared to 80%) or at least one PC device (22% compared to 60%). Despite the "No women left behind" policy, gender inequalities remain in rural areas; the digital divide still marks by gender and area of residence-related inequalities.

# Socio-cultural

Men play a predominant role in traditional Djiboutian society which is patrilineal, patrilocal and virilocal. Men provide household resources by breeding cattle and camels, which requires large transportable herds. Women, on the other hand, are responsible for agricultural activities and family leaders, if necessary. Women, on the other hand, tend to the sheep, goats, perform domestic chores and care for the children. They await the return of the men to the settlements or migrate to the towns. Many women in Djibouti have no access to land ownership.

Legal framework

Djibouti's Constitution of 1992 enshrines the principle of non-discrimination based in particular on gender (Art.1), by guaranteeing the equality of all before the law. Djibouti has ratified most of the international treaties on human rights and gender equality (GE). Under its national legislation, it has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) (1986). However, it expressed reservations regarding abortion in the case of rape and incest (art. 2 Par.2) in the Protocol to this Act. Djibouti is also a party to the Optional Protocol to CEDAW (OP/CEDAW/1995). Djibouti has not ratified Convention 189 concerning decent work for domestic workers nor the 1999 CEDAW Optional Protocols. On the other hand, it subscribed to the post-2015 Global Agenda on gender equality.

The Djiboutian government has adopted a National Action Plan (NAP) in favour of women and girls for gender; the PNO aims "to contribute to the achievement of gender equality and equity in favour of girls and boys, men and women in all areas of economic and social life". Its two overall objectives are: the eradication of all forms of discrimination against women and girls and achieving equality and the effective mainstreaming of gender in all sectors – focusing on gender-sensitive budgeting.

## Gender based violence

In Djibouti, the most common forms of GBV include domestic violence, female genital mutilation (FGM)/cutting and, to a lesser extent, rape. Although government action has FGM, the practice remains widespread in Djibouti with a prevalence rate among women aged 15 to 49 of 70-83%.

### Access to Finance

Women represent 70% of microcredit recipients in Djibouti; however, access to financial services is a common challenge for entrepreneurs in the formal and informal sectors. From 2010 to 2015, the number of women benefiting from the services of microfinance institutions (MFI) increased 15-fold (from 800 to 12,300) and the amount awarded ninefold (DFID $7 million to DJF 60 million). Available documentation suggests that women are more likely than men to be involved in informal or family-based income-generating activities. On the other hand, their main rejections to MFIs are their low penetration rate and note high credit-granting conditions; limited amount, short term and without any grace period, in solidarity groups

# Poverty

According to the 2019 Poverty Assessment, using data from the 2017 Household Survey, 17% of the population lived in extreme poverty, with less than US$1.90 per day (in 2011 purchasing power parity terms). Disaggregated data by sex showed that, from 2013 to 2017, a six-point reduction was observed for women and an eight-point increase for men.

# Unequal Participation in decision making

In Djibouti, the overall women's representation rate in the decision-making spheres was 15.9% in the 2006-2011 period compared to 23.5% in 2012-2017 representing an 11 percentage point increase with small differences for elected and appointed positions at the national level.

The Law establishing minimum representation quotas for eligible posts following the legislative elections produced tangible results. Women have filled 26% of the seats since February 2018 compared to a rate of only 5% in 2017. The number of women in ministerial positions has increased from 34% to 49%. The representation of women officials rose from 20% to 32%. On the other hand, the proportion of women at Director level remained at 27%, though their number increased (46/169).

Regarding local government bodies, in the 58 communes and nine (11) municipal councils and nine (9) regional councilors, women represent 9% of regional councils and 10.3% of elected municipal officials. Indeed, in the Republic of Djibouti, women are absent from the negotiation tables at all levels.

In addition to their limited representation in decision-making positions, women face several obstacles that prevent them from being present more than those of men. As in all essential sectors such as infrastructure, women face severe obstacles to accessing positions of responsibility that give them access to decision-making power. For example, women are not represented at the level of project management for rehabilitation or installation projects are empowering women affected by energy and transport projects.

The report of the study on the evolution of the situation of women in Djibouti 2000 to 2018 mentions that women are increasingly independent, and that they are solicited to settle conflicts within their neighborhoods, families, communities and even within couples. They are increasingly involved in the development of their regions and their countries. However, their access to strategic positions and key sectors remains very limited compared to men.

# 4.6 Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC)

## Demographics and human development

The Democratic Republic of Congo’s (DRC) population is estimated at around 80 million people, the largest and most populous country in Africa. Women represent 52% of the people, and 33% of the female population is under 15 years old. The DRC has a population growth rate of 3.3%, an HDI ranking of 175 out of 189 (in 2016), and one of the highest fertility rates in Africa at 4.8 children per woman, with 40% of women aged 15–19 giving birth. This denotes high gender inequality reflecting a discriminatory family code, inequity in physical integrity, limited access to resources and assets, and lack of freedom as a citizen. The Gender Development Index for the DRC is 0.383, placing women’s human development at about 65% of that of men. (Ghana Gold Program, April 2019)

## Education

The DRC's constitution stipulates free obligatory education and the eradication of illiteracy in Articles 43 and 44. However, the country faces a gender gap in education due to early marriage and pregnancy, which leads to it. At 65% of the population aged between 15 and 24 years is economically active, but only 70% are employed. Women's access to education is particularly low, with only 38% having completed primary school and just 9% having completed secondary education. The reasons for girls not going to school include early marriage, household responsibilities, lack of resources, and the need to work in order to support their families. Girls also have to perform household tasks such as searching for water and firewood further adds an increased burden. Rape is a common problem among young women who are forced into prostitution or sold by their parents to public spaces—spurring girls at risk of harassment and assault. Sexual harassment from teachers and early marriage are further burdens because schools bar pregnant students from attending classes, and most girls in such situations end up dropping out of school.

## Employment

Women's labour force participation stands at 50%, however, women face more challenges than men. Women have less access to skilled jobs than men (35% in paid employment against 12% of men), and they tend to be marginalized in the informal sector. The female unemployment rate is 48%, compared with just 17% for men, and only 43.3% of the population aged between 15 and 24 years is economically active. Women are more likely than men to be unemployed (60%) or underemployed (39%), compared by unemployment than young men. Women's labour is mainly concentrated in subsistence agriculture (70%) and small-scale trade (28%). The DRC has a female-to-male ratio of 1:4, with the majority of women being self-employed. Of all salaried workers are women, the rural population accounts for 75% of the country's total population, 60% of whom work in the agriculture sector—the majority of the subsistence level (10%).

The wage gap between men and women is also significant. The DRC shows that significant gender gaps in the labor market undermine the country’s efforts to achieve inclusive economic growth. Indeed, the proportion of female workers who are employed as salaried employees is 25%, compared with 43% for male employment, compared to 23.9% of their male counterparts. Moreover, improving the level of education and skills of girls and women through the availability of basic infrastructure will improve their access to capital, their physical security and their autonomy.

## Health

In 2017, the maternal mortality rate was 473 per 100,000 live births. Around 20% of women die for reasons related to childbirth; this can be attributed to lack of access to medical treatment, poor health facilities, lack of lighting in rural areas, and limited access to safe water. The DRC has a high prevalence of HIV/AIDS among young people: 15–19 years old when they had their first


---
**Page 227**
---

# Energy, transport, and telecommunications

Limited access to resources and assets increases women’s energy poverty – and women as both consumers and suppliers of energy are disproportionately affected by these constraints. This is true not only in terms of their access but also in terms of disproportionate access and consumption levels. The country's 9% electrification rate is one of the lowest globally, Urban electrification is at 27%, while rural areas have an access rate less than 1%.

Infrastructure development has been a priority for the Government of the Democratic Republic of Congo. Considerable efforts are being made for the rehabilitation and modernization of the country’s basic infrastructure in order to accelerate the country's economic integration, the DRC has several assets in the infrastructure sector including:

- 238 km of railways
- 5,033 km of railway lines
- 500 landing strips, 270 airfields, including (10) open to public traffic; 164 private airfields and 5 international airports:
- 25,000 km of runways
- 16,238 km of waterways
- 2,513 km line of train (1.965 kg/m)
- 89 hydropoweric plants and other renewable resources.

In term of ICT initiatives, the Hostilebit and We Are Social’s Digital 2020 Report revealed that the DRC had on Internet penetration rate of 17% for a population of 10.3 million people. There is 14.36 million internet users.


# Socio-cultural

In DRC women's access to resources and assets are restricted by gender norms—for example, assets obtained within marriage are considered the property of the husband, and women are not allowed to inherit or bequeath land and brothers. Legally, the husband administers the marital property, and women and men do not have equal ownership rights over marital property. Women can inherit according to the law but they cannot own or manage it due to prevailing social norms. In addition, there is a lack of legal protection for women’s property rights, and also prohibition on discrimination based on marital status in access to credit. Women, especially those who are married, have limited access to land and other productive resources. The majority of the rural population is female labour force—their best land still is in the hands of men. Women also lack access to new technology—mostly because they do not own or control assets that would allow them to purchase it. This limits their ability to engage agriculture to their husbands and cannot make productive investments for productive assets, such as improved irrigation systems, livestock, and other agricultural inputs. In addition, women are often responsible for the unpaid work burden in rural areas where they have to perform household tasks such as searching for water and firewood (unpaid domestic work). These household tasks are sometimes performed in the dark due to limited lighting at home and public spaces—putting girls at risk of harassment and attack.


# Legal framework

The key gender-related policies and the other sub-regional and international legal instruments on gender and human rights in DRC include:

- National Gender Policy (2017-2031).
- SADC Memorandum of Understanding on Gender and Development.


---
**Page 228**
---

Page 224

* Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women;
* Maputo Protocol (The Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa);
* UN Convention on the Rights of the Child;
* Universal Declaration of Human Rights;
* UN Security Council Resolution on Women, Peace, and Security (UNSCR 1325).

Gender based violence

Over the past 20 years, the DRC has experienced multiple crises, including wars and intercommunity conflicts. In addition to these major events, there are also many cases of gender-based violence against women and girls, as well as sexual violence against men. In fact, GBV and serious violations against children's rights also remain major issues. In just nine months (January to September 2021), 47,975 cases of GBV were reported, 14 percent of which were women victims. The number of GBV cases is increasing every year, especially among young people and other vulnerable because during the conflict period in 2021, 1,261 children were victims of physical and sexual violence. In addition to the lack of access to justice for victims of GBV, there are also many obstacles to funding to combat GBV, which undermines the implementation of strategies that have been adopted to combat GBV.

Access to Finances

In the DRC, the "informal" sector is the main source of subsistence for the population, especially women. Since "formal" employment is dominated by men, particularly in the public sector. According to the 2016 report by the Financial Sector Deepening Trust (FSDT), only 35% of women have access to financial services compared to 48% of men.

Poverty

Heavily impacted by the war, the DRC remains a country greatly affected by poverty, particularly in rural areas. According to 2012 data from the World Bank, more than 77.25% of the population live below the poverty line, with 46% living on less than $1 a day**. According to World Bank estimates in 2019, 37% of its population, or 60 million people, are poor.

Unequal Participation in decision making

According to the Human Development Report 2020, In Congo (Democratic Republic of the), 12.0 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women. The issue of women's political participation is complex given the obstacles that women face in accessing education and employment opportunities. Women's representation in parliament increased from 5% to 9% between 2006 and 2018. In senatorial elections in 2018, the proportion of women elected was 19% compared to 4.6% in 2006. In the Government, there are 13% of women serving with the Ministry of Gender formed into a Ministry of State. Despite this positive development in terms of representation for women in these institutions, their political representation remains at least five times that of men.

In the country, women's participation in decision-making at the local level depends on the village or region of the county. Within CSOs, women were an integral part of civil society but sometimes remain frustrated in their work because the leadership was entirely made up of men who did not understand or take into account the specific needs and interests of women. Women are also underrepresented in decision-making processes. Despite the fact that the participation rate of women in the labor force is about 46.5% of the participation rate for men, women represent only a small proportion of decision-making bodies appointed in the transportation energy, telecommunications and heavy industry sectors.

# 4.7 Gabon

## Demographics and human development

Gabon is a small Central African country with a low population density and a youthful population, with an area of 267,668 km² and an estimated population of 2.5 million; it has 13 inhabitants per km². It is one of the least dense countries in the world. More than 40 percent of Gabonese are under the age of 15, and with an urban fertility rate of 2.6 children per woman and a birthrate of 37 births per thousand people, its population is growing. Though the youthful population can be an asset for the country's development, the benefits are questionable only if the economy can absorb them productively.

### Education

In Gabon, Law No. 2/2011 of 14 February 2011, on General Orientation of Education, Training, and Research Diplomas, in its article 3 states that education and training are mandatory in Gabon. Access is guaranteed to any young person. Gabonese or foreign residing in Gabon, aged 3-16. Significant efforts have been made in education regarding the number of students enrolled in school. The net enrollment rate has increased from 85 percent in 2004 to 92% in 2014. But the completion rate in primary seems to have decreased, from 62.83% in 2004 to 41% in 2014. The government has made efforts and has also made remuneration progress reaching to rate of 84.5% in 2012; including 92.4% for women and 84.8% for men.

According to the latest Demographic and Health Survey (EDS) of 2012, the net enrollment rate is 93.35 among boys aged 6-17 years old and 92.5 percent among girls in this age group. The gender gap in school attendance disparities remain practically non-existent in terms of access to primary education. However, Gabon is among the African countries where the proportion of children who do not attend school because they are sick or have other reasons for non-attendance at school, perceptible through the net enrollment rates, are probably, the poverty of the population.

According to the 2017 Gabonese Survey on Poverty Evaluation and Monitoring, 24.1 percent of women aged 15 years and older did not have education at all and 39.1 percent of those who have some education did not go beyond primary school. The proportion is even higher among men: 38.6% for those with no education and 40.7%, compared to 11.7 percent of women. Also, around 62 percent of employed women work in low-profit jobs, such as agriculture or trade, while only 59 percent of men are engaged in these activities. Among the unemployed population aged 15-49 years that said a husband has the right to beat his wife for some reasons; the rate reaches 59 percent among women with primary education at lower and 63 percent among those in the lowest welfare quintile, compared to only 28% of men.

### Employment

Women are very vulnerable in the labor market. First of all, 39.1% work for their own account, mainly in informal agriculture and trade jobs or in unpaid jobs, compared to 27.6% for men; then, unemployed women are about twice as many as men (50.8% against 24.8%). Finally, the gender gap is also present on the market work with an activity rate estimated at 43.3%, i.e., more than 15 points difference with that of men, which amounts to 49.6%. According to ILO data, in 2015, women's labor force participation in Gabon was 43%

## Health

Gaborone’s health sector is plagued by malaria, premature birth, acute respiratory infections, HIV and diabetes diseases, which are the leading causes of death among children under the age of five. The country has seen an increase in the prevalence of noncommunicable diseases, in particular cardiovascular disease, diabetes, renal failure, and cancer. These diseases are associated with a sedentary lifestyle and obesity, which is linked to high mortality rates. Through a 2008 health financing reform, Gaborone instituted a system of mandatory health insurance, funded by the national compulsory health insurance and social security fund; currently this fund covers 60% of the population.

## Energy, transport and telecommunications

According to the World development indicators, 95.8% of the Gabon population has access to electricity. This percentage is higher than that in South Africa (74%) and Nigeria (32%), but lower than that in Kenya and Mozambique. Gabon is one of the leading countries in oil exploration and production; however, the country has been facing declining oil revenues since 1986 due to a decline in global crude prices and a decrease in the share of Gabon’s country of production, with its revenues accounting for 45% of total government revenue and crude export accounting for nearly 74% of total export earnings. In 2013-58 Gaborone’s electricity supply is through several hydroelectric power plants, which are located in the northwestern part of the country near the border with Cameroon. Likewise, however new studies are underway to find more economical and efficient power generation facilities. So far, the country has not yet been able to meet its energy needs. According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA), nearly 90% of energy consumption is from biomass and waste (wood, charcoal, animal waste). The remaining 10% comes from oil, natural gas, and electricity. Gabon’s electricity demand is high; among residential and industrial consumers and frequent electricity outages are a major problem in the country. In addition to the lack of infrastructure for energy distribution, the main reason for this situation is that the country has not yet been able to meet its electricity needs. Amidst rising electricity demand among both residential and industrial consumers and frequent electricity outages, the government has been working on improving the country’s power supply system by primarily building hydropower plants and extending transmission lines. However, expansion of hydropower does not ease concerns regarding electricity reliability, as key business hubs in the country have previously experienced extended power outages.

The recent progress in the coverage of telecommunication services, concentrated around large cities Libreville, Port-Gentil, and Franceville, has been slow to reach rural areas. The government is working on extending these services into the remote rural areas, where it typically not economically viable for telecommunications operators to deploy and maintain telecommunications infrastructure; around 15 percent of the population did not have access to mobile telecommunication services in 2013-58. In addition, the country has been working on improving the ICT services (e.g., mobile banking, e-learning, e-health) etc. To these underserved areas as the economic and social development of Gabon is still lagging behind other countries in sub-Saharan Africa. According to the World Information Technology Report show that Gabon has yet to reap the benefits of emerging technologies and to

# Socio-cultural

In September 2021, Gabon adopted new laws designed to reduce the risk of violence against women and provide them with greater autonomy in their personal lives, including access to education, training, housing, land ownership, and access to more employment. Revisions to the country's 1972 Civil Code allow women to be the official head of household, where they live, and can own and manage property in the same way as men. They can also open a bank account without their husband's consent or signature. The law also allows women to inherit land and no longer be legally bound to the duty of wife obedience. Amendments to the Criminal Code protect women from discrimination based on gender in accessing credit.

## Policy and legal framework

The Gabon National Strategy for Equity, Equality and Gender (SNEG): adopted by the government in 2010; the development of the strategy was supported by UNDP and UNFPA. The SNEG is articulated around six fundamental challenges facing women and men in Gabon from a development perspective. These areas are:

- Adherence of all actors to the vision and objectives of gender equality and equity;
- Empowerment through improved productivity in the main sector in which women are found, or any other sector;
- Improving access to production support services;
- Improving access to social services;
- The promotion of equitable participation in the management of power, respect for rights and the suppression of violence;
- The redefinition of the new role of the ministry in charge of gender mainstreaming with regard to strategy issues.

Gabon also has several legal instruments among which:

- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women in 1982 of the CEDAW.
- The Resolution on the Rights of Women in Africa of the African Charter of Human Rights of Man and Peoples in 2011.

The Gabonese constitution guarantees "freedom of conscience, thought, opinion, expression, conviction and profession of religion" for all subjects. Article 34 of the Constitution states: "Article 34 states 'the equality of all citizens before the law, without distinction of origin, race, sex, opinion or religion'."

## Gender-based violence

The official statistics on gender-based violence available in Gabon comes from the DHS-2012. The data showed that 58% (97%) of women aged 15–49 years old said they had been subjected to acts of physical violence since the age of 15 or some point in their life and 22% in the 12 months preceding the survey, in 43% of cases, the perpetrator was a partner. The data also showed that 60% (97%) of women aged 15–49 years old said they had been victims of sexual violence during her life and 8% during the 12 months preceding the survey. Slightly more than

one in ten women (11%) said they had suffered acts of physical violence during pregnancy. Among women aged 15-49, who had suffered physically or only 43% sought help.

Access to Finances

In terms of financial services, Gabon gives all persons - male or female - equal legal right to access bank loans. Nevertheless, certain discriminatory practices persist in favoring women from equally accessing credit. Some banks require a co-signer for loans and others require that women be married before they can apply; this last requirement that husbands be notified. According to the latest data from the World Bank, 54% of women had a bank account at a financial institution in 2017 compared to 68% of men. Mobile money is helping women to access and use their accounts more formally.

Poverty

In Gabon, poverty among prevalent among women. According to the world Bank systematic country diagnostic report (2020), the poverty rate for women-headed households is 34 percent, compared to 32 percent for men headed households. This is due to a higher incidence of poverty among urban women-headed households (33.7 percent) than in rural areas (31.5%). Women-headed households are also more likely to be poor because they have about 10% less income. Women-headed households have less adult members, less education and work less in qualified jobs compared with men headed households. The gender gaps are particularly important among poor households.

Unequal Participation in decision taking

The Gabonese political landscape is marked by the presence of women in certain key positions (such as the presidency of the Senate, or the Prime minister's office) and previously the Liberative City hall but the latter remains largely underrepresented among the elected representatives of the Republic within the government. Indeed, at the national level, only 10% of the members of parliament are women. At the local level, women political officials are occupied by women and at the local level, only 13% of town halls are controlled by female mayors. Women have a very low representation in the electoral process for elections and for the highest political office, even respected in the appointment of implementing decrees.

Globally, at least 50% of women have access to administrative positions with community decision-making possibilities. In Gabon, women's participation is not only limited by their gender policy, but also by their social status and they have benefited from adequate protection of their human rights and have increased access to legal services. In fact, the economic power of women and girls throughout the country has increased by at least 30%.

# Gambia

## Demographics and human development

The population of the Gambia is estimated at 2,416,644 million, with an annual growth rate of 2.4% between 2015-2019. The average life expectancy is 63.8. About 37 percent of the population lives in rural areas and women constitute 51 percent[6]. One in five households is headed by a female, mainly because of the migration of men to urban areas or abroad for work.

The Gambia has one of the lowest levels of human development in Africa, with low levels of education, poor health status, high infant mortality rates, and limited access to economic resources. The absence of economic opportunities, inadequate access to economic resources, including credit, land ownership, skills, and support services, About 67 percent of the population aged below 20 years (2009 National Youth Survey) are not in education, employment or training. Factors such as poverty, lack of decision-making power; low level of education, and lack of or limited technical knowledge, are also vital issues affecting youths.

In 2018, the Gambia had a female literacy rate of 45 percent compared to male literacy rates of 67 percent, putting it at one of the lowest of 19 countries and territories. Gambia has a GDP of $0.820 and this ranked 130th out of 182 countries in the 2019 Index. In the Gambia, 10.3 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women.

## Education

Gambian young women lag the young men with an literacy rate of around 50 percent. According to the Human Development Report 2019, 36.7 percent of adult women have reached at least a secondary level of education compared to 48.4 percent of their male counterparts.

Education in the Gambia is less inclusive as children with disabilities have limited access to education due to social and economic barriers such as poverty, lack of community and family values, social norms and values set limits on the education of boys and girls. Already from secondary school onward, girl's education is compromised by early marriage, pregnancy, and childbearing, which are major factors in limiting women's development. In 2017, the upper secondary completion rate differentiated by sex and age was in favour of men or male counterparts. The female literacy rate is 45 percent compared to a male literacy rate of 67% in secondary education[8]. In recent years, efforts have been made to promote gender equality; education for all, and women's empowerment. However, the employment status of Gambian women with access to good employment is very low compared to their male counterparts.

## Employment

Women make up 78 percent of the economically active population who work in agriculture compared to 51 percent of men[9]. Women are also more likely than men to be involved in subsistence farming, which accounts for about 40 percent of the total agricultural production in the country. Female participation in the labour market is at 37.7 percent and this is lower than male participation (62%). The Gambia has a low level of female participation in agriculture compared with men, and they make a massive contribution. It does not award them the improved social status or economic benefits that are associated with male farmers. Women's limited access to land, credit, and modern agricultural equipment coupled with their relatively low levels of productivity owing to limited control and ownership of productive resources such as land, inputs, credit, and machinery, results in women having lower incomes than men[10]. Despite the fact that women earn relatively good revenues, this income is often invested in supporting the household. Their limited capacity and skills to embark on viable agro-based and entrepreneurial activities, lack of ownership and control over resources such as land and modern agricultural equipment, coupled with the triple roles of women, impedes all efforts for rural

### Legal and Policy Framework

The Government has taken concrete policy decisions and actions related to gender and women in the past two decades. The first significant step was the establishment of the National Commission for Women's Affairs (NCWA) and NWC in 1996, established the Ministry of Women's Affairs in 1998, and created the Federation of Gambian Women's Associations (FGWA). The government has also developed and implemented many national policies to address gender issues. It endorsed the CEDAW in 1992. The country developed its first national policy on women, the 1999-2009 National Women Policy (NAPW). The 2010–2020 Gender Equality Strategy (NGES) was developed and implemented by the Ministry of Women's Affairs and Empowerment Strategic Plan. (Plan 2015–2020). The GDEP was developed through a consultative process informed by community-based participatory research, with input from women’s organizations and other stakeholders.

Furthermore, to put gender equality and women empowerment on a sound legal footing, the Government enacted several laws in recent years. These include the Sexual Offences Act 2013; the Domestic Violence Act 2013; the Women's Act 2013; the Traffic in Persons Act 2005; The Children Act 2006 etc. Women are not represented in the management and decision-making bodies of most of the country’s higher education and research institutions.

### Gender-based violence

Sexual and gender-based violence is a deeply rooted evil in Gambian society and is fueled by a culture or silence. Indeed, approximately one in three (33%) experiences sexual or physical violence in her lifetime. While the majority of women who experience sexual violence are under 18 years old, the majority of those experiencing physical violence are over 45 years old. The forms of violence experienced by them are in the form of rape and other forms of sexual abuse, early marriage. To curb the phenomenon, the government has enacted several laws to address gender-based violence. However, the government has posed laws against sexual and gender-based violence. However, there is a form of impunity due to the silence, stigmatization and fear of both the victims and those around them.

According to the 2019 Demographic Health Survey (DHS), 58% of women aged 15–49 years old have experienced physical violence while, 95% of the age same group have experienced sexual violence. In the Gambia, the most common forms of gender-based violence are domestic violence, sexual violence, and forced marriage. The issue that transcends geographic and cultural barriers. Between 2010-2018, 39% of married women experienced domestic violence, either physical, sexual, or emotional, from their husbands.

On the other hand, there is a lack of awareness about the consequences of gender-based violence. These include the Sexual Offences Act 2013; the Domestic Violence Act 2013; the Women's Act 2013; the Trafficking in Persons Act 2005; The Children Act 2006 etc. Women are not represented in the management and decision-making bodies of most of the country’s higher education and research institutions.

Access to Finances

For all countries, financial inclusion is seen as a key indicator of development and citizen well-being. Its effective implementation can help reduce poverty, gender inequality, and low literacy rates. By 2011, 49% of the total population, or more than 1.2 million people, will be living below the poverty line. Implementing the financial inclusion strategy is therefore a crucial task for the Gambian government, most of whom are unbanked, especially since about 37% of the Gambian population lives in rural areas.

The Republic of the Gambia (RtG) received financial support from the Alliance for Financial Inclusion (AFI) and the European Union. However, according to the final report of the Poirier Survey on Assessing Women's Economic Empowerment in the Republic of the Gambia, 2019, women are more disadvantaged than men by access to financial services. According to the final report of the Poirier Survey on Assessing Women's Economic Empowerment in the Republic of the Gambia, 2019, women are more disadvantaged than men by access to financial services.

Poverty

The Gambia is a country that relies heavily on agriculture, yet the country is subject to extreme weather events such as droughts and heavy rainfall. Agriculture accounts for about 35% of GDP in the Republic of the Gambia and is the main source of food for the majority of Gambians, especially in rural areas. However, the climate, which is characterized by a long dry season followed by a short rainy season, makes it difficult to feed families hungry. The long periods of drought in 2017 caused agricultural production to drop by 25% at the same time. Fluctuating market prices have adversely affected vulnerable households, who spend the majority of their income on food and other basic needs.

In 2015, the incidence of extreme poverty in the Gambia, as measured by the international poverty line of USD $1.90 a day or less, was estimated at about 43% of the total population. Poverty rates are higher in rural areas, where the population represented more than 41% of the total population.

### Sheqaf Participation in decision taking

In the Gambia, women have experienced limited access to participation and representation in the country's national governance. They have been poorly represented in positions of power and decision-making in the civil service, public institutions, and private sector. Women are not allowed to hold any position as a temporary special measure by any body: agency, public institution, authority or private enterprise to accede to their right to participate equally with men in all spheres of life.

The government has taken steps towards increasing women's representation in national governance as well as in private institutions. As early as 2012, the African Center for Democracy and Human Rights Studies (ACDHRS) conducted a study on the representation of women in national governance. The study found that while there is some representation of women in national governance, Women make up more than half of the population of the Gambia, yet they represent only 9% of parliamentarians in 2021.

However, women's candidacy and participation are still low in all sectors and areas of infrastructure in the Gambia. According to a study on the analysis of the energy and gender situation in ECOWAS member states, women's representation is very low in all spheres of life (political, economic, social, cultural, and professional qualification). This is because most people believe that women's role as wives and mothers may interfere with their ability to participate fully in public life. Women are also less likely than men to hold leadership positions which require long working hours or long trips to the field.

# 4.9 Ghana

## Demographics and human development

Located on the coast of the Gulf of Guinea, Ghana’s landscape consists mainly of plains and lowlands covered by savanna-forests in the west and Lake Volta in the east. With a total population of over 31 million by 2021, Ghana has an annual population growth rate of 2.10%. In addition, the urban population represents more than 58% of the total population with an annual growth rate of 3.2% in 2021. The country’s dependency ratio is 47%, which is lower compared to the average for sub-Saharan Africa (69%). However, the role of the inactive population to the active population is very low compared to the average for sub-Saharan Africa. Instead, the proportion of the population aged between age in Ghana in 2021 is only 35%. The country’s Human Development Index (HDI) is higher than the average human development index for all-Saharan Africa. With a score of 0.648, it ranks among the countries with high HDI and is one of the top 10 countries in sub-Saharan Africa. Ghana's HDI is higher than the average human development index for all-Saharan Africa. With a score of 0.648, it ranks among the countries with high HDI and is one of the top 10 countries in sub-Saharan Africa.

## Education

Ghana has made impressive strides in economic growth, poverty reduction, and educational improvement. Indeed, in recent years, the country has adopted approaches to make quality education accessible to more children. According to the World Bank’s 2019 data, Ghana's elementary school enrollment rate (Net N) was 86.1% and slightly lower in 2021 with 82.45%. However, the country's educational system still faces challenges such as a high gender gap in education, which is higher between the southern and northern regions of Ghana. First in 2016, the adult literacy rate was 79.4%, with 3% more men literate than women. In 2021, the adult literacy rate increased to 85%. The proportion of men over the age of 15 who are literate is compared to 47% of women and 76% of men between the ages of 45 and 49. At all levels, the literacy rate for boys is systematically higher than for girls, however, Ghana is doing well in terms of gender parity at secondary level with a ratio of 102%. In addition to this, the gross enrollment rate in women primary education is 108% in 2018. For this indicator, the country is in the 72nd percentile compared to all countries, meaning that 72% of countries have a lower gross


---
**Page 240**
---

Page 236

### Employment

Although Ghana’s female enrolment rate is higher than that of some Sub-Saharan African countries and also has enormous economic potential, the country has large gender disparities in economic participation. The female labour force participation rate was estimated at only 41 percent in 2017 (World Bank, 2021). According to 2019 International Labour Organization (ILO) estimate data, only 38% of women are economically active compared with 65% for men. The low female labour force participation rate of women in the labor market is rooted in societal norms and also prevalent cultural factors that view women as complementary to men whose role was limited to the Home.

The gender pay gap in Ghana’s power sector, for all employed women in Ghana was estimated at 87.1 percent, compared to 63.7 percent for men. For non-agricultural employment, the roles for women and men were 54.8 percent and 49.3 percent.

### Health

With five levels of precedence, Ghana’s health system includes health posts, the first level of primary care in rural areas; health centres and clinics; district hospitals; regional hospitals; and tertiary hospitals. On average, Ghana spends about 4 percent of its GDP on health infrastructure. Ghana has long placed special emphasis on the country's health sector. In this year, the sector received more than $102,230,000 USD, which is a significant increase from previous years. The government allocated 5% of the national budget. Ghana has one government agency: the Ghana Health Service (GHS) established in 1976 as part of the Ministry of Health and Sanitation. Despite this, the majority of Ghanaians still rely on private health services for their care.

A 2019 study found that 81.45% of the population had access to primary health care in Ghana, while only 41.64% had access to secondary level care and just 14.3% to tertiary care. This while the number of specialized facilities has increased over time, the majority of Ghanaians still do not have access to these services. The population has to travel long distances to access primary facilities or consult a specialist. The gender situation with regard to access to health care remains uncritical[6]. However, according to a study conducted in 2017, the majority of women aged 15 to 19 (38%) encountered at least one barrier to

Thanks to these hydroelectric dams and solar and thermal power plants, Ghana has one of the highest electrification rates in Sub-Saharan Africa (84%). However, access to energy sources remains low. Ghana’s national grid is connected to only about half of its population, with an installed capacity of 10,352 MW (3,300 MW or 9%) with a total installed capacity of 47,000 MWh considered reliable.

With a population of 31.7 million spread over a geographically heterogeneous terrain, Ghana’s transport infrastructure plays a major role in the country's economic growth, especially since the country has no natural harbors and relies on its ports for imports (mainly from Accra, Tema, and Takoradi) and to facilitate better mobility of goods and people but also to serve the remote rural areas. Ghana’s transport infrastructure plays a major role in the country's economic growth, especially since the country has no natural harbors and relies on its ports for imports (mainly from Accra, Tema, and Takoradi) and to facilitate better mobility of goods and people but also to serve the remote rural areas.

The rail sector is becoming increasingly important due to key investments made between 2015 and 2016 in the rehabilitation and modernization of western and suburban lines. The railway network has a total length of about 3,487 km (of which 9% are electrified) and does the eastern line, which focuses on passenger traffic between Accra–Niwem and Accra-Tema.

The air and maritime transport sectors remain the country’s key sectors in terms of the quality and safety of passengers and cargo. The country has two international airports (Kotoka International Airport for imports and exports) and two ports (Tema port focused on imports and Takoradi port focused on exports), with two international airports, Ghana's transport sector has contributed significantly to the country’s economic growth over the past decade. In 2016, the total value of goods imported through Ghana’s ports was estimated at $53 billion dollars or 12.8% of Ghana’s GDP%. Nevertheless, the sector is male-dominated and inequalities exist in access between rural and urban areas.

With a population of about 31 million people, compared to other low-income countries in Africa, Ghana has adopted institutional reforms in all key infrastructure sectors of the country. The ICT sector is no exception. Indeed, the country has created an organization to promote the ICT sector, namely the National Communications Authority (NCA). ### Socio-cultural

Transportation in Ghana is by road, rail, air and water. Ghana’s transport and communication networks are dominated by road transport and concentrated mostly in the south. The country seeks to improve the mobility of goods and passengers throughout the country by reducing travel time and vehicle operating costs.

## Policy and Legal framework

In Ghana, the 1992 constitution provides for a multiparty republic with a president as head of state and a vice president. There is a Supreme Court at the apex of the judiciary that has exclusive functions and an unicameral Parliament, whose members are directly elected for a four-year term. The country’s political system is hybrid indeed, in which the executive branch is dominated by the ruling party, the National Democratic Congress (NDC), with the central government. Over the years, this tendency has persisted and even expanded, and the institution of chieftaincy has become increasingly divorced from the exercise of real political power at all levels of government. The country’s legal system is based on English common law, but it also incorporates customary laws and English common law. The constitution: statutes enacted by Parliament; ordinances, rules and regulations made by any authority under the Constitution or any other law; and decisions of courts are the sources from which the laws that make up the legal system of Ghana. Ghana experienced political and economic stability during the period of NDC rule.

Ghana has passed laws and established policy guidelines to encourage foreign investment in the country. These include the Ghana Investment Promotion Centre (GIPC) Act [Act 651] the free Trade Zone Act 1993 [Act 5014], the Foreign Exchange Control Act, 2008 [Act 732], and the Companies Act of 2011. In addition to these laws, the country, in addition, the GIPC regulates both public and private enterprises in all aspects of the economy with a focus on infrastructure. Better yet, industries are governed by sector legislation. The country’s political and legal systems have been designed to ensure that foreign investors can operate freely within the framework in the country and protect foreign investment in the country; the Ghanaian government relies heavily upon application of the bilateral or multilateral investment agreements.

In April 2017, the Board of Directors approved the Ghana Infrastructure Investment Fund Investment Policy Statement (IP) to further strengthen the country’s openness to both achieving the Sustainable Development Goals and addressing climate change through investments in resilient infrastructure.

# Access to Finances

Ghana has a low level of retail financial inclusion and access to finance for businesses remains a major challenge. The main problems facing Ghana's financial sector are low account ownership and usage, predominance of cash, limited access points for transactions (Ghanas small and medium enterprises [SMEs] are anchored in the informal economy), and high transaction costs. These factors have contributed to GDP growth by reducing unemployment and promoting innovation and social welfare. However, these SMEs are severely limited in their access to capital as they play a crucial role in contributing to GDP growth by reducing unemployment and promoting innovation and social welfare. While more than 73% of adults live on incomes above the poverty line, Ghana's population still had a low utilization of financial services. However, access to financial services in Ghana is still higher than average across Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA). In 2015, 48% of adults in Ghana had an account at a bank or other financial institution, compared with the SSA average of 37%. Only 69% of women and men had access to a mobile banking account. The proportion of women who have a savings account in Ghana compares to 43 percent on average in sub-Saharan Africa and 39 percent had access to a mobile banking account compared to 21 percent on average on the continent.

# Poverty

Ghana has long achieved significant economic growth and poverty reduction. With an economy based largely on the mining of gold and cocoa, Ghana has been able to use its natural resources effectively in order to freely improve their crop yields and provide for their families. In 2018, the country was one of the ten fastest-growing countries in the world, and by 2021 it will be the second largest economy in West Africa. This economic growth has been accompanied by a reduction in poverty rates from 45% to 37%. The development of social infrastructure has enabled the country to make considerable progress in reducing poverty over the past 10 years. However, there are still significant challenges ahead and much work remains to be done in addressing inequalities.

## Unequal Participation in Decision Making

Ghana has adopted affirmative action measures reserving 40% of seats in decision-making bodies for women even though women make up nearly 50% of the country's population. In the national parliament, women represent only about 12%. Women are also underrepresented at all levels of government and in public service decision-making in Ghana. Of 275 seats in the lower and single houses after the 2020 parliamentary renewals, only 40 seats are held by women, representing 14.5% of women.

Ghana has made significant progress in increasing the number of women holding positions in the judiciary but they are underrepresented in the sector, especially in key or strategic positions. Through the development of the Gender and Social Inclusion Policy, Ghana is committed to ensuring that at least one-third of all women participate in the various activities in this sector. Indeed, this policy commits to having a workforce composed of 24.4% to 40% women by 2035. Currently only 11% of women hold management positions in companies in the sector, while the objective of this policy is to reach 40% of women in management positions by 2035.

# 4.10 Guinea

## Demographics and human development

The Guinea population is estimated to be 10,233,263 inhabitants according to the Third General Population and Housing Census (BGPHS) of 2014. According to World Development Indicators, it should be above 13 million in 2019. Made up of a majority of women (57%), its demography is characterized by a high rate of growth (2.9), and more than three-quarters of the population is under 35 (77%). Furthermore, according to the World Bank, 48% of Guineans are living below the poverty line (World Bank survey). 70% of Guinean households are male, and 21% are women. From the point of view of natural resources, Guinea is a country rich in minerals (iron ore, bauxite, gold, diamonds, oil, and gas), with large deposits in East Asia (large deposits of iron [billions tonnes], iron ores, copper, gold, diamond, and titanium). What makes Guinea soil and adds to its "geological paradox".

Despite the high rate of growth, the proportion of children under five years old living below the poverty line has dropped from 53.4% in 2011 to 48.7% in 2015 and 47.6% in 2019, which put the country in the low human development category—positioning it at 178 out of 189 countries and territories (Human Development Index). The proportion of children under five years old living below the poverty line is 61.4% in sub-Saharan Africa. The mean years of schooling to 2.8. According to the results of the ENICVA 2018/2019, the proportion of the population living below the national poverty line, i.e., with an annual per capita income lower than 35,000 FCFA (approximately $60), is 47% for men and 55.5% for women, those led by men, respectively 44.9% and 43.5%. The results show the high vulnerability of households headed by women to poverty. In addition, 28.1% of female heads of households are divorced/separated or widowed by others. On the indicator scale of ENICVA 2018/2019 report data showed that more than half of the poor in Guinea are women (53.6%).

## Education

Guinea has the lowest literacy rate, with 22% for women and 38% for men; although significant progress has been made in education, especially in primary school and in technical and vocational education, much remains to be done. In Guinea, education is compulsory only at the primary school level, with primary school enrollment in 2015 being 61% for boys and 49% for girls. Secondary and higher school enrollments are very low: 37% of boys and 28% of girls were enrolled in secondary schools; for higher secondary schools compared to 37% and 14% for men. Girls account for 46.1/100 of the total number of students, while boys represent 53.9%. The gender gap is particularly pronounced at the level of higher education: women are strongly underrepresented in tertiary education. Women account for 87% of learners in tertiary sector training and men represent only 13%.

Moreover, in 2012, public EIS had only 22.15 female students compared to 39.29% in private EIS. Much remains to be done in education to develop skills for the country's socioeconomic transformation. The gross enrollment rate (GER) of primary school increased from 68.7% in 2011 to 74.5% in 2017, significantly in 2015 (79.8%) due to the Ebola epidemic before gradually improving to 88.6% in 2017. The primary completion rate fell from 39.4% in 2016 to 32.2% in 2017. As for secondary schools, the enrollment rate increased from 50.5% in 2011 to 58.5% in 2017 and then decreased again to 57.4% in 2019 (75.6% for girls and a still worrying dropout rate (9.0% for the whole, 10.1% for girls, and 8.2% for boys). Guinea is facing low enrollment among women/girls. As the training curriculum progresses, there has been

Employment

According to the National Gender Policy Document 2017, the working-age population in Guinea is 2396244 of whom 54% are men and 46% are women. In this workforce, 48% of men and 72% of women work for themselves. Despite their demographic weight (31%), Guinean women make up only 1.7% of the formal labour force (public & private). Overall, women make up less than 30% of the total workforce in Guinea. Women are more likely to be self-employed and to work informally or most in the informal sector.

Salaried jobs generally occupy a small share of jobs in African countries. However, there jobs are the least precarious and most regular; they are the best remunerated and provide those who hold them with social security. According to ECHNA 2017 in Guinea, the proportion of employed persons in salaried jobs is 35%. The majority (64%) of these jobs are in the informal sector but can also be paid by commission on sales, or by other means remains very low. 13.35% do by sex show that the proportion of men who receive a salary among employment remains for superior to that of women.

The unemployment rate is higher among women (2.3%) than men (2%), it is the same trend in urban areas (3.4% against 4.4%) as in rural areas (0.8% and 0.5%).

Health

The Guinea DHS 2018 revealed that between 2012 and 2018 there was a 0.9% drop in the level of infant and child mortality, this is mainly due to the decline in mortality (1.4%) which decreased by 23%, during the same period. The two components of infant mortality (neonatal and postneonatal) remained almost constant during this period. Approximately 81% of women aged 15-49 who have had a live birth within the last five years received antenatal care from a health professional. In nearly half of these cases, are nurses or midwives who provided this care prenatal care (49%). Physicians provided care in 17% of cases and technical health workers (ATH) in 13% of cases. One in seven women gave birth to their child at home without any assistance from a health professional.

During DHS V, the HIV prevalence within the whole Guinean population fell aged 15-49 estimated at 12.8%. The number of new infections per year is estimated at 30000. However, the percentages of women and HIV-positive men increase with age. Very low at ages 15-19 and 20-24 years, the seroprevalence increases and reaches a maximum of 2.5% at 30-34 years in women and 6.8% at 35-39 years for men. The prevalence is therefore continuous by age with the peak of prevalence is therefore reached earlier in women than in men.

Energy, transport and telecommunications

The access rate to electricity is 44% (including many illegal connections). The access rate is 20% in rural areas compared to 87% in urban areas. According to Doing Business, the country fell in terms of electricity access in 2019. The government has announced a plan for the development of supply and transparency of tariffs. The electricity tariff does not cover operating expenses. Electricity


---

Page 243

is sold at a maximum price of 800 GNF (less than $0.1) per kWh, while the average cost is 2.700 GNF per kWh ($0.3).

Guinea is developing both the SEALALL Action Agenda and the Implementation Program in the framework of the S4All Initiative launched by the Partnership for Africa's Energy Future, coordinated by IREDAE. Before that, the main objective of the Government was to increase access with a 2012 Energy Policy document that envisaged an increase of the access rate to 50% in 2020, as well as improvements in energy efficiency, on increasing the share of renewable energies and reducing the cost of electricity. The target for the number of people connected to clean cooking by 2025 was also foreseen. These objectives are being revised in the Action Agenda.

Guinea has considerable renewable energy resources, particularly for hydropower, for which around 13% of the total installed capacity has been detected. The total energy demand in Guinea is projected considerably—and according to some estimates—by 2050 to be more than double what it was in 1838 and would be necessary by 2025.

Overall, there is a low per capita energy consumption level by any gender: about 300 kwh of electricity per year. The situation of women is characterized by a decline in the connection to electricity, low access to electricity, and a quasi-dominant use of coal as an energy source in rural areas. This is due to the lack of awareness of the benefits of alternative energy sources and innovative practices, firewood and charcoal remain predominant, affecting women more in their cooking meals [as mothers] than men.

Concerning the ICT use, the DHS data showed that during the 12 months that preceded the survey, 13% of women and 32% of men said they had used the Internet. Among these women and men, respectively, 50% and 64% reported having used it to access information on health or education. The strategic choice to "make ICT a locomotive of the economic and social development of Guinea," the authorities consider that the telecommunications and the digital economy as essential factors for the country's development. The Ministry of Posts, Telecommunications and Digital Economy (MPTDE) is at the heart of this strategy. The MPTDE falls within this perspective by relying on the six main levers for the development of the sector: [i] improving the energy supply; [ii] shaping infrastructures; [iii] strengthening regulations, through a "regulatory authority strong and effective regulation"; [iv] building the capacities of executives in the sector of telecommunications; and [v] the emergence of a telecommunication/ICT ecosystem.

Socio cultural

The Guinean population remains strongly influenced by ancestral customs that not only place the man at the head of the household but also grant him the male dominance over the woman100. Indeed, in most communities in Guinea, there are rules and customary practices that establish the distribution of roles and responsibilities between men and women. In addition to their role as breadwinners, women are often their husbands' brothers or fathers as follows "tabou". Due to the persistence of social and cultural factors and the inequalities they engender, women remain vulnerable and 60% of them are illiterate but also face violence of all kinds.

## Policy and Legal Framework

In 2007, the Ministry for the Promotion of Women, Children and the Family (MIPF) was created as the main government institution responsible for women’s empowerment and gender equality. It is responsible for formulating policies on behalf of the government to promote gender equality.

In 2010, this national policy sets the country's Vision, strategy, and priorities for strengthening gender equality in all areas of life. The vision is that every woman has equal opportunities to participate fully and equitably access to resources. One important component is to improve women’s access to appropriate technologies that are less costly and less reliant on wood energy.

The Gender and Development (GAD) policy was adopted by the government to address gender disparities in the Government, development agencies, and civil society to support the policy's implementation[4]. In 2019, the government decided to adopt a new Family Code to introduce improvements for women’s rights (inheritance, property, marriage, divorce). However, opposition from conservative groups forced the Government to withdraw the code. Two years later, a new code was adopted, resulting in several setbacks in terms of women's rights[5] (World Bank: Mali Gender Assessment).

### Gender-based violence

Gender-based violence (GBV) is a major international health problem that constitutes a threat to individuals’ physical and human dignity. As in many countries, the issue of gender-based violence is a major concern in Guinea. In fact, the phenomenon is becoming more and more recurrent, despite the efforts of women’s rights organizations and human-rights organizations. According to a report by the Ministry for Women, Children and Family (MIPF), 10% of women aged between 15-49 were victims of Guinean civil society organizations documented at least 32,021 cases of gender-based violence (GBV). Incidents include sexual assault, rape and domestic violence.

As mentioned in the section on women’s rights, social action against women and children has increased in recent years in certain regions of the country. The most widespread and recurrent cases are rape of minors, early marriages, physical abuse, and female circumcision. This violence again


---
**Page 249**
---

Page 245

women and vulnerable persons is as widespread in urban areas as in rural areas (although the prevalence rates of violence vary slightly according to ethnicity). In most parts of the country, the prevalence rate of violence against women is above 30%, with a national average of 61%. The highest prevalence rate of more than 70%: while the average for sub-Saharan Africa is 37%. However, the country has made significant progress in the fight against gender-based violence and all forms of inequality. This is reflected in the National Strategy to Fight Against Gender-Based Violence and the National Development Plan for the period 2014-2020 has been developed, including the reduction of social and gender inequality with a particular focus on the promotion of employment for women and girls.

In order to promote financial inclusion of women and youth, and to support their entrepreneurship activities, the country has adopted policies, plans and strategies to serve as a reference guide and achieve the objectives set. Thus, the country has developed the National Strategy to fight against gender-based violence: the National Action Plan for the Fight Against Gender-Based Violence (2014-2018) and the National Poverty Reduction Strategy III. The objective of the latter is to give special attention to the issue of "gender and equity" and in particular to promote the equalization of women in public life and in decision-making bodies in order to correct inequalities between men and women.

Access to financial services

The PROFOOD inclusion of vulnerable people and women is a major issue in most countries. This situation is also of concern in Guinea: where only 23% of the population is banked. Nevertheless, access to credit and financing is essential to enable vulnerable people and women to launch income-generating activity and escape poverty. To promote the financial inclusion of women and youth and their entrepreneurship activities, a National Strategy for Financial Inclusion was adopted by the government in 2014. The first actions taken for entrepreneurship and job creation have been conducted at the National Level.

To promote the financial inclusion of women and youth, studies and strategic plans on the promotion of financial inclusion were carried out. This is the case of the study conducted by the country in collaboration with the World Bank (WB) and the International Finance Corporation (IFC). The Ministry of Social Action and Women's Promotion, banks, microfinance institutions, NGOs and other related associations where a responsive development-oriented financial system was proposed to meet their needs.

However, in 2013, the country had created a decree "ANAMF" with the mandate to design, implement and evaluate the government policy on microfinance with the main objective of improving access to credit for women. In this context, the National Microfinance Fund (NMF) was created. The microfinance fund for women and youth was created, with 80 percent of the resources dedicated to microcredit and 20 percent to financing support activities (SA capacity building, etc.).
