The experience of ANAMIF and the government’s commitment to private sector reform, Guinea has created a Private Investment Promotion Agency (PIPA) whose mission is to implement the government’s strategy for private investment in the country. One of its main objectives is that “all men and women, especially the poor and vulnerable, have equal rights to economic resources and access to new technologies and adequate financial services, including microfinance.” Nevertheless, these various actions taken by the Guinean state reveal important challenges: the main obstacle for women’s inclusion in the economy is the lack of access to credit. The excluded groups have the digital and financial capacity to benefit from the advantages of digital finance.


The Republic of Guinea, despite its enormous natural and human potential, remains one of the poorest countries in the world, with a Human Development Index of 0.477 in 2019, ranking 178th out of 189 countries. The poverty rate is estimated at 53% for the population aged between 15 and 64 years old; that of poor people is estimated at 3,544 (Bn) in 2019. While the rural population represents more than 44% of the total population, it accounts for almost half of the country’s poor.


With a poverty rate of 44.5%, women remain the most vulnerable group in Guinea, compared to 42.8% for men. Although the difference is small, women remain the poorest segment of the population and are more likely than men to be living on less than $1.90 per day. Women also have a lower literacy rate among female-headed households, compared with their male counterparts[3]. More than 77% of the poor are young people under the age of 25. Although the depth and severity of poverty is greater for women in rural areas, it is more pronounced in urban areas where women depend on the most affected. Women thus remain the poorest, most vulnerable, and least endowed with the capacity to effectively assert their rights and interests in the obligations in which they are involved alongside men.


Gendered Participation in Decision Making


In recent years, Guinean populations have been affected by the negative impacts of climate change such as drought and flooding[4]. However, because of the different roles and responsibilities assigned to men and women, they feel the impact of climate change differently. Guinean culture relegates women to domestic tasks while men are responsible for agricultural activities in the fields, at work, and in politics. According to the 2013 report by the Ministry of State for the Economy and Finance, 52% of women are not considered on the same level as men in agricultural water management[5]. The needs of women in this sector are masked by their low participation in decision-making (agricultural sector) where they are satisfied only with market gardening as their source of income. And when, under the effect of climate change, these activities become less profitable and more difficult to carry out, the lack of consideration for women farmers becomes critical while their participation in the policy and decision-

# 4.11 Kenya

## Demographics and human development

Kenya is a multi-cultural and multi-ethnic country with a population of 54,836,205 of which women make up to 52% of the total population. The country is diverse in terms of geography and ethnic populations. Indeed, many different groups make up the country's population. The following ethnic groups are found throughout the country: Kikuyu; Luhya; Luo; Kalenjin; Kamba; Kisi; Meru; Other African; Somali; Swahili; Turkana; Taita; and other.

Kenya is ranked 109th out of 134 countries on the Global Gender Gap Index. Thus, the drops 33 places from the previous year. Similarly, in the Global Gender Gap index ranking, the country is ranked 114th with a score of 0.589. The country has made significant progress since 2006 when it was ranked 127th out of 134 countries in the country. Kenya's main economic sectors remain Agriculture (44.3%) and Industry (6.2%). Services (29.4%) is also a major contributor to the economy.

Kenya has made significant progress since 2006 when it was ranked 127th out of 134 countries in the country's main economic sectors remain Agriculture (44.3%) and Industry (6.2%). Services (29.4%) is also a major contributor to the economy.

Kenya has made significant progress since 2006 when it was ranked 127th out of 134 countries in the country's main economic sectors remain Agriculture (44.3%) and Industry (6.2%). Services (29.4%) is also a major contributor to the economy.

### Education

In Kenya the national education system consists of three levels: compulsory primary education (starting at age six), secondary education, and higher education. Thanks to free schooling at the primary and secondary levels, the country has made significant progress in increasing access to education. In 2010, 97 million children were enrolled in secondary schools in Kenya. Although primary education is now free in Kenya, families sometimes face barriers to sending their children to school. In rural Kenya, one in two girls never completes primary school and only about half of all girls complete secondary school by the age before age 18 is 30.5 percent. This situation prevents women from reaching higher education. In addition, housework was often left to girls. However, the development of girls through infrastructure projects has been a priority for the government in recent years. The government also encourages girls' work in wood projects.

### Employment

According to the country's national statistics, in 2020, about 17.4 million people were employed in Kenya (compared with 16.9 million people in 2019). The majority of employees were in the informal sector. About 4.3 million worked in informal conditions, while 2.9 million were employed in the formal sector.

The unemployment rate in 2021 for the population aged 15-64 increased from 5.4 percent in 2020 to 6.6 percent. It remains low. To produce more quality jobs, investments in resilient infrastructure are needed.


### Health


---
**Page 253**
---

The Kenyan health care system is divided into three categories: public providers, private non-profit organizations (including faith-based hospitals), mission hospitals, and local and international NGOs), and private for-profit providers. In recent years, the country's health care system, the goal is to Stay on Course with Universal Health Coverage in Kenya.

## Energy, transport, and telecommunications

Kenya has a relatively well-developed physical infrastructure. Among other things, the country has four international airports, an extensive road and rail network, a modern deepwater port at Mombasa capable of handling bulk and other containerized cargo, an expanded liberalizing energy sector, and digitally connected telecommunication services. The government is also investing in the transport sector, including energy and roads. Despite a low population density (54.9 million inhabitants for a surface area of 581,307 km2), Kenya has made rapid progress in electrification over the past 10 years. To achieve its Vision 2030, Kenya has made significant investments in roads, railroads, ports, airports, water and sanitation facilities.

Nationally, the rate of access to electricity is 71.4% while only about 65.7% of the rural population has access to electricity. Thanks to these investments in renewable energy, emissions from the Kenyan electricity sector are among the lowest in Africa. The government's policy on electrification aims at improving communities, especially those living in rural areas, by enhancing the agricultural sector. Ultimately, the Kenyan energy sector is guided by various constitutional, policy and legal frameworks. The government has also ratified a number of international conventions and other various statutory instruments and national international protocols and declarations that include these are:

- **The Constitution of Kenya 2010**
- **The Energy Act, 2019**
- **The National Policy on Energy, 2019**
- **Kenya National Electrification Strategy**
- **Kenya’s Vision 2030**
- **National Gender and Equality Commission Act, 2011.**
- **The National Gender and Development, 2000**

In the transport sector, although the Logistics Performance Index (LPI), which measures the quality of trade and transport infrastructure remains average, the country has taken a step backwards from 3.2 in 2014 to 2.55 in 2018. In the telecommunications sector, Kenya’s Vision 2030 proposes an intensified application of its know-how output and efficiency levels across the three pillars.

## Socio-cultural

A multi-ethnic country, Kenya's cultural heritage is dominated by handcrafts. The gestures and methods of work are transmitted from generation to generation. The culture of Ghana is based on ancestral traditions. In fact, women are considered the custodians of these traditions. They are the family to settle there. In fact, queen mothers are chosen from these families, and are the guardians of the cultural traditions. The current governmental situation has not integrated the status of queen mother in the constitution or law. Queen mothers have no official title other than their honorary title. However, as they become better educated and, more importantly, connected, Ghanaian queen mothers are beginning to reclaim their traditional role by modernizing it. They are


---
**Page 254**
---

Page 250

acquiring new skills and collaborating with their counterparts in other African countries. As a result, they are playing an increasingly important role in the fight for girls' education and against female genital mutilation, early marriage, poverty and other social problems. Their involvement in community development projects will further fight against gender inequality.

### Policy and Legal Framework

A unitary state divided into 47 counties, the Republic of Kenya is governed by the national government and 47 county governments. The Parliament of Kenya is a bicameral chamber consisting of the National Assembly and the Senate. The National Assembly of Kenya has a total of 349 members plus the Speaker. The Senate consists of 68 members representing the counties, as well as youth and the vulnerable, including the disabled, who are represented in the Senate (14).

For better management and enforcement of legal texts at the national level, Kenya has abolished a special court for the interpretation of laws and established a Constitutional Court with jurisdiction and criteria for enforcement in the country. Since 1998, the country has adopted the Kenya Information and Communications Act (KICA). The main objective of this Act is to regulate the use of information technology. The Kenya Information and Communications Act (KICA). This Act sets out the general legal framework for telecommunications in Kenya. This Act has enabled the country to attract a lot of foreign investment through PPPs in telecommunications / information and communication technologies.

In general, the Kenyan government has relied on PPPs for the implementation of strategic infrastructure projects. The first major project was the construction of the Mombasa-Nairobi Standard Gauge Railway (SGR) line. Kenya has also passed several pieces of legislation to have legislation governing PPPs. With the new investment, through PPP programs and projects Kenya has advanced the level of infrastructure development while reducing poverty and promoting gender equality. In addition, PPPs have enabled the country to build a resilient infrastructure network. Kenya has passed several pieces of legislation to promote gender equality, protection of the vulnerable and women's empowerment. The Access to Government through Opportunities (AGFO) Act ensures that men and women participate fully in public procurement.

### Gender based violence

Gender-based violence takes many forms, including female genital mutilation, rape, domestic violence, forced marriage, and dowry-related violence. According to the 2019 Kenya Demographic Health Survey, Kenya gender-based violence affects 35% of young women between the ages of 18 and 24. camped in their homes. The country has also been affected by the effects of climate change, particularly droughts and floods. When the drought crisis intensifies, the population especially those in rural areas are heavily impacted. These impacts range from crop failure to livestock deaths. In addition, women and girls are more vulnerable to the effects of the drought, dowries in cash, food, and livestock decrease, sometimes forcing families to marry off more girls (UNICEF, 2022). In northeastern Kenya, early marriage and genital mutilation are increasingly common, with more than four out of ten women being given in marriage before the age

In pursuit of achieving the SDGs goals for the SDG5, one of which is to end all forms of gender-based violence, Kenya has developed specific policies and strategies to prevent and respond to gender-based violence. For example, the National Policy on Prevention and Response to Gender-Based Violence was launched in 2014. The country has also ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Optional Protocol thereto. Kenya is committed to the implementation and management of resilient infrastructure projects that affect their daily lives can help reduce poverty, improve access to education and health services, and provide women with an important source of quality labor to boost and accelerate progress towards the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, at the same time, their contribution will help eradicate poverty in the country.

Access to Finances

With the opening up to investment in the country, Kenya has made significant progress in financial inclusion, tripling its financial sector in 13 years. In Kenya, several surveys have been conducted including the Financial Sector Deepening (FSD) Kenya’s FinAccess Household Surveys. Results from these surveys showed that Kenya has made significant progress in promoting financial inclusion among women and men across all regions of the country.

According to the 2014 report, nearly 7 out of 10 Kenyan adults held a registered account with a formal financial institution[1]. According to the 2019 Financial Access Household Survey, financial access has improved from 8.5% in 2014 to 13.6% in 2019. The gender gap in financial inclusion remains significant. Another household survey conducted by FSD Kenya in 2017 showed that women have lower levels of access to formal financial services. This time, the gender gap in access to financial services has improved from 8.5 percent in 2014, to 5.2 percent in 2019, to 4.2 percent in[2] 2021.

To further involve women in the country’s economic development process, Kenya FSD undertakes several initiatives aimed at increasing access and use of financial services among a number of these include the Women’s Economic Empowerment Project (completed in March), a survey of gender-based violence in the financial sector, and a study on the impact of mobile money on financial loss in Kenya. Notwithstanding the multiple initiatives undertaken to facilitate improved access to financial services for the entire Kenyan population, women in rural areas largely do not benefit from these initiatives.

The use of mobile money is essential to charge phones and other digital equipment necessary for mobile money transfers. The number of mobile money service providers directly registered with the Central Bank of Kenya has increased significantly, as have the number of mobile money services and, in the long term, the implementation of banking services.

As the largest economy in the East African Community (EAC), with a GDP of USD 100 billion in 2019 or USD 1,998 per capita, Kenya is the only middle-income country in the EAC to increase its growth. Kenya has long embarked on the process of modernizing all sectors of economic activity. As a result, the country has experienced significant improvements in poverty levels and access to basic services areas; the poverty rate has dropped significantly from 50% in 2005 to 38.6% ten years later due to the industrialization of the agricultural sector and the electrification of rural areas[1]. The impact of climate change is also felt by Kenya, which is one of the most vulnerable countries in Africa with a large population. The weakness of rural infrastructure, especially irrigation, has limited the growth of agriculture and livestock production. In addition to these factors, the rapid urbanization and urbanization have increased pressure on the environment. Poverty and food insecurity have also increased pressure on natural resources. Also, opportunities for access to arable land are limited due to the high population density in rural areas. The country has a large number of young men and women. New investments in resilient infrastructure are an opportunity for the country to ensure continued economic growth while reducing poverty and inequality[2]. The use of renewable sources of power plants such as solar thermal or wind energy will allow Kenya to reduce greenhouse gases and also increase the country's energy security. According to the Government of Kenya's 2013 report, electricity generation from renewable sources is expected to reach 5% by 2020 which would enable the country to reduce up to 2.5 metric tons of CO2 by 2030 (Government of Kenya, 2013).

Unequal Participation in decision making

After a series of reforms in the country, including the revision of the constitution in 2010, equality for and protection of women's rights have been enshrined as national values. The new constitution was put into effect by Article 10 of the constitution. These national values and principles should guide the country in its policies and legislation. In addition to these constitutional reforms, the 2010 Constitution also provided for gender equality in decision-making; in sum, the 2010 constitution promulgated in Kenya requires that the number of MPs not exceed two-thirds of the members of parliament i.e., at least 33% of the seats should be reserved for women. The 2010 constitution also provides for a quota system to ensure representation in the National Assembly as well, representing less than 20% of the seats; this is because Kenya does not have a specific law that guarantees gender equality and representation in decision-making bodies. To incorporate these principles into various pieces of legislation, policies and other interventions to provide protection to the vulnerable, SIE, the country has achieved an all-time high in the number of women elected as governors and senators ranking 7th among the top 100 countries in the world ranking of women in national parliament[3].

In 2017, women were elected as governors and senators. In addition, the number of women in the country's legislative bodies has increased significantly since 2013 when only four out of 47 seats were held by elected women, up from 146 after the 2013 elections. However, more work needs to be done as significant barriers remain for women seeking elected offices. Now to further engage women in decision-making processes and ensure that they are not left behind, the government has launched a project called "Women's Empowerment in Decision Making" (WEDM). In fact, the project must involve companies at any stage of the infrastructure lifecycle that have more than 30 percent women in management, or more than 30 percent on their boards considering equity in the workplace.

In Kenya, gender mainstreaming in the planning and delivery of transport and energy infrastructure and services is very important because women’s and men’s transport and energy access needs and patterns are different. Women often carry out unpaid domestic work and have limited access to decision-making roles or are sometimes very poorly represented. # 4.12 Mali

## Demographics and human development

Mali’s population is about 15.8 million inhabitants, of which 51 percent are women; the vast majority of the population (960 percent) lives in rural areas, where the maternal re-infestation rate in 2014 was 19.39 percent (39 percent national, 86 percent in urban areas).

Mali’s HDI value for 2018 is 0.427, positioning it at 184 out of 189 countries and territories. The 2018 World Bank Gender Development Index (GDI) score was 0.356, resulting in a Gender Development Index (GDI) value of 0.807. In terms of gender inequality, it is one of the unequal countries in the world; Mali has a GII value of 0.674, ranking it 158 out of 124 countries in the 2018 index. For every 100,000 live births, there are approximately 390 maternal deaths and an adolescent birth rate is 161 (births per 1,000 women of ages 15–19) (Human Development Report, 2017).

## Education

Similar to other countries in the region, women and girls in Mali have less access to education than men and boys. The adult literacy rate for women is 23.7 percent, whereas the rate for men is 46.2 percent. Only 58.9 percent of girls complete primary school compared with 71.0 percent of boys (73.3 percent) is less than half the percentage of their male counterparts (16.4 percent). In comparison, 78.2 percent of men complete secondary education and only 38.1 percent of women do so; 95.2 percent complete the primary school cycle. In some areas of the country, less than 36 percent of enrolled children are girls, one of the widest gender gaps in the world. This is unlikely to improve in the short term due to a lack of funding and closure of many schools, particularly in Central Mali (path Draft Mali country strategic plan 2020-2024).

## Employment

World Bank data show that in Mali, Female participation in the labour market is 61.2 percent compared to 80.2 for men. And as elsewhere in Sub-Saharan Africa, females are under-represented in political decision-making and leadership positions. Women represent only 35.7 percent of senior managers and managers of formal enterprises in Mali—although according to estimates, the informal economy accounts for about half of all jobs in Mali. In 2016, women represented 48.9 percent of employees and 35.7 percent of managers, while in 2007, females were 16.3 percent of owners. Similarly, other Sub-Saharan African countries show that female-controlled firms differ from their male-controlled counterparts.

## Health

Despite the interest given to maternal health in recent decades in Mali, in particular through the policies of free cesarean section (2005), free means of prevention and treatment of malaria in pregnant women (2010) and the institutionalization of notification of maternal death, perinatal and neonatal (2017), pregnancy and childbirth remain associated with high risks for mothers.


---
**Page 259**
---

Energy, transport and telecommunications

The national access to electricity in Mali is 41% (17% in rural areas), and the electrification rate remains low, with around 15% of households connected to the electrical network. The country has enormous potential for renewable energy sources such as solar power, wind power, and hydroelectricity. However, the energy deficit at the county is dependent on imported petroleum products and wood combustible as a result of the lack of infrastructure in the energy sector. This situation makes Mali highly vulnerable to climate change. Indeed, Mali's energy sector remains very vulnerable to climate change due to its high dependence on hydropower. The entire energy system is under the threat of climate change. This situation calls for the diversification of the country’s energy mix by promoting the use of renewable solar energy and biomass. The burden of securing the household’s energy supply falls disproportionately on women. Statistics on total energy consumption in the country in 2016 show that women consume more than men.

Mali faces significant environmental problems such as desertification, floods, and desertification. Women are more victims of the adverse effects of climate change, 58% certain women's activities, such as agriculture, livestock rearing, fishing, and gathering firewood and water, directly impact the environment. So, concrete measures should accompany the ongoing awareness-raising campaigns to promote the use of environmentally friendly technologies in agriculture (use of organic fertilizers, development of soaps works), etc. The national priority is to strengthen efforts to promote the adoption of appropriate technologies that are less costly and less energy-consuming for all men and women.

Mali has a vast road network consisting of 89,204 km, of which approximately 24,000 km are developed, ranging from rural tracks (cotton and pasture) to international road corridors, coated with asphalt. The length of the national roads is 65,173 km, while that of the regional roads has a length of 5,790 km. The road density is 1.80km/100km², one of the lowest in Africa. This weakness is even more accentuated in the northern regions.

In terms of communication infrastructure, Mali's telephone network is at a very low level of development. Only 25% of households have access to telephones. The infrastructure is mostly in the urban areas with 69.9% of all lines in the biggest cities. Its telephone density (telephones per hundred people) in urban areas is 1.78, compared to 0.08 for the rest of the country.


---
**Page 260**
---

## Socio-cultural

In as most African countries, the social and professional situation of Malian women is often inferior to that of men for various reasons, all related to education. Certain ancestral practices are a hindrance to their development. There are also cultural taboos which limit their freedom. The lack of time spent on certain activities such as the search for wood and household chores is a hindrance to the education of girls. Young girls who have been forced into marriage do not benefit from a pre-marriage education, which would allow them to develop their personality and prepare themselves for education and to develop personal projects. Exclusion and infibulations are practices that affect the quality of life of women in certain regions of the country even though they are denounced in the country.

In Mali, there is a gender division of labor based on a patriarchal organization that still keeps women in reproductive functions in society. This situation creates a disparity between men and women in their economic activities. The husband is the head of the family and makes all decisions concerning his wife and his wife owes him respect and obedience. He makes all important decisions and is responsible for providing food, clothing and shelter to his family. The average of literacy decreases because of their multiple occupations, the reluctance of their husbands, socio-cultural constraints and the extreme poverty of the populations, especially in rural areas.

## Policy and Legal Framework

In 1997, the Ministry for the Promotion of Women, Children and the Family (MPEF) was created as the main government institution responsible for women's empowerment and gender equality. It is responsible for monitoring the implementation of the National Policy for Gender Equality adopted in 2010. The MPEF also monitors the implementation of the National Action Plan on Gender Equality and it promotes equal rights for men and women, active and participatory citizenship, and the fight against all forms of violence against women. In order to promote gender equality and to appropriate technologies that are less costly and less reliant on wood energy use. The "Gender and Development Thematic Group" (GIGED) was established to facilitate dialogue between the Government and civil society organizations in the field of gender equality. In 2009, the government attempted to adopt a new Family Code to introduce improvements for women's rights (inheritance, property, and marriage, among others). However, opposition from traditionalists and religious leaders led to the rejection of this initiative by Parliament. The code was introduced, resulting in several setbacks regarding women's rights (World Bank, Mali Gender Assessment).

### Gender based violence

Mali has few national legal instruments working for gender equality. With respect to violence against women, domestic violence, or sexual harassment, the country has no specific law only low ratio is published annually by the Ministry of Women and Family Affairs. According to the latest data from the international instruments that have been ratified, in 2014, more than 40% of women and girls aged 15-49 use violence against their partners. According to the Demographic and Health Survey (EDS) of 2018, 43% of women aged between 15 - 49 have experienced physical or sexual sexual violence in the Kaye region. Approximately 89% of women aged 15-49 have been cut; three-quarters

Access to Finances

Since 2020, the combined effect of the pandemic and poor agricultural performance have impacted the Malian economy, which is experiencing a slowdown in its growth rate (World Bank, 2020). This situation has had an impact on the financial inclusion of women in rural areas.

With a rural population that represents more than 64% of the total population, the country's economic activities are dominated by the informal sector. This situation limits the population’s access to basic services and financial products. According to the World Bank (2020), it is estimated that only 15% of women in Mali have access to formal financial services.

The data shows that the net per capita income for a woman is US$1,349 while men it is US$1,671 and 49.35% of the population lives below the poverty line (World Bank, 2020). In addition, women in rural areas who do not have access to the financial capital needed to develop economic activities (FAO, 2020).

However, to improve the living conditions of its population, the Government of Mali has developed, with the support of its partners, strategies for the financial inclusion of its population, particularly women. In this context, the government launched in 2018 the National Strategy for Financial Inclusion (NSFI), co-financed by IADB and the Kingdom of Denmark, to improve the financial inclusion of women in rural areas. This strategy is based on the implementation of the Agricultural Sector Project (PICAUS), co-financed by IADB and the Kingdom of Denmark, to improve the financial inclusion of women in rural areas.

The government has also launched a National Strategy for Financial Inclusion program since 2011 supporting income-generating activities and community savings groups in the country. Finally, in order to better integrate Malian women, particularly those living in rural areas, into the national economy, the government is working on measures aimed at ensuring equitable access to agricultural land resources and the possibility of taking positive discriminatory measures for vulnerable groups.

With a GDP per capita of $462.5<sup>10</sup>, and growing increasingly since 2012, most of the Malian population is experiencing food insecurity. Mali's population is growing at a rate of 3.8% per year, while more than three-quarters of its population lives in rural areas. In recent years, Mali has been faced with numerous natural disasters, including droughts and floods. These recurring droughts and floods have left thousands of Malians facing hunger. In fact, recurrent droughts and floods entrench poverty and undermine people's resilience. In 2017, 135,000 pregnant or breastfeeding women were acutely malnourished in Mali. The number of children under five years old who suffered from wasting across the country. This problem specifically impacts women since they tend to be the first to suffer from food insecurity and are more likely than men to die as a result.

Between 2011 and 2013, the monetary poverty rate rose from 45.4% to 47.1%, an increase of 1.7%. However, in 2013 more than 54% of the rural population dominated by women lived below the poverty line compared with only 48% of urban dwellers (World Bank, 2020). The gender gap in poverty is also large: 69% of poor households are headed by women. At the national level, about 43.5% of the population lived below the national poverty line, 18% of whom (mainly women and children) live in rural areas over the 2018-2019 period<sup>11</sup>.

Unequal Participation in decision making

In Mali, women's decision-making capacity in reproductive health, financial empowerment, and politics is low. However, they play a very important financial role within households, especially when it comes to managing household savings and investments. Indeed, there are obstacles to Malian women’s access to decision-making positions at the community level as well as in terms of political participation and representation in decision-making bodies. In 2015, only 8 women were elected as mayors out of 703 available positions and 927 communal council members out of 10,574. As a representation rate of 6.8% for women versus 93.2% for men in the commune councils<sup>12</sup>. In addition to this, only 1 woman was elected as a deputy and 1 as an MP<sup>13</sup>. At the national level, about 43.5% of the population lived below the national poverty line, 18% of whom (mainly women and children) live in rural areas over the 2018-2019 period<sup>11</sup>.

In December 2015, a law was adopted to include women in decision-making bodies and at all levels of public administration. This law calls for a 30% quota for women in normative and elective positions in Mali. In fact, after the legislative elections of 2020, nearly 28% of women will be sitting in parliament<sup>14</sup>. However, this is still far from the 50% target set by the UN. The number of women parliaments has dropped considerably. In February 2021, only 67% of women sit at parliament; 17.4% of women hold leadership positions. However, senior and middle management positions are where women are better represented with a rate of 38.7%.

# 4.13 Mauritania

## Demographics and human development

According to the National Agency of Statistics, the Mauritanian population is estimated to 4.3 million inhabitants (2019). The Gini index value for 2019 is 0.864, which puts the country in the very high human development category—positioning it at 66 out of 189 countries and territories. Between 1999 and 2019, Mauritania’s HDI value increased from 0.624 to 0.804, an increase of 38.8 percent. The 2019 female-to-male ratio in terms of the Human Development Index (HDI) is estimated at 0.756, placing it into Group B of countries with high HDI achievements between women and men. The Gender Inequality Index (GII) is estimated to a value of 0.347, ranking it 78 out of 142 countries in the 2019 index.

## Education

Significant progress has been made in the education sector. The HDI 2020 shows that the mean years of schooling increased by 3.8 years between 1990 and 2019 and the expected years of schooling increased from 5 to 7.4 years, which is a significant improvement. The literacy rate has also improved significantly over time. According to the National Demographic and Health survey (2021) results showed an improvement in the literacy rate from the older generations to the younger but the gender gap remains. Among women aged between 15 and 49 years, the literacy rate increased from 38% in 2017 to 67% in 2021 respectively among men. The proportion of adult women who have reached at least a secondary level of education has also increased significantly compared with their male counterparts. The World Bank strategic country diagnostic in January 2022 revealed that the more pressing gender gaps in education are around sex segregation by subject, with women less likely to follow subjects such as mathematics and science. Women are also underrepresented in vocational education and, among those in tertiary education, less likely to be on doctorate (PhD) and master of business administration (MBA) programs. This segregation, informed by gender norms around the types of subject that are suitable for women and girls, feeds into gender gaps in employment.

## Employment

According to the World Bank, in Mauritania, women stop working at an early age to marry and raise their children. So, their rate of participation in the labor market is particularly low: 45.2 percent compared to 73.9 percent for men (World Bank, 2018). The female unemployment rate is estimated at 16%, and this difference seems to be particularly accentuated among 24 to 35-year-old women who are the right age to give birth. In 2012, female unemployment stood at 12.6%, i.e., 46% higher than that of men (MPCD, 2018). The World Bank also notes that women in Mauritania have less access to education and training opportunities compared with men. The combination of several factors has shown that women have often had less access than men to productive resources, education, skills development, and the labor market. They still remain confined to their traditional roles as housewives and mothers, which are considered more appropriate for them, and often work in lower paid jobs than men. This situation confirms their underrepresentation in several sectors of economic activity, particularly in the public sector (15% of employees), in agriculture (20%), in transport and communication sector (7.6%, of employees are women against 92.4 % for men). A recent Enterprise Survey conducted jointly by the Mauritanian National Productivity and Competitiveness Council (NPCC) and the World Bank found that women account for only 13 percent of top managers.

**Page 265**

# Health

Even if there is room for improvement, Mauritius has a free and high-quality universal healthcare system and close to 100 percent of births are attended by skilled health staff; only one-third of pregnant women initiate prenatal care in the first trimester and the maternal mortality rate (61 deaths per 100,000 live births) is high compared to the average for middle- and high-income countries (43) and represents a significant proportion of total infant deaths. The country has made progress in reducing neonatal deaths, from 25.9 in 2018 to 15.3 in 2017. Although there are many encouraging results, the impacts of climate changes will have with a more detrimental impact on women if gender equality is not addressed on time.

## Energy, transport and telecommunications

In Mauritius, the transport sector is under increasing pressure from congestion. Much of the primary and secondary road network, and virtually the entirety of the tertiary network, are not engineered roads and have been constructed by paving off former farmland across sugar cane production areas. The country has also experienced significant changes in its infrastructure and quality transport infrastructure remains a major obstacle to the growth of the situation. In addition, the lack of access to electricity is still a problem for many households, especially in rural areas. Indeed, rural women will still continue to suffer great problems for the sale of their products derived from crafts, market gardening and pickling. Given the vastness of the country and the complexity of its terrain, it is difficult to provide adequate infrastructure services that can promote the exchanges, reduce production costs, strengthen the competitiveness of the national economy in urbanized or rural areas which are areas where the poor are concentrated (including the majority of women), which moreover contain a significant potential for production and economic growth.

According to the World Bank Systematic Country Diagnostic report (2021), earlier efforts in the telecommunications sector have resulted in Mauritius having one of the highest mobile density and provide competitive connectivity rates. Consequently, Mauritius is doing well in terms of access to telecommunication services with 98 percent for mobile penetration and 87 percent for broadband penetration respectively. Much progress has also been done on the conventions for the digital governance. Recent years have been marked by the adoption of a National Strategy of Digital Transformation as a digital government, thus the country's adoption of digital transformation is thus expected to achieve greater maturity in the coming years.

120 World Bank (2021): Investing in Foundational Skills in Mauritius, Input to the World Bank’s 2021 Systematic Country Diagnostic Report. March. Washington D.C.


---
**Page 266**
---

Access to energy is a major problem in Mauritania, as the energy needs of the Mauritanian population are not covered. Indeed, only 47.35% of the population has access to electricity in 2020. In addition, there are significant inequalities between urban and rural areas. The role of access to electricity in 2020 is also a major challenge for the country's development strategy. To address this issue, Mauritania adopted a national strategy for the transformation of its energy sector and aims to increase the access rate of the national population by turning more towards renewable energy. The country’s goal is to increase the share of renewable energy to 40% by 2030.


### Socio-cultural

Mauritania is a multicultural and multireligious country, made up with different Arab and black African communities who all share the same religion, Islam; the Islamic ties are the cement that unites the people of the country despite their cultural differences. In addition to this religious factor, which has a strong influence on the Mauritanian society, the status of men and women is strongly influenced by religion but also by the customs and traditions of each community. The social structure in Mauritania is very different from one community to another and from one social class to the other. Despite the strong political commitment of the Mauritanian Government towards the adoption of several strategies and texts in favor of the advancement of women and gender equality generally.


### Policy and Legal Framework

The Ministry of Gender Equality, Child Development and Family Welfare is the government entity in charge of policies related to gender equality and women's rights. The country has ratified a number of international instruments. For the Legal Framework the Government has taken the following actions towards gender quality:

- Mauritius has ratified the Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Discriminations Against Women (CEDAW) in 1985.
- Mauritius is party to the 1997 SADC Declaration on Gender.
- Mauritius is party to the 1998 Addendum on the Prevention and Eradication of Violence against Women.
- Mauritius is party to the 1998 Addendum on the Prevention and Eradication of Violence against Women.
- Mauritius has signed the African Union Declaration on Gender Equality in 2004.
- Mauritius has signed the Commonwealth Plan of Action on Gender Equality 2005–2015.

Mauritania has agreed and ratified the Protocol on the Rights of Women at the African Charter on Human and People's Rights in 2003.


### Gender based violence

The Demographic health survey 2019-2021 data revealed that in Mauritania, the percentage of women aged 15–49 who declared having already suffered violence since the age of 15 is 10%. and this situation vary according to education level. The percentage of women with primary education or less who reported physical violence less frequently than the others (7% against 15% among those with primary education and 13% among those with no level of education). They were also more likely to report having been

**Page 267**

Page 263

### Access to Finances

According to Global Index 2017 data, Mauritius has the highest financial inclusion rate on the African continent: 89.8%, more than double the average for sub-Saharan Africa (42.6%) and well above the average for high-income countries (73.1%). The financial inclusion rates were 80.15% and 82.75% in 2015 and 2016, respectively. Access to credit is also very low: only 49.5% of adults have access to formal credit, while the average for sub-Saharan Africa was 30%. The proportion of women with access to domestic financial systems is minimal: 87.1% of women have access to the domestic financial system. In 2017, a Mauritius had an average of at least two bank accounts. Just under one in two (42%) bank accounts registered the name of a woman as the account holder.

### Poverty

Climate change affects men and women differently - which in turn affects exposure to poverty - especially for women who are more vulnerable than men to climate-related disasters. Climate change has a disproportionately greater effect on women. The national official statistics showed that women were more likely to be in relative poverty (8%). The proportion of female in relative poverty was 1,106,524 compared with 739,200 males. In 2017, 15.9% of female-headed households were in relative poverty as compared to 7.6% of male-headed households.

### Sheqeq Participation in decision taking

In July 2006, the adoption of the ordinance relating to the promotion of the involvement of women in the political decision-making process which imposed a minimum quota of 25% for the representation of women on every municipal council and in all public service bodies. The law also provided that women should be represented in all spheres. The 2020 human development report data showed that 29% of parliament seat are held by women and 18% of ministerial positions, while only 5% of the 34 ministers in charge of the public service were women. Also, women also hold 25% of political ministries, five of the 25 ministries being headed today by women.

A study on the gender quota profile conducted in the Islamic Republic of Mauritius by the African Development Bank (AfDB) showed that women are underrepresented in all spheres of public administration. Within the civil service, key positions are mostly held by men and women are mainly administrative staff. Women hold 25% of ministerial positions, while only 10% of ministers are women administrators[1]. The country's energy sector, particularly the oil and gas sector, is a male-dominated industry. As a result, women are largely underrepresented in these sectors in decision-making positions.

# 4.14 Namibia

## Demographics and human development

According to World Bank (2018), the total population of Namibia is about 2.5 million, of which 63% live in rural areas and 37% in urban areas. Namibia's 2019 HDI of 0.646 is above the average of 0.631 for countries in the medium human development group and above the average of 0.547 for countries in Sub-Saharan Africa, between 1999 and 2019. Namibia's HDI value increased from 0.581 to 0.646 during this period.

Namibia has made progress on reducing poverty, with the proportion of people living below the national poverty line falling by about 3 percentage points between 2017 and 2019. The number of children out of school decreased from 15% in 2018 to 14% in 2019. The net primary enrollment rate increased by 6%, the gross secondary enrollment rate increased by 3 percentage points, and the gross tertiary enrollment rate increased by 7%. The proportion of people with at least a secondary education increased from 55% in 2018 to 57% in 2019. The proportion of children under five years old who are fully immunized against measles increased by about 3 percentage points between 2016 and 2019.

## Education

According to the UNDP, Namibia National Human Development Report 2019, Namibia has made progress in making education accessible to all boys and girls in primary and secondary schools. The differences in access to education are still significant: only about 5% of women have no formal education in Namibia is high, with only 5 percent of women and 8 percent of men having no formal education.

Women are more likely to reach higher levels of education than men; in most regions in Namibia, the proportion of women who completed secondary school was greater than that for men. Women have reached at least a secondary level of education compared to 42.0 percent of their male counterparts. The World Bank data shows that the youth female literacy rate is 76.25% and 13.8% lower than males, while in Namibia, according to recent data (2018) on primary school completion rates is also high for women (94.9%) men (91.2%).

## Employment

According to the 2018 Labour Force Survey, there were 875,638 youth aged 15 to 24 in Namibia, of whom 310,884 (35.8%) were employed and 263,770 (30.3%) were unemployed. This means that the labour force in this age groups totals 574,624 persons, giving a Labour Force Participation Rate (LFPR) of 65.8%. Sex disaggregated data show a Labour force participation of 65.0% for women and 66.1% for men.

The youth unemployment rate is 39%, which is higher than the national average of 27.5% compared to the youth unemployment rate of 43.4% reported in the year 2016. Female youth

In Namibia, culture has been integrated into key documents such as the 2001 Arts and Culture Policy and the National Development Plan 3 (2007-2012). However, in recent years, its role in the development process has been less important, especially since it is not integrated into the National Development Plan+4 (2013-2017) [1]. The country, therefore, faces the complex challenges of realising social and economic justice for all Namibians. In addition to this, the country is divided into thirteen regions containing eleven ethnic groups with a wide range of tribes among them.

It should be noted that for a long time women were considered the property of men until the country's independence. After independence, many changes to improve gender equality, as well as equality for all, have incorporated into the country's new constitution [2]. As a result, women have the right to own, contract, and regulate property in their own names and on directors of companies.

### Policy and Legal Framework

The Namibian Constitution provides the framework for principles of gender equality in Namibia. These principles are enshrined in Article 10 (Equality) [3]. These constitutional provisions form the basis of the commitment of the Namibian Government to the improvement of the status of women in society and the eradication of the injustices of the past. Towards this end, measures have been implemented to promote gender equality.

The National Gender Policy conforms to the provisions of several national, regional, and international legal instruments which the Namibian Government has adopted, signed, and/or ratified to promote gender equality [4].

- The SAIC Declaration on Gender and Development and its Addendum on the Prevention and Eradication of Violence against Women and Children
- The SAIC Protocol on Gender and development
- The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPFA)
- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW 1997) and its Optional Protocol
- The Convention on the Rights of the Child [1990]
- The Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (2003)
- The UN Convention against Transnational Crime, 2000 (UNTOC) and the Protocol to Prevent, Suppress and Punish Trafficking in Persons, especially Women and Children, known as the Palermo Protocol
- The UN Security Council Resolution 1325 on Women, Peace, and Security [2000]
- The International Conference on Population and Development [1994]
- The Universal Declaration on Human Rights (UDHR)

* The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR)

The Government adopted the National Policy Frameworks for promoting gender mainstreaming in Namibia, including the National Development Plans (NDPs) and Vision 2036, which recognize the NDPs as a key tool to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The first National Development Plan (NDP1) represents the first systematic attempt to translate the Vision 2030 objectives into concrete policies and actions. NDPS identified five core areas to be mainstreamed through the NDP1, including gender equality, education, health, economic empowerment, environment, information, communication, and technology. The Gender Policy seeks to create an enabling environment for institutions and sectors to mainstream gender perspectives according to the NDP priorities.

Gender based violence

At an equitable and gender-sensitive nation, the integrity of Namibia's population, and women in particular, is a critical environment largely free from violence including gender-based violence and crime. The country is prepared to face and respond to any man-made or natural calamity. According to the 2013 Namibia Demographic and Health Survey, 33 percent of women aged 15 to 49 were victims of physical violence in their lifetime. Women are disproportionately affected by poverty and vulnerable to exploitation through trafficking and other forms of forced labor. In 2015, the first human trafficking case was successfully prosecuted in Namibia. As a result, the country has seen a significant decrease in the number of cases. As a result, the country has set a target to lower the GDP prevalence rate from 3% (2013) to 20% by 2026[1].

Access to Finances

Financial inclusion plays a central role in fighting poverty and contributing to Namibia's inclusive economic growth. Indeed, the Namibian government has long viewed financial inclusion as a key driver of inclusive economic growth and prosperity for its people. In fact, the government has made it an overarching national program integrated into Namibia’s financial sector strategy.

Poverty

With a population of over 2.5 million in 2020, 30% of the population lives on less than US$1.9 per day and unemployment remains high. Women and people living in rural areas are among the most affected populations[2]. The country has one of the highest poverty rates (48%) according to the Human Poverty Index (HPI) and 13th out of 188 countries in the world. With the COVID-19 crisis and the effects of climate change impacts such as drought, the poverty situation especially in low-income communities has worsened.

Indeed, the Namibia Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) report[2021] shows that the incidence of multidimensional poverty is 43%, which means that 43.8% of the Namibian population is poor.


---
**Page 272**
---

multidimensionally poor. The report also highlights the fact that the incidence of multidimensional poverty is higher among female-headed households (with a rate of 46%), than among male-headed households (with a rate of 41%).

### Shequl Participation in decision taking

Nambia is one of the countries with a high rate of women's participation in decision-making positions. In fact, since 2013, the 50/50 gender policy that requires "equal representation of men and women" is applied to all public institutions and private companies in Namibia.

After the implementation of this policy, women's positions in parliament increased from 2% to 44%. Thus, an inclusive environment in political and urban settings has been created for women in leadership roles. The percentage of women in politics and decision making has increased from 10% (2013) to 45% (2016). The percentage of women in politics and decision making has increased from 44% to 47% (2015) with a target of 50% by 2020.

For several years now, communities in the north have been working towards women's empowerment with many stakeholders dedicated to increasing the role of women in the justice system. The main actors are the interventions of the Ministry of Justice and Constitutional Development (MOJCD), the National Institute for Development (NID), and inclusiveness group with community members from every constituency in northern Namibia. In addition, women are increasingly participating in decision-making at the local level. Traditional gender norms have evolved and become more inclusive. Women's chiefs are already working in the customary courts.

# 4.15 Federal Republic of Nigeria

## Demographics and human development

The population of Nigeria is estimated about 184.6 million inhabitants, of which 39.17 percent are women. Nigeria also has the fastest-growing demographics, about the average population growth rate for West Africa i.e., 2.75 per year. The ratio between the urban and rural population growths is 48.3 percent to 51.7 percent. According to UNDP (2020), Nigeria's Human Development Index (HDI) value for the 2020 was 0.69, placing the country in the low human development category, ranking it at 161 out of 188 countries.

Nigeria is ranked 139 out of 154 on the 2020 UNDP Gender Inequality Index (GII), with a score of 0.427. On the ADB Human Development Index (HDI) for 2020, Nigeria is ranked at 86th place in the world. The ADB index ranks countries based on three dimensions: equality; economic opportunity, social development and law, and institutions. The ranking is a 0-100, with 100 representing perfect gender equality. Nigeria's overall score of 59.2 indicates that women have fewer opportunities, an unsatisfactory 32nd in human and social development, and 30th in laws and institutions.

### Education

Data from the Demographic and Health Survey 2018 showed that men are better educated than women. Fifty-five percent of women and 57% of men aged 15-49 have no formal education, while 11% of women and 6% of men have a secondary or higher education. The proportion of women with no education has decreased since 2003, from 42% to 33%. The median number of years of schooling completed by women is 5.9 compared to 7.8 for men. The median number of years of schooling has increased from 6.6 to 10.5 years. Results also revealed that the percentage of women who have a secondary education or more is highest in Lagos (48%) and lowest in Sokoto (13%).

### Employment

According to World Development Indicators, the female labour force participation rate is 44.2% in 2021. The latest Demographic and Health Survey showed that seventy-four percent of currently married women aged 15-49 were employed at least 12 months before the survey, as compared with sixty-nine percent of currently married men (Table 13). Among those employed, women earn less than men but be paid in cash only (78% versus 80%). Fifteen percent of women and 8% of men do not receive any payment for their work. The proportion of employed women who worked at least 24 hours per week has increased slightly over time, from 71% in both 2008 and 2013 to 74% in 2018. After increasing from 61% in 2008 to 79% in 2013, the percentage of employed married women who worked for their own account or business declined slightly over time, from 59% in both 2008 and 2013 to 48% in 2018. The proportion of employed married women not paid for their work declined from 17% to 6% between 2008 and 2013 before rising to 15% in 2018.


---
**Page 274**
---

# Health

The health profile of Nigeria has improved in recent years, but it remains overall poor. The under-5 mortality rate has been enhanced from more than 191 per 1,000 live births in 1990 to 89 per 1,000 live births in 2014. The leading causes of child mortality are malaria (20%), pneumonia (17%), other diseases (14%), prematurity (12%), and diarrhea (11%). Child malnutrition is widespread. The rate of children under five with stunted growth has been reduced from 53% in 1990 to 38% in 2014, but remains high compared to the region's average (27%) and the world average (26%). Nigeria is one of the few countries where malaria transmission is year-round. The fertility rate for all children per woman above the regional average (5 children) and the world average (2.5 children). Maternal mortality was 343 per 100,000 live births in 2014, which was higher than the world average of 216 deaths per 100,000 live births. The life expectancy at birth was 68 years in 2005 and has increased to 53.3 years in 2012 (53.4 years for women and 51.7 years for men).

## Energy, transport and telecommunications

Although Nigeria is rich in energy resources and the largest oil-producing country in Africa, access to modern energy such as electricity, liquid and gaseous fuels, modern cooking appliances, and mechanical power is limited. The majority of households (80%) have no access to electricity; 95% of rural areas are unelectrified, and most households, especially in the rural area (only 25.3% access), use mainly biomass for cooking.

While access to modern energy services is documented as necessary for economic growth and development, Nigeria's energy sector has struggled with low investment, inadequate, and ailing infrastructure, which resulted in shortages and consequent rise in costs, access, and affordability to the various forms of energy. In addition, the heavy reliance on fossil fuels in the Ecowas region and most Sub-Saharan African countries, there is heavy dependence on traditional cooking methods that still cooking with charcoal and firewood. Biomass represents 57% of the final energy consumed.

The primary energy source for most rural people in Nigeria is biomass, which may be sourced in some cases more than five kilometers away. This harms women as they traveling long distances on foot, but women are also responsible for collecting water and firewood, which can lead to poor health and wellbeing.

On electricity (electrification) access: there's a differential access by men and women to electrification possibly because of socioeconomic status; it) women and men have different preferences for off-grid lighting products and appliances; ii) in patriarchal societies of which Nigeria is one, men usually make buying decisions and the household with men going for luxury type goods (e.g., television) rather than non-electric appliance. Women are more likely to buy a solar lantern or other off-grid lighting use electricity differently.

Concerning access to ICT, according to a youth survey report issued by the National Bureau of Statistics in 2015, young men are almost twice as likely to have a career in computer science and technology-related fields as women. Also, no access to information and Communications Technology

**Page 275**
---

# Social-cultural

The culture of Nigeria is one of the oldest in West Africa, with several cultural practices from many civilizations that have followed. However, some of the socio-cultural factors in the country pose a threat to the health and well-being of Nigerian women. These factors limit: (a) the access of the girl child to education; (b) the economic and political activities of the country.

Similarly, the Nigeria society's traditionally patriarchal which places the man at the head of the family and community has a negative impact on women's participation in formal and informal decision making. Discrimination against women is one of the factors that limit their full participation in development processes. According to the World Bank (2013), the majority of them are poor, more than 60% live below the poverty line.

To date, several women’s and vulnerable people's rights organizations are advocating for women-friendly constitutions and legal frameworks and financial inclusion of women for their development.


## Policy and Legal Framework

Nigeria has developed a national gender policy (NGP) to replace an erstwhile National Policy on Women in actuality.
- Eliminate barriers
- Advance gender equality and reduce poverty levels
- Not only economically empowering women through income earnings, but also consciously empowering them to own production assets.

Various international instruments on the protection of the reproductive health rights of women has been signed by Nigeria:
- Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR) 1948
- International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR) 1966
- Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) 1979
- The Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa


---
**Page 276**

Gender-based violence remains one of the most persistent human rights violations in Nigeria particularly in rural areas. These cases of violence are observed in homes, schools, health facilities and administrative settings. Indeed, with the insistent activities of armed groups in some parts of Nigeria, women and girls have been victims of sexual abuse, forced marriages, early and early marriages to physical, mental, and sexual assaults on women and minor female children.

According to a 2013 Nigeria Demographic and Health Survey study, nearly 30% of Nigerian women reported having experienced sexual violence in their lifetime. The 2015 Nigeria Demographic and Health Survey, the proportion of women aged 15-49 who are victims of emotional abuse has increased from 68% to 72%. This is a clear indication that there have been steps to protect women girls and vulnerable persons from violence in all its forms. It is clear that these cases of violence have increased in recent years. Indeed, there has been an increase in cases of violence against women and girls in Nigeria.

In 2018, the rate of female genital mutilation (FGM) was higher than in 2018 than in 2008 (31%) and at 2013 (25%). However, civil society organizations, women's groups, and other stakeholders have stepped up efforts to combat gender-based violence. This was the case with the project "Strengthening the Response to Sexual and Gender-Based Violence (SGBV) in Nine Nigerian States" funded by the United States Agency for International Development. Communities in these states were sensitized on SGBV.

Access to Finances

In Nigeria, there is a large and growing gender gap in financial inclusion. In fact, there is a problem of differential access to financial services between men and women. The 2018 EITI report saw financial inclusion as a driver of economic development. As a result, in 2010, the exclusion rate for women was at 53% compared with 47% for men. In 2018, this rate dropped to 49% for both populations, down from 53 percent in 2008. According to the results of the 2018 Improving Financial Innovation and Access Study, financial inclusion nationwide stood at 59.1% for women versus 67.3% for men.

As in some African countries, there is a gender gap in access to financial services in Nigeria. However, the gender gap in Nigeria is relatively smaller and has been steadily decreasing over the past 10 years with the rate of female financial inclusion increasing from 49 percent in 2008 to 53% in 2017.

Since 2009, several surveys have been conducted by Microfinance by EITI on access to financial services. The 2018 results reveal that 39.7 million (39.7% of the adult population) have access to a bank account compared with 45.6 million in 2016, representing an increase of 15.9 million people.

### Poverty

In 2014, 53% of the Nigerian population lived in rural areas where poverty was particularly acute at 44%. The recession crisis (induced by the fall in oil prices) that the country experienced in 2016 and the strong demographic growth exceeding the growth of the real GDP have plunged again some large cities into poverty. In addition, the increase in the number of small and medium-sized companies producing bananas, maize, cassava, cocoa, groundnuts, and the installation of large industries in petrochemicals, Nigeria obtained a strong growth of its GDP (in purchasing power parity) from 2013 to 2016. However, this growth was not evenly distributed across all regions: in 2015, the GDP per capita was $948 in Lagos and $700 in Osun, while it was only $391.5 in Katsina.

However, women remain the most affected by poverty. According to the study "Poverty Among Women in Nigeria - Psychological and Economic Perspective" conducted in southwestern Nigeria, women account for over 60% of the poorest people in the country. With over 87 million people in extreme poverty, Nigeria has about 32 million people living with extreme poverty (IMF, 2018). Furthermore, according to the World Bank data, about 4 out of 5 people are poor or extremely poor today in line, or about 80 million people, while Nigeria aspires to lift its 67% of its people out of poverty by 2030. However, achieving this goal remains difficult because the country has gone through more than less compared to other countries in Africa and the world, shocks and disasters that disproportionately affect the poor in Nigeria. A difficult situation for women with difficult access to the financial system and to this is added the COVID-19 crisis.

### Unequal Participation in decision making

In recent years, Nigeria has made significant progress in human development indicators. Nigeria has long been a patriarchal society, where social and legal organization is based on men holding authority over women. This situation has led to the exclusion of women from public life and efforts to eliminate gender inequalities that hinder women's participation in the country's economic activities. At times, women are simply relegated to the background, with reduced power to act and decide in all areas - economic, social security. According to the results of the Promoting Women

# Rwanda

## Demographics and human development

Rwanda is a landlocked country in East Africa with an estimated population of 13,276,517 of which over half are under age 18. Covering an area of only 26,338 km², the country has one of the highest population densities in Africa at 538 inhabitants per km² and an annual population growth rate of 2.5% (World Bank, 2021).

In terms of human development, the country has made excellent progress as between 1990 and 2019, its Human Development Index increased from 0.248 to 0.543, an increase of 119%. Despite this progress, the country is classified in the low human development category, ranking 160 out of 189 countries and territories.

## Education

Rwanda has long linked the modernization of the education system to the country's development process. In fact, over the years, the education budget has been increasing while the budget for other sectors such as social protection, health, public financial management, water and electricity also contribute significantly to the country’s development.

The government has allocated 184 billion RWF to various public institutions that indirectly contribute to education.

## Employment

Considered one of the fastest growing economies in the world in recent years, Rwanda has clearly demonstrated its quality in socio-economic development. Already in 2007, the country achieved the regional average age dependency ratio (53%) and the national average age dependency ratio (48%). This allowed Rwanda to move from a very poor country to a middle-income country with employment of all adults aged between 15-64 years old. In this category, the proportion of women employed more than 30% or (643,749) of the total population. Within this category, the proportion of women is approximately 31.8% of the active population, although this rate is down from 51.8% in 2019.

According to the International Labor Organization (ILO), 2009 Country report, less than 50% of the Rwandan working age population is employed in the formal sector and the average age dependency ratio is only 43.7%. In addition, unemployment in 2019 affects more than 15% of the population. The three sectors that employ most people are Agriculture (68%), Trade and Services (15%) including hotels and restaurants (14%) and Construction (13%). The infrastructure sector such as transport, telecommunication, energy (electricity, gas, water) and manufacturing, together employ only 10.7% of the working population.

Despite the orientation of investments in these infrastructures, they are for the moment less job creators compared to the other sectors, the vulnerability of some sectors, such as transport and industry, to the impacts of climate change could explain their low capacity to generate sustainable employment. However, the government has already taken steps that are needed to address both the urgency of climate change and the country's economic development.

Rwanda is a country vulnerable to the impact of climate change. The agricultural sector is economically important for the majority of the population; however, the agriculture sector and the manufacturing industry, are also affected and sometimes stopped. This situation naturally impacts the employment sector, which is reflected in the low contribution of these sectors to the employment of the active population. On the other hand, investments made by the government in projects to make infrastructure resilient could contribute to the creation of sustainable employment.

In the aftermath of the political crisis that Rwanda experienced several years ago to rebuild the country's infrastructure were undertaken; in the health sector considerable investments have been made to improve the health infrastructure, particularly in the country's major cities. As a result, maternal and child health has improved considerably over the past two decades and has exceeded the MDG targets. The number of children under five who died from malaria decreased by 70% between 2005 and 2016; the number of children dying from all causes also decreased by 48%. Similarly, the number of children born with low birth weight decreased by over 30%, while the number of children born with severe malnutrition decreased by almost half. The number of children who died before their fifth birthday has decreased by more than 50% since 2000; and child mortality decreased by over 70% over the same period[1]. Thanks to its rapid economic growth and strong development potential, Rwanda has improved the livelihoods of its people at all levels. Life expectancy has increased from 49 years in 2000 to 68.4 years in 2015.

Energy, transport, and telecommunications

Despite the rapid growth of the Rwandan economy in recent years, the population's access to electricity remains low. Indeed, according to World Bank data, only 44.6% of the Rwandan population has access to electricity in 2020. The total installed capacity of Rwanda's power grid is estimated at 153 MW (as of December 2020) of Gicye. So the total installed capacity of the country's electricity production has increased from 228,418 MWh to 296,373 MWh in 2020.

In the transport sector, Rwanda has about 44,671 km of national road network, of which 1,973 km is paved. However, national roads account for 72% of the total. Similarly, the country has at least 97% of its paved national road network in good condition[2]. As of 2018, Rwanda has three airports (Kigali, Butare and Huye), one national airport (Gitarama) and two international airports (Kigali and Kigembe).

Through a partnership with the private sector, Rwanda has strengthened its telecommunications infrastructure, including the installation of 7,000 km of fiber optics and 4G LTE coverage of 95% of the territory [1].

### Socio-cultural

Rwandan society is characterized by a patriarchal social structure that underlies unequal social power relations between men and women, boys and girls. In fact, men have naturally assumed a form of dominance over women in the household and community. These gender inequalities were not considered unjust, but as a social normality that especially women had to respect. However, women have long played a largely dominant role in Rwandan society, and certain positive trends are now being observed with regard to the empowerment of women [3].

Indeed, women played a central role in the management of household resources and participated in decision-making at various levels.

### Legal framework

Rwanda has long been committed to promoting equality and equity among all Rwandans by ensuring that the vulnerable, socially and historically disadvantaged, especially women and children, are given the same opportunities as the rest of the population. According to Article 16 of the Constitution of the Republic of Rwanda, men and women have equal rights in civil and political matters, equal rights and freedoms; discrimination based on ethnic origin, skin color or race, gender, economic categories, economic status, physical or mental disability are prohibited and punishable by law.

To achieve its objective in implementing the 2010 National Gender Policy, Rwanda has also put in place a number of measures to ensure that women's rights are respected. These include:

- Organic Budget Law No° 12/2013, instituting gender responsive budgeting: This law applies accountability measures for gender resource responsive allocation in all sectors;

Gender based violence

In Rwanda, gender-based violence (GBV) is widespread throughout the country and remains one of the problems that hinders not only the well-being of the population, but also the development of the country. According to a UN study conducted in 2019, 35% of girls aged 18 years are victims of sexual violence before the age of 15. According to the Rwanda Bureau of Investigation's analysis report, the number of GBV cases recorded by the police in 2019 was 47,600 and 10,842 GBV-related crimes were recorded in 2019 and 12,637 in 2020; the most cited being child detention, assault and domestic violence.

Access to Finances

Despite the country’s openness to large-scale investment, Rwanda has a low rate of bank penetration. However, up to 93% of the Rwandan population now has access to financial products and services through mobile money accounts (e.g., M-Pesa). The number of people with mobile money accounts rose from 10 million in 2012 (72%) and 2014 (89%) [15], financial inclusion is on the rise in the country. The country has taken advantage of the rapid growth of mobile money to provide access to financial services, savings, and money transfers from their phones. The population now has a secure solution for storing cash, even for those working in the informal economy. On the other hand, the rate of formal financial inclusion is still low compared with other countries in sub-Saharan Africa. Rwanda has made progress through several initiatives, programs and policies. Considered an indispensable tool for achieving its development and poverty reduction objectives, the country aims to reach 100% formal financial inclusion by 2035 [16]. The government has also been working on the expansion of mobile money account ownership in Rwanda. It should be noted that the proportion of men is significantly higher than women (74% versus 26%) and that women are more likely to use informal mechanisms much more to conduct banking transactions; 245 for women versus 178 for men.

In summary, the gender gap in financial inclusion in Rwanda narrowed from 14 percent in 2014 to 11 percent in 2017, according to the Global Findex database.


Povety

Thanks to its growing economies, Rwanda has been able to reduce inequality and poverty supported by numerous investments in several areas including infrastructure and some changes in the employment structure. The country is also working on the implementation of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) in addition to Vision 2050 allowing the country to contribute to economic development at all levels and social welfare of the population. According to World Bank data, the multidimensional poverty rate has decreased since 2013 from 46.8% to 27.9%. In rural areas, the country is still in the low-income category.

Despite the reduction in poverty rates, there are significant regional disparities and pockets of intense poverty that affect about 27% of the urban population while, in rural areas, the impact is much greater, with up to 83% of the rural population living in poverty [16]. In addition, the country has difficulty achieving the objective of the National Poverty Reduction Program, while the rate of unemployment remains high. The government has therefore decided to focus its efforts on the Sustainable Development Goals in Rwanda labor market issues, up to 44% of workers are living
