In spite of the numerous laws and strategies in favor of women, they face enormous difficulties regarding their participation in the decision-making bodies of local and institutional governments. The lack of representation of women in these institutions is due to the existence of informal networks. These networks support and reproduce the social systems that local governments use.

Nevertheless, the constitution guarantees representation in Parliament for particular categories of Rwandans, including young women, women with disabilities and historically marginalized people. Clearly, the country's constitution contains at least 30% of quotas for women in all decision-making bodies. In spite of this quota system, women are still underrepresented in local government bodies. In men in decision-making bodies in Rwanda, women represent more than 50% of the population; they are part of the decision-making bodies. # 4.17 Sierra Leone

## Demographics and human development

Sierra Leone’s HDI value for 2019 is 0.463—which puts the country in the low human development category—ranking it 152 out of 189 countries and territories. The rank is shared with Burkina Faso.

Between 1990 and 2019, Sierra Leone’s HDI value increased from 0.287 to 0.463, increasing 57.5 percent. Sierra Leone’s life expectancy at birth increased by 16.1 years, mean years of schooling increased by 10.1 years and expected years of schooling increased by 9.2 years.

Sierra Leone’s GNI per capita decreased by about 6.0 percent between 1990 and 2019.

Sierra Leone’s 2019 HDI of 0.452 is below the average of 0.313 for countries in the low human development category, but above the average of 0.387 for sub-Saharan African countries. On this basis, Sierra Leone is compared with Burundi and Gambia, with HDIs ranked 185 and 178.

The female HDI value for Sierra Leone is 0.423 compared to 0.479 for males.

Sierra Leone has a GII of 0.344, ranking it 135 out of 142 countries in the 2019 Index. In Sierra Leone, 83 percent of children under five years old are immunized, and 20.1 percent of adult women have reached at least a secondary level of education compared to 33.0 percent of their male counterparts aged 15–19.

Female participation in the labour market is 57.3 percent compared to 85.8 for males.


## Education

The Government of Sierra Leone considers the issue of narrowing the gender gap as a key priority in its development programming activities. The Government introduced free education for girls in public primary schools and has made efforts to promote female literacy.

90 percent of Sierra Leoneans should read and write while their development programming activities. The Statistical Abstract 2018 shows that 54 percent of women aged 15–19 years old are literate, compared with 63 (51 percent) in Sierra Leone are literate but that men make up a much higher proportion of that number [39 percent] than women [44 percent]. Data also shows the gender disparity of male and female students at the primary level. In 2018, there is a difference as they move from the primary level to the junior secondary (2:1 for males and 5:7 for females). There is a drop for both sexes from primary level to junior secondary school. The drop is even sharper when women reach senior secondary school. This gender gap in education is also reflected by the low rate of girls in Sierra Leone face in their education. Young girls’ education is shrouded in challenges related to early marriage, teenage pregnancy, and parents not being ready to support girls in their educational pursuits.


## Employment

The 2015 census results showed that there is a slightly higher proportion of women in the working population of Sierra Leone (ages 15–64), with 54.4 percent female compared to 48.4 percent male. However, there is a highly significant proportion of males in the under 14 years age-dependent population but slightly more women in the 60 years plus age-dependent population.


---
**Page 286**
---

Page 282

For every 100,000 live births, 1120 women die from pregnancy-related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 112.8 births per 1,000. Overall, the sanitation situation is more or less critical compared to some other countries in West Africa. The country has a high prevalence of malaria, which is related to an improved source of drinking water. This situation is much more critical in rural areas, where more than 31% of the population does not have access to drinking water, compared to only 8% in urban areas.

Moreover, climatic phenomena such as floods and tornadoes threaten the health of the population and particularly those living in rural areas. In 2017, for example, heavy rains, a mudslide and flash floods destroyed several villages in the Western Area Rural District, including parts of the Freetown Urban Area, Western Area Rural and Western Area Urban districts, as well as Freetown low-lying communities. In some areas, health centers were affected, which further aggravated the health situation of these populations.

All in all, the country has the highest maternal mortality rate in the world and one of the highest under-five mortality rates. For every 10 live births, more than 31 mothers and 111 children under the age of five die each year. The government is working with international organizations such as UNICEF, including the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation, have provided technical and financial support to help the country improve its health situation.

The government has also established a National Health Commission that will establish a national public health institute that will serve as the central structure for public health functions.

Energy, transport and telecommunications

Less than 10% of the population has access to electricity. About 87% of energy usage in the country is biomass, firewood, and charcoal. More than 80% of the population uses fuelwood or charcoal for cooking and heating. Women are responsible for collecting these resources from distant locations. They have to walk long distances, which leads to fatigue and endangers their physical security and health.

In Sierra Leone, only 13.6 percent of persons, 10 years and older have access to the Internet; the proportion of males (18%) with access is higher than females (10%). The results clearly show that most women and men have no access to the Internet. The ICT policy launched in January 2011 focuses on, among other things, increasing awareness about the use and benefits of ICT for women, create resource centers nationwide to train and encourage greater access to ICT for women; set up internet access points throughout the country targeting women and girls; provide training opportunities for women and girls; establish partnerships with business and entrepreneurial opportunities and finally, disseminate national, regional and international policies, conventions and activities on gender equality and women's empowerment.

Socio-cultural

Sierra Leone is a highly patriarchal society where power it held solely by men. In fact, the country has strong institutional gender inequalities, which are the result of discriminatory customary practices with


---
**Page 287**
---

respect to marriage, property rights, inheritance, civil action, and sexual offenses, Sierra Leone has "one of the highest rates of female genital mutilation" in the world, with 90 percent of girls and women between the ages of 15 and 49 undergoing the violent procedure. There are discrete societies in the country that preserve this practice as a cultural tradition. The government has not yet enacted any law to provide protection for the community. Genital mutilation procedures "without anesthesia" are often performed by female community members using knives, razors, and even shards of glass.

### Policy and Legal Framework

The GoSL's approach and response to gender equality are informed and influenced by local and international commitments and frameworks. At the local level, the Truth and Reconciliation Commission (TRC) was established in 2003 as a result of the Sierra Leone Civil War. The TRC is responsible for all of its statutory and customary laws that discriminate against women. At the international level, Sierra Leone ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR), the Optional Protocol to CEDAW, the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights, the African Charter on the Rights and Welfare of the Child, the African Charter on Civil and Political Rights (CCPR), Child Rights Convention (CRC), International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR), the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and People's Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol), and the National Action Plan for the Beijing Platform for Action, among others.

Sierra Leone’s policy framework for promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment is tied mostly to the national policies: the Gender Mainstreaming Policy and the National Policy on the Advancement of Women, both adopted by Parliament in 2000. The National Policy on the Advancement of Women aims to create an enabling environment to improve women's status and participation in all spheres of life. It also seeks to ensure that women are fully involved in national development objectives in the country. It emphasizes Government’s commitment to gender-responsive employment and sees to strengthen and provide a legal basis for gender-oriented sectoral policies.

### Gender based violence

Gender-based violence (GBV) is a major and urgent concern in Sierra Leone. Sierra Leone is a country that has experienced periods of conflict resulting in forced displacement and family separation. This has influenced the nature and extent of gender-based violence in the post-conflict phase of the country. However, the state's support and consideration for women is insignificant in addressing GBV. Women are disproportionately affected by the consequences of sexual violence experienced during the war and the persistent discrimination they face.

According to the 2019 Sierra Leone Demographic and Health Survey, 42 percent of women aged 15–49 reported having been physically or sexually abused at some point in their lives; while in 2018 this percentage was 19.8 percent[1], suggesting an increase in the incidence of gender-based violence. The absence of a law, legal text, or mechanism to prosecute perpetrators of violence against women has contributed to a culture of impunity for crimes committed against women.

# Access to Finances

Gender mainstreaming and women’s empowerment are important objectives of Sierra Leone’s national plan. Indeed, Sierra Leone is among the first countries in Africa to include a stand-alone policy on gender equality. Nonetheless, despite progress made over the past decade, most of the gains for women in Sierra Leonean society have been concentrated at the household level. At the macroeconomic and private sector levels, women’s access to economic assets remains low. Sierra Leone is one of the poorest countries in Africa with a per capita GDP of $180 (World Bank 2017). Women are underrepresented in the formal economy, which is dominated by men at all levels, from the informal sector to private enterprises. At the household level, women’s access to economic assets remains low. At the macroeconomic and private sector levels, women’s access to economic assets remains low.

# Poverty

Poverty is widespread in Sierra Leone. The country's first Poverty Reduction Strategy Paper (PRSP) of 2005-2007 states that 70% of the population are poor and 34% food poor. As such, they cannot afford a basic diet. Additionally, poverty was identified as a rural phenomenon, with the rural areas accounting for almost 73% of the country's poor, exceeding their population share of 66%. Urban poverty is concentrated in Freetown, where it affects about one-third of its population (UNDP 2015). The PRSP also states that 48.9% of Sierra Leoneans live below the national poverty line of $1.25 per day and 37.5% are food poor. According to the World Bank, 60% of the population lives on less than $1 a day (AIDB 2009). Although the GoSL has concentrated its development efforts since the end of the civil war in 2002, poverty remains widespread and deep-rooted. The country’s poverty profile shows that the main poverty indicators are inadequate food, poor housing, poor health care infrastructure and mortality matters, high illiteracy, limited access to clean water, and lack of money.

# Unequal Participation in Decision Making

In Sierra Leone, girls and women have limited access to educational opportunities, decision-making, and government compared to boys and men. Indeed, women hold only 12.3 percent of parliamentary seats in 2011 (Societal barriers are among the obstacles that prevent women from entering politics). According to a UN Women report on women’s participation in decision-making, governance, and politics, 70% of women said that their region disregarded their participation in political life. Women make up 32 percent of the total number of elected representatives in Sierra Leone (UN Women, 2015). Despite this low visibility, active participation, decision-making, and representation in elected and appointed positions remain very low compared to men. In addition to these challenges, there is also a lack of


---
**Page 289**
---

Page 285

economic independence, high illiteracy, and entrenched customs and traditions in social and sometimes administrative practices.

In the majority of Sierra Leonean communities, women were not allowed in some community meetings and excluded from decision making. This is the case in Gima where the notion of women as leaders was not part of the culture of this community. When in 2015, the community began working with One Village Partners, women held only two leadership positions; as a result, women rarely attended meetings. In 2016, the community decided to hold a women's meeting and elected three women leaders. This was the first time that women had been elected to lead their community and made decisions on behalf of the community. However, a new model of leadership that includes women, youth, and community members outside of the ruling council is beginning to emerge in some communities across the country where women leaders are becoming more vocal in decision-making bodies.

# 4.18 Togo

## Demographics and human development

According to the UN survey on the Togolese household's livelihood (ENCGA-TOGO, 2016; 2019) data, Togo’s population is estimated to be 7,353,994 inhabitants, of which 52.3 percent are women. This population is very young, with those under 15 years representing 43.5 percent of the total population and those over 64 are 3.4 percent. With an annual growth rate of 2.67 percent in 2021, this population will increase to 8,920,000 by 2050. The fertility rate is estimated at 4.8 children per woman (UN DESA, 2019). Togo's HDI value for 2019 is 0.516—which puts the country in the low human development category—positioned between 189 countries and territories. Between 2000 and 2019, Togo’s HDI value increased from 0.436 to 0.516 (UN DESA, 2019). Between 2000 and 2019, the proportion of children under five years increased by 5.2 years, mean years of schooling increased by 2.0 years, and expected years of schooling increased from 7 to 8.4 years (UN DESA, 2019).

Gender Inequality Index value is 0.537, ranking Togo 145 out of 162 countries in the 2019 index. In Togo, 16.5 percent of parliamentary seats are held by women, and 27.6 percent of adult women have reached secondary education compared to 54.4 percent of their male counterparts. For every 100,000 live births, 396.0 women had a pregnancy-related causes, and the adolescent birth rate is 89.1 per 1,000 women ages 15–19.

## Education

The Integrated Regional Survey on Employment and the Informal Sector (ERI-EBI: Togo 2017) shows a literacy rate of 64 percent at the national level and highlights a big gender gap between men and women regardless of age group. Men are more literate than women at the national level, with 73.5 percent of men and 49.2 percent of women literate. While most women and men went to primary school, only 18.6 percent of women completed secondary education compared to 30.5 percent of men who visited a secondary school. This is true for only 28% of women. Women are much less represented in tertiary education, accounting for only 49% of male persons benefiting from tertiary education.

## Employment

According to the ERI-EBI Togo 2017 survey report, the majority of Togolese (56.8 percent) find themselves in informal sector jobs and more employment vulnerability (87.4%), i.e., workers for own account or unpaid family workers. The unemployment rate is estimated at 39.5 percent compared to 78.9 for men. (Human Development Report, 2019). The logartithmic salary ratio is 2.25 times higher than among men (36.8 percent) with the low prohibiting discrimination in employment and occupation law. Women are more represented in the informal sector compared to men. Women are more represented in the informal sector where the Government did not effectively enforce the law, it confronts many challenges such as: for example, the salary rate below the minimum wage affects women (73.8) more than men (9.8).

Under traditional law, which applies to most women, a husband legally might restrict his wife's freedom to work and control her earnings. Gender discrimination in employment and occupation existed even though there is no specific law in Togo that prohibits it. Women are underrepresented in the public sector, which is rigid and uncompromising and employs only about 9.7 percent of the workforce.


---

addition, there are three main areas of employment in Togo. These are the agricultural sector, the informal sector and the modern sector (public and private).

### Energy, transport, and telecommunications

The role of access to electricity in Togo is in progression (from 17 percent in 2000 to 35 percent in 2018) but with huge disparities between urban areas (access rate = 87 percent) and rural (access rate = 7 percent). The country has a total installed capacity of 496 MW, which is far below the national demand. Capacity. In the energy sector, women are less represented than men. Indeed: statistics show that, within the Compagnie Electrique Béninique du Togo (CEBT), only 140 out of 818 employees (17%) are women.

According to the National Development Plan 2018-2022, the ICTs is confronted with the inadequacy of communication infrastructures and equipment in relation to new conditions based. Among other things, the country has a total installed capacity of 496 MW, which is far below national demand. The State infrastructure are in a state of advanced deterioration as well as equipment are obsolete and underperforming. In this context, Togo must invest heavily in ICTs to achieve its objective of a logistics hub of excellence and a first-class business center in the Sub-Saharan region, in particular through the improvement of existing infrastructure and multimodal connectivity and ICT is one of the set priorities of this agenda.

### Socio-cultural

According to the National Equity and Equality Policy (PNEEG), Togolese society is characterized by a patriarchal social structure based on unequal social and power relations between the sexes. Traditional customs, which are still deeply rooted in Togo, reinforce these inequalities. The man, head of the family, embodies the authority within the household. He establishes the rules, ensures the control and management of family assets, decides on the distribution of land assets, family and plans its use. It is more than a simple decision-making process: it is a way to ensure that his wife and children owe him respect and obedience. The woman, mother, and wife remain the first assistant responsible for household chores and child care. She has no say in decisions concerning the management of the domesticated. It also has the role of supporting man in his social mission at the family level, taking charge of everything that contributes to accomplishing this mission, including executing these decisions. The woman is therefore a passive partner who does not have any decision-making power and has to replace him in the realization of exercise its prerogatives. She becomes de facto "head of the family" when her husband leaves for work or study abroad and the resulting privations. These sociocultural factors weigh heavily on women's status in the family and in society, which is reflected in their limited ability to make decisions and participate in the management of public and private affairs of the community with the same opportunities as men.

### Policy and Legal Framework

In Togo, the principle according to which "men and women are equal before the law" enshrined in Article 11 of the Constitution is achieved through various actions combining the strengthening the

Gender-based violence

In Togo, violence against women and girls is multifaceted. They include domestic violence, sexual harassment, forced marriage, early female genital mutilation, food prohibition, widowhood rites, internment in convents, etc. The study conducted on gender-based violence (GBV) in 2019 reveals that out of 1,357 child victims of violence, abuse and exploitation, 862 (63.5 percent) are girls and 495 (36.5 percent) boys. Among the victims of GBV, women and girls are particularly affected; in particular women and children, The Togolese government has taken several initiatives to promote gender equality and reduce violence against women and girls. In this regard, the number of gender-based violence cases decreased in 2020 compared to 2019, women and female minors are still the most affected. In fact, of the 389 cases of violence recorded in 2020, 65.5 percent were committed against women.

Since 2015, Togo has put in place gender equity and equality coordination mechanisms, community justice structures and gender-based violence management structures for the implementation of the national strategy for the promotion of gender equality and the fight against violence against women. The egalitarian penal code is a land code that sweeps away the ambivalence of customary and modern laws that penalize women; a national strategy for integrating gender into development policies and programs, as well as the establishment of a National Commission on Gender Equality. In addition, the country has put in place various sectoral programs that encourage women to report cases of

# Access to Finances

According to 2015 World Bank statistics on Togo, women represent more than half of the country’s total population. However, women's access to the labor market and to bank financing for their development remains limited. The Togolese banking sector has since developed considerably and the country has a number of microfinance institutions (MFIs) that have contributed significantly to their financial inclusion. Thus, since 2014, Togo has implemented some social development instruments including the National Fund for Inclusive Finance (FIFI) which is currently deployed in 35 communes. The FIFI aims at providing access to credit and savings services through Access to Financial Services for Youth (AASE). Access to Financial Services for Farmers (AFSEF), Access to Financial Services for Women (AFSW), Access to Financial Services for Small and Medium Enterprises (AFSEM), Access to Financial Services for the Poor (APSEF), Seasonal Product (SP), Special Support Product (SSP), Formalization Support Product (FPS). Indeed, within the framework of the PAFIV, the country benefited from a financing of about 1.334 billion euros from the African Development Bank.

# Poverty

The incidence of poverty is lower in male-headed households than in female-headed households: it is 54.8 percent and 60.2 percent respectively. The proportion of poor households decreased between 2011 and 2015 (from 59.4 percent to 54.4 percent), while female-headed households are more affected by poverty than male-headed households. The government believes that poverty remains far too high in the country, in rural areas, where more than 60 percent of households lived below the poverty line in 2015. Moreover, women are the most vulnerable, as they have less access to economic opportunities.

In Togo, as in some countries in the sub-region, the level of poverty is twice as high in rural zones (38.8%) as in urban areas (19%). The country has a very low agricultural productivity and some periods of heavy rainfall create flooding phenomena that ravage fields at the beginning of the harvest season, which affects rural areas much more during the dry and rainy seasons. These factors aggravate the already difficult situation of rural populations, especially women.

# Unequal Participation in decision taking

In Togo, women represent more than 50.2% of the population in 2020; however, they are less represented in decision-making positions at all levels of society and in public life. Despite the measures that have been put in place by the country, women encounter difficulties, which not only limit their access to certain activities and economic opportunities but also prevent them from participating effectively in the political and economic context of the country. Women have long been poorly represented in the country's decision-making bodies, although there have been gradual improvements in recent years. Indeed, the proportion of women

The proportion has risen from 1.2% in 1995 to 18.7% in 2020[6]. Bertil still, based on data from the World Bank, the proportion of women in the workforce will represent 48.8% in 2030, whereas it was less than 47% in 1995.

Since 2011, Togo has developed a document on the National Policy for Gender Equity and Equality which stipulates that women should be represented at all levels of public administration, including in decision-making positions, and their full participation in decision-making at all levels of the country's development process. Despite the adoption of this policy, women are still underrepresented in public administration and decision-making bodies. Women are poorly represented in decision-making bodies. Moreover, 39.4% of wage earners in the non-agricultural sector are women.

The proportion of seats held by women in parliament increased from 15.4% in 2014 to 18.7% in 2015[6]. These proportions are far from the MDG target of 30%, but the country has made remarkable progress. In 2015, 17.4% of women were appointed as ministers and 9.5% of women were appointed as vice-ministers. Women also hold positions in addition to the President. Key ministerial posts traditionally held by men are now headed by women.

In Togo, women play a leading role in the development of part-time activities where they are promised official employment. However, this is not always respected and women are often employed on a contractual basis or as unpaid workers. In 2015, 783 agents, 67% of whom were women, or about 9% of the staff[7]. At the government office (PSE), 49 women occupied positions in the civil service and 10 women held ministerial posts. Women also hold important positions such as the head of the Port of Lomé, the Medical-Social Center and the General Administration. The head of the Study and Development Department is a woman engineer in Civil Engineering[8].

# 4.19 Zambia

## Demographics and human development

Zambia, a southern African state, and a member of the Commonwealth shares its borders with 8 countries including the Democratic Republic of Congo, Tanzania, Malawi, Mozambique, Zimbabwe, Botswana, Namibia and Angola. In 2021, the Zambian population is estimated at 18,925,657 people.

In terms of gender inequality, the country is making considerable progress in promoting the rights of girls and women. However, much remains to be done to promote gender equality and achieve gender parity in education, health, employment, and political participation. The country has seen a decline from 0.137 in 2017 to 0.540 in 2018; the report records a slight decrease in its gender inequality index which places it in 146th place among 189 countries according to the Gender Inequality Index.

When it comes to health, Zambia is still far off as a disconnection compared to their male counterparts. Women in some parts of the country continue to experience high risks during pregnancy or childbirth suffer from the lack of health infrastructure, the lack of qualified and specialized personnel, and poor access to essential medicines. The maternal mortality ratio (MMR) increased from 111 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2018, while the pregnancy-related mortality rate (PRMR) was 276 deaths per 100,000 women.

## Education

Zambia has made considerable progress in education, as enrolment and completion of primary schooling are almost universal. However, the country faces some challenges, particularly in terms of relevance, efficiency, equity and the shortage of qualified teachers. Clearly, women are disadvantaged compared to men with regard to access to education, their participation in gender equity in schooling, to the quality of school and post training. Zambia has embarked on the process of teacher professionalization by introducing a new framework for setting professional standards for its teachers, with the aim of improving the efficiency and performance of schools.

According to the 2018 Demographic and Health Survey report, the majority of Zambians have no formal education or only some primary education. Indeed, 60 percent of women and 54 percent of men aged 6 years and older have no or only some primary education. In 2017, the net secondary school enrolment rate was 38.9 percent for girls compared to 48.5 percent for boys; the net enrolment rate in primary school decreased from 90.4 percent in 2016 to 87.9 percent in 2017.

## Employment

The statics of the Gender Inequality Index report show that there is an increase in inequalities that exist between women's participation in the labour market. In Zambia, the employment sector is dominated by men; while women make up about 50.3 per cent of the country’s population. In 2019, women accounted for only 48.6 percent of the total employed population compared to only 24.7% of women participate in the labour market, compared to 44.8% of men. Women's participation in traditionally male-dominated industries remains remarkably low, at 23 per cent while their representation in the public sector and in the formal economy, women’s labor force participation rate is 78.3 percent, compared to 95.0 percent for men.


---
**Page 296**
---

# Renewable energy and transport infrastructure projects, including roads and rail voices, will increase the involvement of women, girls and vulnerable people in the country’s workforce.

## Energy, transport, and telecommunications

Zambia has extensive energy, transportation, industrial, and telecommunications infrastructure. However, Zambia's economic infrastructure is dominated by the industrial sector, particularly the extractive industries. The majority of Zambia's electricity generation capacity comes from non-renewable energy (coal and wind) are available in Zambia. However, only 31% of the country’s population has access to electricity; in Zambia, the majority rural population is particularly energy poor.

Zambia has been mainstreaming and monitoring gender in its development policies, particularly across the transport sector. In 2018, a Gender Mainstreaming Assessment was conducted by the Ministry of Energy has developed a gender mainstreaming strategy and action plan, which will inform the Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) process.

In Zambia, as in most African countries, women make up more than half of all road users. However, they are less involved in transport infrastructure construction and redevelopment projects. Gender mainstreaming is essential to ensure that the needs and interests of women are considered when improving rural road transport on an impact is significant. In recent years, Zambia has faced significant challenges including the impact of climate economic conditions, and poorly maintained roads. The Ministry of Energy has developed a gender mainstreaming strategy and action plan which will inform the Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) process to address these challenges.

### Socio-cultural

A predominantly patriarchal society whose customs and traditions often prohibit women's empowerment in Zambia, gender inequalities are perceived at all levels, and at all levels of the country’s development.

In Zambia, there are certain socio-cultural beliefs that limit women's access to socio-economic activities and are the root causes of gender-based violence. In the majority of the country, women are expected to meet their family demands in accordance with societal norms, regardless of their personal health and choice.

### Policy and Legal framework

To promote gender equality and empowerment of women and girls and the vulnerable, Zambia has strengthened its policy and legal frameworks. Indeed, Zambia has been working with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) to strengthen its policy and legal frameworks legislation. As regards the legal framework, the Constitutional (Amendment) Act No 2 of 2016 confirms the equal value of women and men. Indeed, the country signed a new constitution in February 2015 which guarantees equality between men and women. The law also provides for fair elected and appointed positions, while recognizing equal rights and establishing a Gender Equality Commission.

A number of other progressive laws exist in the country and are among others:

- The Gender Equity and Equality Act (GEQA) of 2015, which aims to incorporate into domestic law certain provisions relating to women's rights and gender equality contained in regional, continental and international instruments to which Zambia is a party.


---
**Page 297**
---

The National Gender Policy formulated in 2014 is the main framework for the implementation of gender equality commitments. The policy also provides for equal opportunities for women and men to participate in and contribute actively to national development.

In 2005, the penal code was revised, making penalties for (sexual) violence against women and children more severe.

However, effective implementation of these laws and policies has been slow and an acceleration of their implementation is desired.


Gender based violence

Gender-based violence is used by all social strata of the Zambian population and all age groups. According to police nation 1 annual report on gender-based violence, disaggregated annual data shows that 3,301 child victims of abuse across the country, accounting for 28.5% of all victims of GBV in Zambia. In addition, 47.9% of women aged between 15 and 49 years have been raped more than 77.6 per case in total, more than 20,000 cases of GBV were recorded in 2021. More than 38% of the victims are under the age of 18 years old. The majority of the victims aged between 15 and 14 have experienced sexual violence 183.In short, women and girls are more vulnerable to the growing trend of GBV due to exclusion from services and opportunities.

In addition to access to justice for victims of GBV, there is a need for swift judicial resolution of cases. Zambia has established expedited courts for gender-based violence cases.


Access to finances

Since the 1990s, Zambia has undergone extensive financial sector reforms. However, the expected benefits of a market-based banking system have not materialized. Indeed, more than 46 percent of adults in Zambia are unbanked and do not use any formal financial services at all time. About 60 percent of adults who do have access do not use them, according to Zambia's 2017-2022 National Financial Inclusion Strategy document.

Also, the majority of women in rural areas still rely on remote rural cash or barter, or use unregulated and unsecured channels, to make payments and store savings. Just as globally, women’s financial inclusion is a top priority for Zambia. Indeed, women's access to banking services is critical because it empowers economically empower women and supports several Sustainable Development Goals in the country.

In 2015, women's financial inclusion in Zambia was 30%. Through the use of mobile money, the gender gap in access to financial services has been reduced and up to 86% of women are financially included.


Poverty

The impacts of climate change are strongly felt among the Zambian population, especially in rural areas. In addition, human action such as deforestation combined with the negative effects of

According to World Bank data, poverty rates among Zambia’s rural population were stubbornly high, reaching 78 percent of the population and disproportionately affecting adolescent girls and women.

Unequal Participation in Decision Making

The statics of the Gender Inequality Index report show that there is an increase in the inequalities that exist in the number of women parliamentarians. Only 14.9% of parliamentary seats are held by women, which means that women make up more than half of the population in Zambia, they are poorly represented in decision-making and accountability bodies in public office. In 2016, the country signed a new constitution to help ensure equal rights for women in elected and appointed positions while recognizing equal rights and establishing a Gender Equality Commission.

In Zambia, many ethnic groups follow a matrilineal system, where women own the land and pass it on to the maternal line. However, ownership does not necessarily grant access, use, and control of resources. Women are also underrepresented in decision-making bodies that manage public facilities within local communities. Women are very limited in their participation in the energy and industrial sectors, which are dominated by men in the mining sector or other extractive industries such as energy or heavy industries. Many Zambian women have better qualifications; however, they are not considered for higher positions in institutions and strategic positions.

V. GENDER DISPARITIES TO INFRASTRUCTURAL ACCESS AND SERVICES AND CONTRIBUTION OF THE PROJECT

Owing to growing social science and interdisciplinary research on the effects of infrastructure disruption in time of disasters [10], it is possible to understand gender and socioeconomic considerations of climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction goals. It's still possible to understand how infrastructure disruption can affect populations and economies, considering gender-specific impacts.

Such research has established the criticality of electric power and telecommunications services in advancing gender equality and the heightened vulnerability of certain population groups to infrastructure disruption. While the importance of understanding and reducing infrastructure disruptions is widely recognized, there are still gaps in knowledge about how such disruptions can lead to underinvestment in mitigating the consequences of the disruption.

When extreme events damage infrastructure, the impact materializes not only in terms of physical damages but also the socio-economic impacts. For example, when a road or bridge damage disrupts access to health facilities, women and girls from low-income households are more likely to be affected than men. In some countries from receiving urgent medical care and could result in maternal death, defined as deaths while pregnant or within 45 days of giving birth. Lack of resilient transport infrastructure can also negatively impact the ability of women and girls to access education, health services, financial services and prevent their access to more activities that generate remuneration. Access to medical facilities is critical for women's reproductive rights and opportunities. Inadequate transport systems are shown to be an enabler of gender-based violence.

The promotion of climate resilient infrastructure will help achieve universal energy access, environmental sustainability, and gender equality in the energy sector, leading to positive development outcomes. Women and girls are disproportionately affected by power outages because non-existent or regular electricity supply can considerably increase their time poverty, reducing the number of productive hours in a day and increasing the unpaid domestic work burden. In addition, women and girls are more likely than men to be exposed to indoor air pollution caused by unclean, combustible fuels for household energy, accounting for 6 out of 10 of the deaths from indoor-generated global disease.

By promoting sustainability and access to clean energy, IECR is critical to contribute to a better access for all including women and reduce gender inequalities by helping lengthen the day by one to two hours, allowing women to spend more time on productive, income-generating or leisure activities. Through this program, the role of women is recognized as key for improving electricity access.

In today's world, access and control over information increasingly symbolize empowerment. Digital communications infrastructure is instrumental in enabling knowledge sharing and creating more inclusive and empowered societies. However, within the digital divide – 32 percent of the world's

population is without access to the Internet – there is a wide gap between male and female digital communications users. There is an estimated Internet usage gap of 11 percent globally, which increases to 30 percent in low- and middle-income countries. This gender divide also extends to the use of mobile phones; in low- and middle-income countries, women are 10 percent less likely to have a mobile phone and 26 percent less likely to have a smartphone than men. The likelihood that women will be able to access digital communications technology is further reduced by multiple causes reinforcing this gender divide. The cost of digital communications technology and the significant number of women living in poverty are considered as the greatest barriers to accessing digital communications devices and services. Women are also less likely than men to have equal work and have more difficulties accessing financial assets, consequently limiting their ability to own or use any type of technology, including mobile phones.

Furthermore, there is a gender divide among young people, who make up nearly two-thirds of the world’s Internet users, and the lack of knowledge of English in rural areas, which is the primary language of the Internet, are significant obstacles to closing the digital gender gap. This program can have significant positive outcomes for women by increasing their access to information and communication technology (ICT) and technology for women. First, it can improve access to education for women and girls, giving the wide range of learning opportunities, content, and tools available on the web. Second, it can trigger new economic activities in rural areas where women are more likely than men to be involved. Third, increasing access to international markets and online service-based industries, where women are more likely than men to be involved, can help reduce poverty and increase income. Fourth, it can lead to a reduction of gender-based violence and insecurity through mobile applications where women can report unsafe areas, quickly reach emergency services, and share their position to ensure their safety. Especially in the context of COVID-19, this technology can also contribute significantly to access to emergency services. For example, gender-based violence against women has increased worldwide due to COVID-19; technology can facilitate the reporting of incidents or cases like this.

In all of ICRC’s target countries, there are major challenges and setbacks to gender equality. In addition, women are more vulnerable than men in the impact of climate change. Therefore, specific intervention strategies for each target group are needed. Across critical infrastructures, opportunities exist to improve access to information and communication technology (ICT) services equitably across all genders. In short, in the analysis of gender and socio-economic indicators, it appears that countries do not have the same characteristics in terms of gender mainstreaming at all levels of society. Therefore, there is a need for specific strategies and actions to be taken by governments to contribute effectively to the improvement of people’s living conditions and above all to reduce gender inequalities and discrimination against women and vulnerable groups.

In addition, the development of renewable energy infrastructure and the expansion of water infrastructure remain an opportunity for countries and an excellent way for better gender mainstreaming through development projects and the effective and active participation of women in the national development process.

Energy: The installation or expansion of renewable energy plants such as solar, wind, etc. offer significant potential to support the development of women-owned microenterprises, especially in rural areas. The project therefore aims to extend the electricity network to areas not covered in order to provide access to electricity for women and girls who are unable to reach these areas because they require energy such as cell phones. As a result, vulnerable populations, especially women, will be able to use this technology more easily. This is particularly important given the challenges faced by most countries. The Solar Power Plant Project requires maintenance, upkeep, cleaning and verification. Thus, the planned training programs will allow a significant participation of women and youth in order to strengthen their skills and improve their employability and to reinforce their social and economic power. In rural areas, two factors increase exposure to violence against


---
**Page 301**
---

Page 297

Transport: Through the construction or upgrading of road transport facilities, social inclusion, especially for women and girls in rural areas, will be possible thanks to economic opportunities, health care, education, and other services and infrastructure needed to improve their well-being. Finally, the agricultural community in these areas, sometimes dominated by women, will be able to access the domestic market more easily.

Industry: The industrial platform projects offer an excellent way to reduce gender inequality and especially for women and girls in rural areas. In addition to direct employment opportunities that these industrial platforms offer to women and youth, the project also encourages the establishment of cooperatives farmers and breeders to deliver products in quantity and quality. This will allow the project to be more sustainable by creating new value-added generating activities, but also reduce or eliminate the socio-cultural practices they suffer from. The industry platform projects will also contribute to the development of women's entrepreneurship and innovation. In addition, women-owned businesses will be further included in the manufacturing value chain. Thus their capacities will be strengthened to assume new roles in the transition to industry and consequently increase their share in decision-making.

### VI. RESULTS OF SURVEY THE GENDER PERCEPTIONS IN ICRF COUNTRIES

To find out the level of gender mainstreaming as well as the degree of women participation in all stages of the infrastructure life cycle, an online survey was conducted from March to June 2012 on gender and social inclusion for infrastructure projects in countries covered by the ICRF project, including Benin; Cameroon; Chad; Côte d'Ivoire; Djibouti; DRC; Gabon; Gambia; Ghana; Guinea; Kenya; Mali; Mauritania; Niger; Nigeria; Rwanda; Senegal; Sierra Leone; Togo; and Uganda.

This section presents a summary of the results of the survey, which was designed to determine the level of gender mainstreaming in infrastructure projects, particularly in transport, energy, heavy industry and telecommunications. This section completes the presentation of the gender mainstreaming level by presenting the results of the survey on women's participation in each stage of project implementation.

The survey collected information from the questionnaire sent to women's groups and associations, and officials of implementing agencies and institutions on how to mainstream gender into environmental policies, programs and projects; the role played by women's groups and associations in promoting gender equality; their participation throughout the infrastructure life cycle was collected as well as information on the national gender strategy of these countries.

It should be noted that the questionnaire is organized into different sections including project objectives and target group; control of resources and impacts of infrastructure projects; women's participation and consultation strategies; women role as decision-makers; and finally sex-disaggregated data collection. As such, the analysis covers the areas of transportation; energy; industrial platforms; and telecommunications in the context of climate change.

#### 4.1 Project objectives and target group

According to the survey, the design and implementation project recognizes that women and men have different needs and priorities when it comes of their use of infrastructure, but more often than not women's needs are not taken into account or are only partially taken into account for women. For women's groups, the constraints and obstacles to women's participation in infrastructure projects activities and access to project resources are numerous: lack of information on the project; lack of financial means; reluctance to include women in the workforce in certain sectors; lack of anti-discrimination protections; lack of gender-specific policies and benefits, etc.

According to the responses of one Sierra Leonean agency that responded to the questionnaire, the gender mainstreaming policy is not effectively implemented. Effective implementation of gender policies and programs through infrastructure projects remains problematic: the tasks performed by women are affected, particularly in the area of transport where the redesign of a road slows down or sometimes stops the progress of the project. For this reason, women's groups and promotion agencies have not been consulted and/or involved in the decision-making process concerning the "location and timeframe" of the work.

#### 6.2 Resource control and impacts of infrastructure projects

Gender equality is an important aspect in the development process of countries but is largely neglected in the planning and provision of infrastructure. Consultation and involvement of women or women's groups in decision-making regarding the location of type of infrastructure provided in the locality and/or region of the country remains very limited in some ICRF countries.


---
**Page 303**
---

According to the survey results, the participation of women, especially rural women, in public works as wage labor is hindered by some major factors. In Namibia, for example, the division of domestic labor and childcare responsibilities among family members limits the time they can devote to productive activities, and often means that they can only participate in work done in their locality. For the National Network of Rural Women's Associations RENATER, women are not always included as decision-makers or involved in the management of infrastructure in a formal way will help overcome the often traditional constraints and obstacles that hinder women's participation in leadership positions.

Opportunities for women to be employed and trained in the construction and operation of infrastructure projects are limited. In Namibia, for example, there is no specific training as childcare and health centers to encourage their participation in infrastructure projects are either lacking or simply not considered.

Similarly, women have been left out of involved in infrastructure projects but they benefit from their installation. For example, roads have been built to enable women to transport their agricultural products to markets in the nearby city. It should also be noted that women are not integrated into the planning and implementation stages of these projects. The imposition of quotas for women at all levels of design and implementation of infrastructure projects remains the best strategy to ensure that they participate and are not disadvantaged by these projects. Women must be involved from the beginning until the end of the project cycle. Women must be integrated into the infrastructure project in the planning and implementation stages.

According to the DRC-based National Network of Rural Women's Associations RENATER, women are not sufficiently included into infrastructure projects. According to RENATER, the main constraints and obstacles to women's participation in infrastructure activities remain gender-based discriminatory practices and the lack of education and training opportunities.


### Strategies for women's participation and consultation in infrastructure projects

Strategies for gender mainstreaming in the development process exist, but they have not been identified to overcome barriers to women's participation and benefits from infrastructure projects. Indeed, there are no specific strategies or plans at national level that address gender equality and/or gender mainstreaming at all levels. In most cases, sector ministries/agencies are responsible for implementing these national strategies or plans. In the case of Chad, Namibia, Nigeria, and Togo, for example, the Ministry of Women's Affairs and Child Welfare; Namibia's Ministry of Women's Affairs and Child Welfare; the Federal Ministry of Women and Social Development; and the Minister of Social Welfare and Women Development, respectively.

Regarding equal access between men and women to infrastructure projects, women suffer more than men from the lack of access to quality Infrastructure. For the associations, it is essential that women are involved in all stages of project implementation so that they effectively contribute to both development and the benefit of women. Despite the implementation of these national strategies and plans, there are constraints and obstacles to women's participation in infrastructure projects. These include gender-based discriminatory practices and lack of education and training opportunities. It is therefore essential for governments to develop specific strategies and plans for the monitoring of the implementation of the strategy throughout the life cycle of the infrastructure. As a


---
**Page 304**
---

Page 300

result, there is a gap between the main directions of the decisions taken within the technical ministries and the implementation of actions to fully integrate gender into infrastructure projects.

Moreover, the implementation of these strategies sometimes remains concentrated in one area without covering all areas. In Nigeria, for example, in order to implement the national gender strategy, the government developed the National Gender Policy, the National Action Plan on Gender and Climate Change followed by the development and validation of its implementation plan. However, the lack of a clear institutional framework has been identified as one of the main factors preventing women and girls from accessing basic services to promote their upward social mobility and reduce gender disparities.

In Sierra Leone, there are no specific gender mainstreaming guidelines for infrastructure, but there is a national gender policy. In Côte d'Ivoire, the government has developed policies and laws to build women's capacity, awareness and empowerment opportunities. In Côte d'Ivoire, most ministries and structures show a clear commitment to gender mainstreaming and have fully implemented gender equality strategies. However, in some sectors, economic and social infrastructures have been designed, planned and implemented without taking into account the gender dimension.

6.4 Women's role as decision makers in infrastructure projects

Regarding the rise of women in decision-making, particularly through infrastructure projects, there is little opportunity for the project to support women as infrastructure managers in either a formal or informal way. When asked whether women and women's groups are often consulted and involved in decision-making processes related to infrastructure projects, only 10% answered yes, but most respondents said no. However, women's groups are organized in a way that allows them to participate in the decision-making process. However, women's groups are organized to participate in decision-making.

Furthermore, the possibilities for the project to support women as infrastructure managers in a formal or informal way must go through the identification of women's groups and their integration into the management of infrastructure projects with women. With regard to the practical needs and strategic interest that could be taken into account by the project, the training of "gender officers" responsible for gender issues is considered necessary. The inclusion of women in decision-making in infrastructure projects would be an asset for these women. In this regard, the majority of respondents emphasize that the issue of "practical and strategic gender interests" is becoming more widespread among the technical ministries involved in infrastructure projects. This will contribute to women's empowerment and sustainable development. For the respondents, there are many ways to achieve their objectives: training, awareness-raising and data collection mechanisms disaggregated by sex would be a step towards achieving these objectives.

According to some of the agencies interviewed, gender inequality issues in infrastructure projects depend on the sector or area of infrastructure. In the energy and transport sectors, for example, the creation of women's groups is not always considered as an important issue. However, it should be taken into consideration in the planning process; in fact, society has traditionally granted (and in some regions or localities continues to grant) differential rights based on gender, in the sense that women could not perform certain jobs and were excluded from traditional economic regions in which people, based on their gender, had different opportunities to access the labor market.


---
**Page 305**
---

Page 301

financial capital and human capital. These inequalities have ended up being transposed into the various policies and plans for energy, transport and industry. Work in these sectors is thus approached in a gendered manner.

For example, women’s groups emphasize the need to build childcare, maternity facilities, schools, and health care facilities near industrial centers. In Nigeria, for example, both men and women are involved in the decision-making process, particularly with regard to infrastructure, but not at all stages of project planning. The fact that gender is taken into account throughout the infrastructure life cycle will further promote gender equality in Nigeria.

### 6.5 Gender-disaggregated data on infrastructure

Climate change considerations that need to be taken into account throughout the life cycle of infrastructure should be thoroughly analyzed at the project planning stage, and the essential tool for monitoring any project is data collection. However, it should be noted that there is a lack of collection and centralization of gender-disaggregated data related to infrastructure projects in most countries. The lack of such data makes it difficult to assess the impact of infrastructure on women’s lives.

The synergy between environmental agencies and institutions with the National Bureau of Statistics to collect gender-disaggregated data has been noted as an important step towards improving data collection. In Namibia, for example, data is regularly collected in the areas of infrastructure, energy (electricity, oil, gas), and Telecommunication through geographic information. Although the majority of this data is not gender-disaggregated, it provides a good starting point to conduct research on the gender dimensions of climate-resilient infrastructure that incorporates the differential needs and use of infrastructure by women and men.

Ultimately, this survey highlighted the provisions of the gender equality and social inclusion policy in the transport sector. The same applies for other sectors such as water, power, and nuclear power sectors. The development of practical tools to facilitate gender mainstreaming in sustainable infrastructure project interventions will be used for both monitoring and collecting sex-disaggregated data.

## VIII. INTRODUCTION TO ICRF

Current and future infrastructures in Africa face risks associated with climate change which is often not a key consideration in infrastructure planning, design, financing, commissioning and construction. Climate-resilient infrastructure would help provide the quantity, quality and accessibility to infrastructure and the associated services to connect people and enhance the quality of life.

The Africa Finance Corporation (AFC) wholly-owned Capital Partners is establishing the Infrastructure Climate Resilient Fund (ICRF) to offer tailored financial products (concessional equity and parametric insurance) to finance climate-resilient infrastructure and reduce the impact of climate hazards on infrastructure. The ICRF will also provide technical assistance, capacity building, targeted technical assistance and policy interventions to ensure a long-term and systematic approach towards infrastructure financing in Africa.

This analysis is prepared to assess potential Gender and Social Inclusion Risks that might be associated with the implementation of the ICRF in the targeted countries, with the view to propose an action plan to ensure a gender responsive implementation of the ICRF.

The proposal targets 19 countries in Africa and is structured around three (3) components:

- **Component 1 – Financial instruments to reduce market failure and attract investment in Climate Resilience:**
  
- **Component 2 – Strengthening capacity, systems and networks to improve understanding of current and future risks:**

- **Component 3 – Policy interventions to support investments in Climate Resilient Infrastructure.**

X.GENDER ACTION PLAN

At the beginning of each project, further gender analyses will be undertaken by the promoters to identify (i) gender disparities that may affect the success of these projects; (ii) the opportunities offered and risks for improving women's access to resources, education, employment, income, and, above all, decision-making, which they are often victims of; and (iii) at the end of specific indicators to monitor progress to ensure that women and men are included at and benefit from all stages of the project.

For the implementation of the action plan described below, a call for tenders will be launched to establish a portfolio of reputable companies covering a wide range of expertise in order to strengthen AFC's capacity to implement gender equality. In parallel with this tender process, and throughout this process, the AFC will have a roster of gender equality experts to review gender assessments submitted by promoters and provide advice on the selection of tenders. The aim is to ensure that applying the principles of inclusion, equal opportunity and non-discrimination with regard to the selection of consultative files.

However, in addition to procurement opportunities for women-owned businesses, an invitation to these women-led stakeholder companies or those committed to gender and the advancement of women to apply will be duly taken into account in the selection process to ensure equal opportunities. The AFC will also seek to include at least 20% (P1) of women on its board supporting the management of open tenders. Companies are selected on a rigorous competitive basis, taking into account their track record and infrastructure.

Over the life of the fund (phase I), ICFR will aim to have in place 15 to 20 projects. Thus, for each project to be approved by ICFR, the project proponent will submit a comprehensive documentation that will include a detailed gender and social inclusion assessment and an action plan including a budget for the implementation of the associated action plan. This will be the gender analysis at the project level.
